# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 1

## Page 3

Volume One
The Book of the Oneness of Allah in Worship

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

“All praise is for Allah, who created the heavens and the earth and made darkness and light; yet the disbelievers assign equals to their Lord.”

He knows all that has been, all that is, and all that will be:

“His command, when He wills something, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

He is the One who

“creates whatever He wills and chooses. They have no choice. Glory be to Allah! Exalted is He above what they associate with Him.”

“He is Allah; there is no deity except Him. All praise belongs to Him in this life and the Hereafter. His is the judgment, and to Him you will be returned.”

Every kind of sign attests to His oneness as God. The perfect craftsmanship within creation reveals His knowledge to His creatures, and the diverse things He has brought into being display His power over His creation. The variety of changing circumstances points to His action according to His established ways. In His mercy, He bestows upon His servants blessings that none but the Lord of the heavens can count. The signs of the praise and commendation He deserves in every circumstance attest to His consummate wisdom.

His servants cannot fully recount His praise; rather, He is as He has praised Himself, by virtue of His names and attributes. His are the qualities of perfection and the attributes of majesty, in which nothing that exists resembles Him. He is the Most Holy, the One free of all imperfection, exalted above anything resembling Him in His qualities of perfection or any defect afflicting Him. Glory be to Him! Exalted is He, far above what the wrongdoers say—He who created

---

## Page 4

the heavens and the earth, has taken no offspring, and has no partner in sovereignty. He created everything and determined it with precision.

He sent the messengers as bearers of glad tidings and warners, so that people would have no argument against Allah after the messengers. Allah is ever Almighty, All-Wise. They brought glad tidings to those who obeyed them of the fullest realization of their desires—everything that souls love and regard as bliss—and warned those who disobeyed them of being cursed, cast away, and subjected to painful punishment. He commanded them to call creation to worship Him alone, without any partner, devoting their religion sincerely to Him, even though the polytheists dislike it.

As He, the Most High, said:

“O messengers, eat of the good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know well what you do.”

“And indeed, this religion of yours is one religion, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

He appointed for each of them a law and a way, so that they would follow a straight course toward Him and seek no deviation from it. He concluded their succession with Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the best of the first and the last, the chosen one of the Lord of all worlds: the witness, the bearer of glad tidings, the warner, the guide, and the illuminating lamp. Through him, He brought people out of darkness into light and guided them to the path of the Almighty, the Praiseworthy—

“Allah, to whom belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth. And woe to the disbelievers from a severe punishment.”

He sent him with the finest ways and laws, and through him brought to nothing the various forms of disbelief and religious innovation. He revealed to him the finest of books and tidings, making that Book a guardian over the heavenly scriptures that preceded it. He made his community the best community brought forth for mankind: they enjoin what is right, forbid what is wrong, and believe in Allah. They complete the number of seventy communities and are the best and most honored of them in Allah’s sight. The Prophet is a witness over them, and they are witnesses over mankind in this world and the Hereafter, by virtue of the inward and outward blessings He has lavished upon them. He protected them from agreeing upon misguidance, since no prophet would remain after him to make clear what had been altered in the message. He perfected their religion for them, completed His favors upon them, approved Islam as their religion, and made it prevail over

---

## Page 5

all other religions, both through victory and ascendancy and through proof and clarification. He made their scholars heirs to the prophets, standing in their place to convey the revealed Book, and established among them a victorious group who will continue to prevail upon the truth, unharmed by those who oppose or abandon them, until the time of reckoning.

He preserved for them the Reminder, which He sent down from the well-guarded Book, as He, the Most High, said:

“Indeed, We have sent down the Reminder, and We will surely preserve it.”

Thus their Book suffers none of the distortion and alteration that occurred at the hands of the followers of the Torah and the Gospel. He distinguished them by the transmission of reports and chains of narration, through which expert critics distinguish truth from falsehood. He entrusted this inheritance to the upright members of each successive generation—people of knowledge and religious devotion—who rid it of the distortions of extremists, the false attributions of those who promote falsehood, and the interpretations of the ignorant. Through them, His blessing upon the community endures, light emerges from darkness, and the religion of Allah with which He sent His Messenger is kept alive. Through them, Allah has made His path clear to mankind. The best of creation, therefore, are those who most closely follow this noble Prophet, described in His words, Most High:

“There has certainly come to you a Messenger from among yourselves. Grievous to him is what you suffer; he is deeply concerned for you, and kind and merciful to the believers.”

I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without any partner: the Lord of all worlds, the God of the messengers, and the King of the Day of Judgment. And I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger, whom He sent to all mankind. He sent him when disbelief, ignorance, and misguidance had left people in the most abject loss and the worst condition. He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—continued striving to convey the religion, guide mankind, and struggle against the disbelievers and hypocrites, until the sun of faith rose, the night of falsehood receded, the hosts of the Most Merciful prevailed, the party of Satan was humbled, the light of the Criterion shone forth, the recitation of the Quran became widespread, and the call to prayer was proclaimed,

---

## Page 6

and the people of the deserts and towns were illuminated by Allah’s light, and Allah’s proof was established against mankind and jinn, when the one who answered the call, from the line of Maʿadd ibn ʿAdnān, arose. May Allah bless him, his family, his Companions, and those who follow them in righteousness, with blessings pleasing to the King, the Judge, and grant them peace accompanied by His good pleasure.

To proceed:

There is no happiness for Allah’s servants, nor salvation in the Hereafter, except through following His Messenger.

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, He will admit them to gardens beneath which rivers flow, to abide therein forever. That is the great triumph.”

“And whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger and transgresses His limits, He will admit him to a Fire in which he will abide forever, and he will have a humiliating punishment.”

Obedience to Allah and His Messenger is the axis around which happiness revolves, and the abiding foundation from which salvation never departs.

Allah created creation to worship Him, as He, the Most High, said:

“I did not create jinn and mankind except to worship Me.”

He commanded them to worship Him through obedience to Him and His Messenger. Thus, nothing constitutes worship unless it is obligatory or recommended in Allah’s religion; anything else is a departure from His path. For this reason, he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever performs a deed that does not conform to our teachings, it is rejected.”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded it in their Sahīh collections.

He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—also said in the hadith of al-ʿIrbād ibn Sāriyah, reported by the authors of the Sunan collections and graded authentic by al-Tirmidhī:

“Those of you who live after me will see much disagreement. Therefore, adhere to my sunnah and the sunnah of the upright, rightly guided caliphs after me. Hold fast to it and bite firmly onto it with your molars. Beware of newly introduced matters, for every innovation is misguidance.”

The authentic hadith reported by Muslim and others states that he would say in his sermon:

“The best speech is the speech of Allah, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad. The worst matters are those newly introduced, and every innovation is misguidance.”

Allah has mentioned obedience to the Messenger and following him in approximately forty places in the Quran, such as His words, Most High:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

And His words, Most High:

---

## Page 7

“We sent no messenger except to be obeyed by Allah’s permission. If, when they wronged themselves, they had come to you and sought Allah’s forgiveness, and the Messenger had sought forgiveness for them, they would have found Allah Accepting of repentance, Merciful.”

“But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge concerning their disputes, then find within themselves no unease over what you have decided and submit completely.”

And His words, Most High:

“Say, ‘Obey Allah and the Messenger.’ But if they turn away, then indeed Allah does not love the disbelievers.”

He, the Most High, also said:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive you your sins.’”

Thus, He made a servant’s love for his Lord require following the Messenger, and made following the Messenger a means of attaining Allah’s love for His servant.

He, the Most High, also said:

“And thus We have revealed to you a spirit by Our command. You did not know what the Scripture was or what faith was, but We have made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants.”

Through what He revealed to him, Allah guides whomever He wills of His servants, just as Allah, the Most High, guided him—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—through it. As He, the Most High, said:

“Say, ‘If I go astray, I go astray only to my own detriment; but if I am guided, it is through what my Lord reveals to me.’”

He, the Most High, also said:

“There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book.”

“By it Allah guides those who pursue His good pleasure to the ways of peace, brings them out of darkness into the light by His permission, and guides them to a straight path.”

Through Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the distinction became clear between disbelief and faith, gain and loss, guidance and misguidance, salvation and ruin, waywardness and right guidance, deviation and rectitude, the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire, and the God-fearing and the wicked. So too did the preference for the path of those whom Allah has blessed—the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous—over the path of those who have incurred His wrath and those who have gone astray.

People have a greater need to know and follow what he brought than they have for food and drink. For the loss of food and drink brings death in this world, whereas the loss of what he brought brings punishment. It is therefore incumbent upon everyone to devote all their effort and ability to learning what he brought and obeying him, for

---

## Page 8

this is the path to deliverance from painful punishment and to happiness in the abode of bliss. The way to attain this is through narration and transmission, for reason alone does not suffice. Rather, just as the eye’s light requires illumination before it for sight, so the light of reason finds guidance only when the sun of the prophetic message shines upon it. Thus, conveying the religion is among the greatest obligations of Islam, and knowing what Allah commanded His Messenger is obligatory upon all people. Allah, glorified is He, sent Muhammad with the Book and the Sunnah, and through them completed His favour upon his community.

He, the Most High, said:

“And so that I may complete My favour upon you, and that you may be guided.”

“Just as We have sent among you a messenger from yourselves, reciting Our verses to you, purifying you, teaching you the Book and wisdom, and teaching you what you did not know.”

“So remember Me; I will remember you. Be grateful to Me, and do not be ungrateful to Me.”

He, the Most High, also said:

“Allah has certainly bestowed His favour upon the believers by sending among them a messenger from themselves, reciting His verses to them, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom.”

He, the Most High, also said:

“Remember Allah’s favour upon you, and what He has sent down to you of the Book and wisdom, by which He admonishes you.”

He, the Most High, also said:

“It is He who sent among the unlettered a messenger from themselves, reciting His verses to them, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom.”

He, the Most High, said concerning Abraham, His close friend:

“Our Lord, send among them a messenger from themselves who will recite Your verses to them, teach them the Book and wisdom, and purify them.”

He, the Most High, also said:

“Remember what is recited in your homes of Allah’s verses and wisdom.”

A number of scholars—including Yahyā ibn Abī Kathīr, Qatāda, al-Shāfiʿī, and others—have stated that “wisdom” means the Sunnah. For Allah commanded His Prophet’s wives to remember what was recited in their homes of the Book and wisdom. The Book is the Quran, and whatever else the Messenger recited was the Sunnah.

---

## Page 9

It has also been reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through several chains of transmission, in the hadiths of Abū Rāfiʿ, Abū Thaʿlaba, and others, that he said:

“Let me not find any of you reclining on his couch when word reaches him of something I have commanded or forbidden, saying, ‘The Quran is the arbiter between us and you: whatever we find in it to be lawful, we deem lawful, and whatever we find in it to be forbidden, we deem forbidden.’ Indeed, I have been given the Book and its like along with it.” In another narration: “Indeed, it is like the Book.”

The Quran was distinct in itself because Allah endowed it with inimitability, setting it apart from human speech. As He, the Most High, said:

“Say, ‘If mankind and the jinn gathered together to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce its like, even if they helped one another.’”

Since it was also conveyed through mass transmission, no one aspired to change any of its words or letters. Satan, however, aspired to introduce distortion and alteration into its meanings through changes and reinterpretation, and to introduce omissions and additions into the hadiths by which he could mislead some of Allah’s servants. Allah, the Most High, therefore raised up expert critics, men of guidance and sound judgment. They repelled Satan’s party, distinguished truth from falsehood, and devoted themselves to safeguarding the Sunnah and the meanings of the Quran against additions and omissions.

Each scholar of religion, drawing upon what Allah had graciously bestowed upon him and the Muslims, fulfilled the role of those endowed with deep understanding—those who grasped the meanings of the Quran and hadith—by countering the errors that had arisen in these matters in earlier and later times. Some of these matters were manifest and clear, permitting no departure from them; others were subtle, allowing upright scholars to exercise independent scholarly judgment.

The scholars of transmission and the critics undertook the study of narrations and their chains of transmission. For this they travelled to distant lands, forsook the pleasure of sleep, parted from their wealth and children, spent both their newly acquired and inherited wealth, endured hardships patiently, and contented themselves with no more of this world than a traveller’s provisions,

---

## Page 10

and there are well-known accounts and transmitted stories of their efforts, familiar to those versed in the subject and recorded for anyone wishing to learn about them. These tell of scholars resting their heads on the earth, forgoing delightful food and drink and the company of family and friends, patiently enduring the bitterness of being far from home, and braving terrible hardships. Allah made this pursuit dear and sweet to them so that He might thereby preserve His religion. Likewise, He made the Sacred House a place of return and safety for people, who journey to it from every distant mountain pass and endure painful hardships along the way. He also made fighting with one’s life and wealth dear to those who engage in battle, in His wisdom, thereby preserving the religion, leading the rightly guided, and making the guidance and religion of truth with which He sent His Messenger prevail, even though the polytheists dislike it. Whoever sincerely performs the deeds of religion for Allah is among Allah’s pious allies, destined for everlasting bliss.

As He, the Most High, said:

“Indeed, the allies of Allah will have no fear, nor will they grieve.”

“Those who believed and were mindful of Allah.”

“For them are glad tidings in the life of this world and in the Hereafter. There is no changing the words of Allah. That is the supreme triumph.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained that glad tidings in this world take two forms:

The first is the praise that others give a person.

The second is a good dream that a righteous man sees, or that someone else sees concerning him.

He was asked, “O Messenger of Allah, a man performs a deed for his own benefit, and people praise him for it.” He replied, “That is the believer’s glad tidings received in advance.”

Al-Barā’ ibn ‘Āzib said: The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked about His words, “For them are glad tidings in the life of this world.” He replied, “It is a good dream that a righteous man sees, or that someone else sees concerning him.”

Those who undertake to preserve the knowledge inherited from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the body of custodians who safeguard it against additions and omissions—are among the greatest of Allah’s pious allies and members of His party

---

## Page 11

who attain success. Indeed, they enjoy a distinction above other people of faith and righteous deeds. As He, the Most High, said:

“Allah will raise those who have believed among you and those who were given knowledge, by degrees.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said: Allah raises [those believers who have been given knowledge by degrees above those who have not been given knowledge] (1).

The science of chains of transmission and narration is among the distinctive gifts Allah has bestowed upon the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, making it a means of attaining understanding. The People of the Book have no chains of transmission through which they pass down their reports. The same applies to the innovators within this community, the people of misguidance. Chains of transmission belong only to those upon whom Allah has bestowed His great favor—the people of Islam and the Sunnah. Through them, they distinguish the authentic from the unsound and the crooked from the upright. Others—the people of innovation and the disbelievers—have only reports that they pass down without chains of transmission, yet their religion rests upon them. They cannot distinguish truth from falsehood in these reports, nor what is valuable from what is worthless.

As for this community blessed with mercy and the members of this community safeguarded from error, those among them possessed of knowledge and religious devotion are certain of their affairs. Truth stands apart from falsehood as clearly to them as dawn appears to one who has eyes. Allah has protected them from collectively agreeing upon an error in His religion, whether in matters of reason or transmission. He has commanded them, whenever they disagree over anything, to refer it to Allah and the Messenger. As He, the Most High, said:

“O you who have believed, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. And if you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is best and best in outcome.”

Thus, when the scholars of jurisprudence agree upon a ruling, it can only be true; and when the scholars of

## Page 12

hadith agree that a hadith is authentic, it can only be true. Each of these two groups employs both evident and subtle forms of reasoning to establish its conclusions, through which those thoroughly versed in this field can be recognized. Allah, the Most High, inspires them to reach the correct judgment in this matter, as the scriptural proofs indicate and as practical experience has shown. For Allah has inscribed faith in their hearts and strengthened them with a spirit from Him, because they were sincere in their allegiance to Allah and His Messenger and in their opposition to those who turn away from Him.

The Most High said:

“You will not find a people who believe in Allah and the Last Day having affection for those who oppose Allah and His Messenger, even if they were their fathers, their sons, their brothers, or their kindred. Those are the ones in whose hearts He has inscribed faith and whom He has strengthened with a spirit from Him.”

Those versed in the knowledge transmitted from the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, uphold these principles more fully than anyone else. In Allah’s cause, none of them fears a critic’s reproach, nor do formidable hardships turn them away from Allah’s path. Rather, each speaks the truth even when it tells against him, and speaks critically of those dearest to him, acting upon the words of the Most High:

“O you who believe, stand firmly for justice as witnesses for Allah, even against yourselves, your parents, or your close relatives. Whether one is rich or poor, Allah has a greater claim upon both. So do not follow your desires, lest you depart from justice. If you distort your testimony or refuse to give it, Allah is indeed fully aware of what you do.”

And His words:

“O you who believe, stand firmly for Allah as witnesses in justice, and do not let hatred of a people lead you to be unjust. Be just; that is nearer to righteousness. And fear Allah; indeed, Allah is fully aware of what you do.”

Their commendable efforts and worthy work in assessing transmitters’ reliability and flaws, and in judging hadiths weak or authentic, have helped preserve the religion and protect it from the fabricators’ inventions. They occupy different levels in this work: some confine themselves to transmission and narration alone; others possess knowledge of hadith and expertise in its critical study; and others have a deep understanding of it and knowledge of its meanings.

---

## Page 13

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded the community that those present should convey his words to those absent, and offered an answered supplication for those who convey them. He said in an authentic hadith:

“Convey from me, even a single verse. Relate reports from the Children of Israel; there is no harm in doing so. Whoever deliberately attributes a lie to me, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

He also said in his sermon during the Farewell Pilgrimage:

“Let those present convey this to those absent, for many a person to whom it is conveyed understands it better than one who heard it directly.”

He also said:

“May Allah grant radiance to a person who hears a hadith from us and conveys it to someone who has not heard it. Many a bearer of religious knowledge lacks understanding, and many a bearer of religious knowledge conveys it to someone with greater understanding than himself. There are three things toward which a Muslim’s heart bears no resentment: doing one’s deeds sincerely for Allah, offering sincere counsel to those in authority, and remaining with the Muslim community; for their call encompasses those behind them.”

In this, he supplicates for anyone who conveys his hadith, even if he lacks deep understanding, and for anyone who conveys it even when the listener has greater understanding than the transmitter. This accounts for the radiance bestowed upon those who convey it. Hence Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna said:

“You will find no one among the scholars of hadith without radiance in his face, because of the Prophet’s supplication, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Both nadara and nadura mean “to be radiant,” though the form with the a vowel is more eloquent.

Scholars, past and present, have always held hadith transmitters in high esteem. Indeed, al-Shāfiʿī, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“When I see a scholar of hadith, it is as though I have seen one of the Prophet’s Companions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Al-Shāfiʿī said this because they occupy the Companions’ position in conveying the hadith of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Al-Shāfiʿī also said:

“The scholars of hadith have preserved it, and we therefore owe them a debt of gratitude, for they have preserved it for us.”

End of quotation.

---

## Page 14

Shaykh al-Islām, may Allah Most High have mercy on him, also said:

A Principle Concerning Unity and Division, Their Cause and Consequence

Allah Most High said:

“He has ordained for you in religion what He enjoined upon Noah, that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: establish the religion and do not become divided in it.”

He, glorified is He, tells us that He has ordained for us what He enjoined upon Noah, what He revealed to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and what He enjoined upon the three others mentioned. These are the messengers of firm resolve from whom the covenant was taken, as stated in His words:

“And when We took from the prophets their covenant, and from you, Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, son of Mary.”

In His words:

“what He enjoined upon Noah, that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined,”

He used the relative pronoun alladhī (“that which”) and the wording of revelation in reference to Muhammad, whereas for the other messengers He used the wording of “injunction.”

Then He said:

“Establish the religion.”

This explains the injunction. Here, (an) is the explanatory particle that follows a verb conveying the meaning of saying without itself being a form of the verb “to say,” as in His words:

“Then We revealed to you: follow,”

and:

“We enjoined upon those who were given the Scripture before you, and upon you: fear Allah.”

The meaning is: We said to them, “Fear Allah.” Likewise, His words, “Establish the religion,” have the sense of His saying: “The religion prescribed for you is what He enjoined upon messengers; We said, ‘Establish the religion and do not become divided in it.’” Thus, what has been ordained for us is what was enjoined and revealed, namely:

“Establish the religion.”

Thus, “Establish the religion” explains

---

## Page 15

what has been ordained for us, enjoined upon the messengers, and revealed to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It may therefore be said that the pronoun in “Establish” refers to us; or that it refers to the messenger; or that it refers to everyone. This last interpretation is the best. Comparable examples are: “I commanded you to do what I commanded Zayd to do: obey Allah,” and “I enjoined upon you what I enjoined upon the sons of so-and-so: do this.”

On the first interpretation, it functions as an appositional substitute for mā (“what”): that is, “He has ordained for you to establish the religion.” On the second interpretation, He has ordained what He addressed to them: “Establish.” This too is an appositional substitute, and He has stated what was said to the earlier peoples. On the third interpretation, He has ordained the injunction: “Establish.” Since this collective address follows the statement that these words were spoken both to us and to them, it is evident that the pronoun refers to both groups together. This is the soundest interpretation, if Allah wills.

The meaning of the first two interpretations ultimately comes back to this, for what has been ordained for us is what He enjoined upon the messengers: the command to establish the religion and the prohibition against becoming divided in it. The uncertainty concerns only whether the wording of the pronoun encompasses them, while it is known that the same was said to us; or the reverse; or whether it encompasses us all.

If Allah has commanded the earlier and later peoples to establish the religion and not become divided in it, and has stated that He has ordained for us what He enjoined upon Noah and what He revealed to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then this admits of two interpretations.

One is that what He revealed to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, includes his revealed law, which is specific to us. For Allah revealed to him everything with which Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent, both fundamentals and subsidiary matters. The case of Noah and the other messengers is different: of their religion, He has ordained for us only what they were enjoined to do—establish the religion and refrain from becoming divided in it. The religion upon which they agreed consists of the fundamentals. Thus, the passage encompasses several points:

---

## Page 16

The first is that He has ordained for us both the shared religion—Islam and faith in their general sense—and the religion specific to us—Islam and faith in their specific sense.

The second is that He has commanded us to establish this religion in its entirety, both what is shared and what is specific to us, and has forbidden us to become divided in it.

The third is that He has commanded the messengers to establish the shared religion and has forbidden them to become divided in it.

The fourth is that He conveyed this meaning by placing His words, “and that which We have revealed to you,” between His words, “what He enjoined upon Noah,” and His words, “and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus.”

He then said:

“They did not become divided until after knowledge had come to them, out of transgression against one another.”

He thus stated that their division arose only after knowledge had come to them, making clear what they should guard against. For Allah would not lead a people astray after guiding them until He had made clear to them what they should guard against. [He also stated that they became divided only out of transgression. Transgression is overstepping the bounds, as Ibn Umar said . . . (1): arrogance and envy.] This differs from division arising from independent scholarly reasoning in the absence of knowledge, without any intention of transgression, such as permissible disagreement among scholars. Transgression consists either in neglecting what is right or in overstepping a limit; it is therefore either abandoning an obligation or committing something forbidden. It is thus clear that this is what brings about division.

This accords with His words concerning the People of the Book:

“And from those who say, ‘We are Christians,’ We took their covenant; but they forgot a portion of what they had been reminded of, so We stirred up enmity and hatred among them until the Day of Resurrection.”

He thus stated that their forgetting a portion of what they had been reminded of—that is, failing to act upon some of what they had been commanded—caused enmity and hatred to be stirred up among them. The same occurs among the followers of our religion, as we find among the factions disputing over the fundamentals of their religion and many of its subsidiary matters, among those versed in

## Page 17

the fundamentals and subsidiary matters, and likewise between scholars and worshippers among whom Mosaic or Christian tendencies predominate, so that they retain a resemblance to the two communities, each of which said: “The other has no ground to stand on.” Thus, we find that a student of jurisprudence who holds fast to the outward acts of religion and a Sufi who holds fast to its inward acts each rejects the other’s approach and claims that the other is not among the followers of the religion, or disregards him as though his approach had no place in the religion. Enmity and hatred then arise between them.

The explanation is that Allah has commanded purity of the heart and purity of the body. Both forms of purity belong to the religion that Allah has commanded and made obligatory. He, the Most High, says:

“Allah does not intend to place you in difficulty, but He intends to purify you and complete His favor upon you.”

And He says:

“In it are men who love to purify themselves, and Allah loves those who purify themselves.”

And He says:

“Indeed, Allah loves those who repent and loves those who purify themselves.”

And He says:

“Take charity from their wealth to purify and cleanse them with it.”

And He says:

“Those are the ones whose hearts Allah does not intend to purify.”

And He says:

“Indeed, the polytheists are unclean.”

And He says:

“Allah intends only to remove impurity from you, O people of the household, and to purify you completely.”

We find many students of jurisprudence and devoted worshippers whose sole concern is purity of the body. In their attention to it and their practice, they go beyond what is prescribed, while neglecting the purity of the heart that they have been commanded to observe, whether as an obligation or a recommendation. They understand purity to mean nothing beyond bodily purity.

We also find many Sufis and mutafaqqira whose sole concern is purity of the heart, to the point that their attention to it and their practice exceed what is prescribed. Meanwhile, they neglect the purity of the body that they have been commanded to observe, whether as an obligation or a recommendation.

The former fall into blameworthy obsessive misgivings: pouring excessive amounts of water, treating as impure what is not impure, and avoiding what they are not prescribed to avoid, while their hearts harbor various forms of

---

## Page 18

envy, arrogance, and rancor toward their brothers. In this there is a clear resemblance to the Jews.

The others fall into blameworthy heedlessness. They exaggerate the notion of inner soundness until they regard ignorance of the evil they must know about—and must avoid—as a sign of inner soundness. They do not distinguish between an inner self free of the desire for forbidden evil and a heart devoid of the knowledge of evil that they are commanded to acquire. Along with this ignorance and heedlessness, they may not avoid impurities, and they perform the obligatory purification, in imitation of the Christians.

Enmity arises between the two groups because they neglect a portion of what they were reminded of and because of transgression, which means overstepping the bounds: either through negligence and disregard for what is due, or through aggression and acts of injustice. Sometimes they transgress against one another, and sometimes they transgress against the rights of Allah. The two are inseparable. Hence He says:

“Out of mutual transgression.”

Each group transgressed against the other, neither acknowledging the truth the other possessed nor refraining from aggression against it.

The Most High says:

“Those who were given the Scripture did not become divided until after clear evidence had come to them.”

The Most High also says:

“Mankind was one community; then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of good tidings and warners, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning that over which they differed. None differed over it except those who were given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual transgression.”

The Most High says:

“We certainly gave the Children of Israel the Scripture, judgment, and prophethood,”

and the remainder of the verse. The Most High says something similar concerning Moses son of ‘Imrān. He also says:

“Do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them.”

And He says:

“Indeed, those who divided their religion and became sects—you have nothing to do with them.”

And He says:

“So direct your face toward the religion, inclining to truth—the natural disposition with which Allah created mankind. There is no changing Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.”

“Turning back to Him, fear Him and establish prayer, and do not be among

---

## Page 19

those who associate partners with Allah.”

“Among those who divided their religion and became sects, each faction rejoicing in what it has.”

For each of those who associate partners with Allah worships a deity according to his own desires. As He says in the first verse:

“What you call them to is difficult for those who associate partners with Allah.”

And He says:

“O messengers, eat from the good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know well what you do.”

“And indeed this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

“But they divided their affair among themselves into factions, each faction rejoicing in what it has.”

It is thus clear that unity and harmony arise from embracing the religion in its entirety and acting upon all of it: worshipping Allah alone, without partner, as He has commanded, both inwardly and outwardly. Division arises from abandoning a portion of what the servant has been commanded to do, and from mutual transgression.

The fruits of unity are Allah’s mercy, pleasure, and blessings; happiness in this world and the Hereafter; and radiant faces. The consequences of division are Allah’s punishment and curse, darkened faces, and the Messenger’s disavowal of them, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

This is one of the proofs that consensus constitutes conclusive evidence. For when they unite, they thereby obey Allah and receive His mercy. Yet obedience to Allah and His mercy cannot result from something Allah has not commanded, whether a belief, a statement, or a deed. If the statement or deed upon which they agreed had not been commanded by Allah, it would neither constitute obedience to Him nor be a cause of His mercy. Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz used this argument at the beginning of “al-Tanbīh” (Drawing Attention), highlighting this subtle point.

---

## Page 20

And he said:

Section

In the well-known hadith recorded in the Sunan collections and narrated by two jurists among the Companions, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd and Zayd ibn Thābit, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Three things toward which a Muslim’s heart bears no malice: devoting one’s deeds sincerely to Allah, offering sincere counsel to those in authority, and adhering to the Muslim community; for their call encompasses them from behind.”

In the reliably preserved hadith narrated by Abū Hurayrah:

“Allah is pleased with three things for you: that you worship Him and associate nothing with Him; that you all hold firmly to the rope of Allah and do not become divided; and that you offer sincere counsel to those whom Allah has placed in authority over you.”

In these hadiths, he brought together three qualities: devoting one’s deeds sincerely to Allah, offering sincere counsel to those in authority, and adhering to the Muslim community. These three encompass the foundations and principles of religion, the rights owed to Allah and His servants, and the interests of this world and the Hereafter.

To explain this, rights fall into two categories: the rights of Allah and the rights of His servants. Allah’s right is that we worship Him and associate nothing with Him, as expressly stated in one of the two hadiths. This is what is meant by devoting one’s deeds sincerely to Allah, as stated in the other hadith.

The rights of His servants likewise fall into two categories: particular and general. Particular rights include each person’s duty to treat his parents kindly and to fulfil the rights of his wife and neighbour. These are subsidiary matters of religion, because a person accountable under religious law may not be subject to these obligations, and because the benefits they secure are particular to individuals.

As for general rights, people fall into two groups: rulers and those they govern. The rulers’ right is to receive sincere counsel, while the right of those they govern is that their community be upheld through adherence to it. Their welfare cannot be fully secured unless they are united, and they do not unite

---

## Page 21

upon misguidance; rather, the well-being of their religion and worldly affairs lies in their unity and in their holding firmly, all together, to the rope of Allah. These qualities encompass the foundations of religion.

They are explained in the hadith narrated by Muslim from Tamīm al-Dārī, who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Religion is sincerity. Religion is sincerity. Religion is sincerity.”

They asked, “Toward whom, O Messenger of Allah?”

He replied, “Toward Allah, His Book, His Messenger, the leaders of the Muslims, and the Muslims at large.”

Sincerity toward Allah, His Book, and His Messenger falls within the right owed to Allah and the worship of Him alone, without any partner. Sincerity toward the leaders of the Muslims and the Muslims at large consists in offering sincere counsel to those in authority and adhering to the Muslim community. Adhering to their community expresses sincerity toward them collectively. As for sincere counsel directed to each one individually, some of it is possible, but extending it comprehensively to every individual is impracticable.

---

## Page 22

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah alone, without any partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and grant him peace.

To proceed:

This is an important principle concerning the oneness of Allah and sincerely devoting oneself and one's deeds to Him, in worship and in seeking help. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘O Allah, Owner of sovereignty, You give sovereignty to whom You will and take sovereignty away from whom You will. You honour whom You will and humble whom You will’”—to the end of the verse.

He Most High also says:

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah. Then, when harm touches you, it is to Him that you cry out for help.”

He Most High also says:

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it but Him; and if He touches you with good, He has power over all things.”

In another verse, He Most High says:

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it but Him; and if He intends good for you, none can repel His bounty.”

He Most High also says:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

He Most High also says:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

He Most High also says:

“In Him I put my trust, and to Him I turn.”

He Most High also says:

“Whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth glorifies Allah. His is the dominion, and His is all praise, and He has power over all things.”

He Most High also says:

“Know, then, that there is no god but Allah, and seek forgiveness for your sin and for the believing men and women.”

He Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah intends harm for me, can they remove His harm? Or if He intends mercy for me, can they withhold His mercy?’”—to the end of the verse.

---

## Page 23

He Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Invoke those you claim besides Allah.’ They do not possess an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth, nor do they have any share in either, nor does He have any helper among them.”

“Intercession does not benefit anyone with Him except one whom He permits.”

He Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Invoke those you claim besides Him.’ They have no power to remove harm from you or to transfer it elsewhere.”

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of nearness to their Lord, striving to be the closest. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, the punishment of your Lord is something to beware of.”

He Most High also says:

“Do not invoke another god alongside Allah. There is no god but Him. Everything will perish except His Face. His is the judgement, and to Him you will be returned.”

He Most High also says:

“Put your trust in the Ever-Living, who never dies, and glorify Him with praise. He is sufficient as One fully aware of His servants’ sins.”

“He who created the heavens and the earth and whatever is between them”—to the end of the verse.

He Most High also says:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah with sincere devotion to Him, upright in faith, to establish prayer and to give zakah”—to the end of the verse.

There are many similar passages in the Quran, as there are in the hadiths. This is likewise affirmed by the consensus of the Muslim community, particularly its people of knowledge and faith. For them, this is the very axis around which religion revolves, as indeed it is.

We shall explain this from several perspectives, first setting out an introduction. The servant—indeed, every living being, and even every created thing other than Allah—is dependent and needs to obtain what benefits it and ward off what harms it. For a living being, benefit consists in bliss and pleasure, while harm consists in pain and torment.

It therefore requires two things:

The first is the desired, intended and beloved object from which it derives benefit and pleasure.

The second is the helper who brings it to that intended end and secures it, and who prevents the averting of what is disliked.

These are the two distinct things: the agent and the end. Thus, there are four things here:

The first is something beloved whose existence is sought.

---

## Page 24

And the second is something disliked and hated whose nonexistence is sought.

The third is the means of attaining what is sought and loved.

The fourth is the means of warding off what is disliked. These four things are indispensable to the servant—indeed, to every living being, whose existence and well-being depend upon them. As for things that are not living, they must be discussed differently.

With this established, what I have mentioned can be explained from several perspectives.

The first is that Allah Most High loves to be the One intended, invoked and sought. He is the One who helps in attaining what is sought; whatever is besides Him is what is disliked, and He is the One who helps in warding off what is disliked. Thus He, glorified be He, alone brings together all four matters. This is the meaning of His words:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

For servitude encompasses the intended, sought-after goal, but in the most perfect manner. The One whose help is sought is the One who assists in attaining that goal. The first pertains to divinity, and the second to lordship. For the Deity is the One taken as a god and worshipped with love, devotion, reverence and honour; and the Lord is the One who nurtures His servant, giving him his created form and then guiding him in every aspect of his life, in worship and otherwise.

Likewise, He Most High says:

“In Him I have put my trust, and to Him I turn.”

And He says:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

And He says:

“Our Lord, in You we have put our trust, to You we have turned, and to You is the final return.”

And He Most High says:

“Put your trust in the Ever-Living, who never dies, and glorify Him with praise.”

And He Most High says:

“In Him I have put my trust, and to Him is my return in repentance.”

And He says:

“And devote yourself entirely to Him.”

“Lord of the east and the west; there is no god but Him, so take Him as your Trustee.”

These are seven passages that bring together these two comprehensive principles.

---

## Page 25

The second perspective:

Allah created His creatures to worship Him—a worship that encompasses knowing Him, turning to Him, loving Him and being sincerely devoted to Him. Through remembering Him, their hearts find tranquillity; through seeing Him in the Hereafter, their eyes find delight. Nothing He gives them in the Hereafter is dearer to them than looking upon Him, and nothing He gives them in this world is greater than faith in Him.

Their need for Him in worshipping Him and devoting themselves to Him is as great as—and indeed greater than—their need for Him as their Creator and Lord. For this worship is their intended ultimate goal, and through it they act and move. Without it, they can have neither well-being nor success, neither bliss nor pleasure, under any circumstances. Rather, whoever turns away from the remembrance of his Lord will have a life of hardship, and We shall raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection. This is why Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but forgives anything less than that for whomever He wills. This is also why “There is no god but Allah” is the best of good deeds, and why affirming His oneness through the declaration “There is no god but Allah” is the foundation of the whole matter.

As for affirming Allah’s oneness in His lordship, which creation has acknowledged and speculative theologians have established, this alone is not sufficient. Rather, it forms part of the proof against them. This is the meaning of the reported saying:

“O son of Adam, I created everything for you, and I created you for Me. By My right over you, do not let what I created for you distract you from what I created you for.”

Know that this is Allah’s right over His servants: that they worship Him and associate nothing with Him, as stated in the authentic hadith narrated by Mu‘ādh from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He said, ‘Do you know what Allah’s right over His servants is?’

I replied, ‘Allah and His Messenger know best.’

He said, ‘Allah’s right over His servants is that they worship Him and associate nothing with Him. Do you know what the servants’ right over Allah is if they do that?’

I replied, ‘Allah and His Messenger know best.’

He said, ‘Their right is that He not punish them.’”

---

## Page 26

And He loves this and is pleased with it; He is pleased with those who practise it and rejoices at the repentance of whoever returns to Him. In this, too, lie the servant’s pleasure, happiness and bliss. Elsewhere, I have explained something of what Allah’s love for this and His rejoicing in it mean.

Nothing in existence other than Allah, glorified is He, can give the servant repose and tranquillity, or delight through turning towards it. Whoever worships anything other than Allah—even if he loves it and finds affection and some pleasure through it in this worldly life—suffers harm greater than the harm of enjoying poisoned food.

“Had there been gods in the heavens and the earth besides Allah, both would have been ruined. Glory be to Allah, Lord of the Throne, above what they attribute to Him.”

For the sound existence of the heavens and the earth depends on worship of the true God. If there were gods other than Allah within them, such a god would not be a true God, for Allah has neither peer nor likeness. They would therefore fall into ruin through the absence of that upon which their well-being depends. This concerns His divinity and worship. As for His lordship, that is a different matter, which we shall explain in its proper place.

Know that the servant’s need for Allah—to worship Him without associating anything with Him—has no counterpart against which it can be measured. In some respects, however, it resembles the body’s need for food and drink, though there are many differences between the two. For the servant’s true essence is his heart and soul, and it can have no well-being except through its God, Allah, besides whom there is no god. It finds tranquillity in this world only through remembering Him. It strives laboriously towards Him and will meet Him; it must inevitably meet Him, and it can have no well-being except through meeting Him.

Even if the servant finds pleasures or joy in something other than Allah, this does not last. Rather, he moves from one kind to another and from one person to another. He finds delight in something at a particular time and under certain circumstances, yet at another time, what once gave him delight and pleasure gives him neither delight nor pleasure; indeed, it may even

---

## Page 27

harm him through his attachment to it and its presence with him, causing him injury. As for his God, he needs Him in every circumstance and at every moment; wherever he may be, He is with him. This is why our imam, Abraham, the intimate friend of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“I do not love those that set.”

And the greatest verse in the Noble Quran is:

“Allah—there is no god except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all existence.”

I have discussed the meaning of “the Sustainer of all existence” at length elsewhere, and we have explained that He is the Everlasting, the Abiding One, who never passes away, never ceases to exist, and never perishes in any way whatsoever.

Know that this line of reasoning rests upon two foundations:

The first is that faith in Allah itself, together with worshipping, loving and revering Him, is a person’s nourishment and sustenance, the source of his well-being and that which sustains him. This is the understanding of the people of faith, and it is what the Quran indicates. It is not as some speculative theologians and others maintain: that worshipping Him is a burdensome obligation and a hardship, contrary to what the heart seeks, imposed merely as a trial and a test, or in order to provide compensation through reward, as the Muʿtazilah and others say.

Although righteous deeds may include things that run counter to the soul’s desires—and Allah, glorified is He, rewards the servant for commanded deeds that involve hardship, as He, Most High, says:

“That is because no thirst or fatigue afflicts them…”

—to the end of the verse—and as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to ʿĀʾishah:

“Your reward is commensurate with your hardship”—

this is not the primary purpose of the religious command. Rather, it occurs incidentally and as a secondary consequence, for reasons that are not to be discussed here. This will be explained in its proper place.

For this reason, neither the Quran, nor the Sunnah, nor the statements of the early generations describe faith and righteous deeds without qualification as a burdensome obligation, as many speculative theologians and those engaged in jurisprudence do. Rather, the imposition of burdens is mentioned only in statements of negation, such as His saying:

“Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity.”

---

## Page 28

“You are responsible only for yourself.”

“Allah does not burden a soul beyond what He has given it.”

That is, even when a command imposes a burden, it does not exceed a person’s capacity. This does not mean that He calls the whole of the divine law a burdensome obligation, when most of it brings delight to the eyes, joy to the hearts, pleasure to the souls and perfect bliss. This comes through seeking the Face of Allah, turning back to Him, remembering Him and turning one’s face towards Him. He is the true God in whom hearts find tranquillity, and nothing else can ever take His place in this regard.

Allah, Most High, says:

“So worship Him and be steadfast in His worship. Do you know of any equal to Him?”

This, then, is one foundation.

(The second foundation:

Bliss in the Hereafter likewise includes looking upon Him. It is not as some speculative theologians and others claim: that bliss and pleasure come only through created things, such as food, drink, marital intimacy and the like. Rather, their complete pleasure and bliss lie in their enjoyment of the Creator, glorified and exalted is He, as expressed in the transmitted supplication:

“O Allah, I ask You for the pleasure of looking upon Your Face and the longing to meet You, without harmful adversity or a trial that leads astray.”

This was narrated by al-Nasāʾī and others. In Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, Suhayb narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the people of Paradise enter Paradise, a caller will announce: ‘O people of Paradise, Allah has made you a promise which He wishes to fulfil for you.’ They will say: ‘What is it? Has He not brightened our faces, admitted us to Paradise and protected us from the Fire?’” He said: “Then the veil will be lifted, and they will look upon Him, glorified is He. He will have given them nothing dearer to them than looking upon Him. This is the additional reward.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus explained that, despite their full enjoyment of what Allah has given them in Paradise, He will have given them nothing dearer to them than looking upon Him. It is dearer to them precisely because the bliss and pleasure they experience through it are greater than those they experience through anything else. For pleasure follows awareness of what one loves: the dearer something is to a person, the more pleasurable attaining it becomes, and the greater his enjoyment of it.

---

## Page 29

It is also reported that Friday is the Day of Increase—the Friday of the days of the Hereafter. Hadiths and transmitted reports bear this out. Allah, Most High, says concerning the disbelievers:

“No indeed! On that Day they will be veiled from their Lord.”

“Then they will surely burn in Hell.”

The torment of being veiled from Him is therefore the greatest kind of torment, and the pleasure of looking upon His Face is the highest of pleasures. Their enjoyment of all other created things cannot take the place of their enjoyment of Him, Most High.

These two foundations are established in the Quran and the Sunnah. They are affirmed by people of knowledge and faith, discussed by Sufi masters of spiritual insight, and upheld by Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah and the ordinary members of the Muslim community. They belong to the natural disposition with which Allah created humankind. Those who affirm them may sometimes argue against their denial through scriptural texts and transmitted reports, and at other times through direct experience and inward feeling—when it is the pleasure that is denied—for tasting and experiencing it refutes its denial. At other times, they may argue by drawing comparisons between similar cases, using rational analogies.

The third aspect:

No created being holds the power to benefit or harm the servant, to give or withhold, to guide or lead astray, to grant victory or abandon him, to lower or raise him, or to honour or humiliate him. Rather, it is his Lord who created him, provided for him, granted him insight, guided him and bestowed His blessings abundantly upon him. If Allah afflicts him with harm, none but He can remove it; and if He grants him a blessing, none but He can take it away. A servant, however, can neither benefit nor harm him except by Allah’s permission.

This aspect is more evident to ordinary people than the first, and for this reason the Quran addresses them through it more often than through the first. Yet when a discerning person reflects upon the Quran’s approach, he finds that Allah calls His servants through this aspect to the first.

This aspect calls for relying upon Allah, seeking His help, supplicating to Him and asking of Him, rather than anyone else. It also calls for loving and worshipping Allah because of His kindness towards His servant and His abundant bestowal of

---

## Page 30

blessings upon him, and because of the servant’s need for Him for these blessings. Yet when they worship Him, love Him and rely upon Him on this basis, they enter into the first aspect. A parallel in this world is someone afflicted by a great calamity, severe poverty or unsettling fear, who begins to supplicate to Allah and implore Him until Allah grants him such pleasure in intimate prayer to Him that it becomes dearer to him than the need he originally sought to fulfil. Initially, however, he had not known this pleasure so as to seek it and long for it.

The Quran is full of references to the servants’ need for Allah rather than anyone else, to His blessings upon them, and to the various forms of bliss and pleasure He has promised them in the Hereafter. No created being possesses any of this. This aspect therefore establishes reliance upon Allah, gratitude to Him and love for Him on account of His kindness.

The fourth aspect:

The servant’s attachment to anything other than Allah harms him if he takes from it more than he needs to worship Allah. If he consumes food and drink beyond his needs, it harms and destroys him; the same applies to marital relations and clothing. If he loves something completely, to the point of intimate attachment, he will inevitably grow weary of it or part with it.

A transmitted report states:

“Love whatever you wish, for you will surely part with it. Do whatever you wish, for you will surely encounter it. Be however you wish, for as you act, so you shall be repaid.”

Know that whoever loves something for other than Allah’s sake will inevitably be harmed by what he loves, and it will become a cause of his torment. Thus, those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend it in Allah’s way will have their treasure take the form of a bald-headed serpent on the Day of Resurrection. It will seize its owner by the jaw, saying:

“I am your treasure. I am your wealth.”

Similar examples occur in hadith. Allah will say on the Day of Resurrection:

“O son of Adam, is it not just of Me to give each of you over to whatever he attached himself to in this world?”

And the basis of such attachment

---

## Page 31

is love. Thus, whoever loves anything apart from Allah will, on the Day of Resurrection, be given over by Allah to whatever he attached himself to, and Allah will consign him to Hell—an evil destination. Whoever loves something for other than Allah’s sake suffers harm whether it is present or absent. If it is absent, he is tormented by separation and suffers pain; if it is present, it brings him more pain than pleasure. This is known through reflection and observation. Whoever loves anything apart from Allah, for other than His sake, finds that its harm outweighs its benefit. Created things thus become a source of misery for him, except where his attachment is for Allah’s sake and in devotion to Him; such attachment perfects and beautifies the servant.

This is the meaning of the saying reported from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

“This world is accursed, and accursed is everything in it, except the remembrance of Allah and whatever is connected with it.”

Reported by al-Tirmidhī and others.

The fifth aspect:

Depending upon a created being and placing one’s trust in it brings harm from that very source, for it is there that one will be let down. This, too, is known through reflection and observation. Whenever the servant places his hope and trust in anyone other than Allah, he is disappointed from that quarter; whenever he seeks support from anyone other than Allah, he is forsaken.

Allah Most High has said:

“They have taken gods besides Allah to be a source of strength for them.”

“No! They will disown their worship and turn against them.”

These two aspects concerning created things correspond to the created servant’s worship and seeking help. Thus, when He said:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help,”

the servant’s well-being lay in worshipping Allah and seeking His help, whereas worshipping anything else and seeking its help brought him harm, ruin and corruption.

The sixth aspect:

Allah, glorified be He, is Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy, Generous, possessed of abundance, and Merciful. He bestows kindness upon His servant despite having no need of him. He wishes him well and removes harm from him, not to obtain any benefit from the servant or to ward off any harm to Himself, but out of mercy and kindness. Created servants, however, cannot conceivably act except for some benefit of their own. The most they can offer the servant is to love and honour him, and to bring

---

## Page 32

him some benefit and ward off some harm from him. Although this, too, is made possible by Allah Most High, they do it only for what they themselves gain from the servant, unless their action is for Allah’s sake.

When they love him, they seek to fulfil their own purpose through that love, whether they love him for his inward or outward beauty. When they love the prophets and the friends of Allah, they seek to meet them, for they wish to enjoy seeing them, hearing their words, and the like. Likewise, whoever loves someone for his courage, leadership, beauty or generosity must receive his own share of benefit from that love; were it not for the pleasure he takes in it, he would not love him. If they bring him some benefit, such as service or wealth, or ward off some harm, such as illness or an enemy—even through supplication or praise—they seek something in return, unless their action is for Allah’s sake.

The soldiers of kings, the slaves of an owner, the hired workers of a craftsman and the assistants of a leader all seek only to achieve their own purposes through the person they serve. Most give no thought to benefiting him, unless they have received instruction and moral training from another source—in which case this belongs to the religious sphere—or there is a natural disposition toward justice and kindness arising from reciprocity and compassion. Otherwise, their primary aim is their own benefit. This is part of Allah’s wisdom, through which He has secured the welfare of His creation, apportioned their livelihood among them in this worldly life, and raised some above others in rank:

“so that some may employ others in their service.”

Once this is understood, it becomes clear that a created being’s primary aim is not to benefit you, but to benefit himself through you. This may even cause you harm if he does not observe justice. Thus, when you call upon him, you have called upon someone more likely to harm you than to benefit you.

The Lord, glorified be He, wills your good and your benefit through you, not His own benefit from you. This brings you benefit without harm. Reflect upon this: keeping this aspect in mind prevents you from placing your hope in a created being or

---

## Page 33

seeking benefit for yourself from him, for this is not his primary aim, nor is he able to provide it. Do not let this lead you to treat people harshly, or to cease showing them kindness and patiently bearing the harm they cause. Rather, show them kindness for Allah’s sake, not because you place your hope in them. Just as you should not fear them, neither should you place your hope in them. Fear Allah in your dealings with people, and do not fear people in fulfilling your duty to Allah. Place your hope in Allah in your dealings with people, and do not place your hope in people in fulfilling your duty to Allah.

Be among those of whom Allah said:

“The most righteous will be kept away from it—he who gives his wealth to purify himself, not to repay anyone for a favour done to him, but only seeking the Face of his Lord, the Most High.”

He also said of them:

“We feed you only for the sake of Allah. We desire neither reward nor thanks from you.”

The seventh consideration:

Most people seek to fulfil their needs through you, even if this harms you. A person in need is blind to everything but satisfying that need.

The eighth consideration:

If some harm befalls you, such as fear, hunger or illness, created beings cannot ward it off except by Allah’s permission, nor do they seek to ward it off except to serve some purpose of their own.

The ninth consideration:

If created beings strove to benefit you, they could benefit you only with something Allah had already decreed for you. If they strove to harm you, they could harm you only with something Allah had already decreed against you. They can neither benefit nor harm you except by Allah’s permission, so do not place your hope in them.

Allah Most High said:

“Who is there to serve as an army for you, to help you besides the Most Merciful? The disbelievers are only in delusion. Or who is there to provide for you if He withholds His provision? Yet they persist in insolence and aversion.”

Help encompasses warding off harm, while provision encompasses obtaining benefit.

---

## Page 34

Allah Most High said:

“Let them worship the Lord of this House, who has fed them, saving them from hunger, and made them safe from fear.”

He also said:

“Have We not established for them a secure sanctuary to which fruits of every kind are brought as provision from Us?”

Abraham, Allah’s close friend, peace be upon him, said:

“My Lord, make this a secure city and provide its people with fruits”—the verse continues.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Are you provided for and granted help except through the weak among you?”

Through their supplications, prayers and sincerity.

---

## Page 35

Section

The sum of this is that if you yourself do not know what is best for you, cannot secure it, and do not desire it as you should, then other people are even less likely to know what is best for you, to be able to secure it, or to desire it for you. Allah—glory be to Him—is the One who knows while you do not, who has power while you do not, and who grants you from His immense bounty, as expressed in the hadith of istikhāra:

“O Allah, I seek Your guidance through Your knowledge, and I ask You to grant me ability through Your power, and I ask You of Your immense bounty. For You have power and I do not; You know and I do not; and You are the Knower of all that is unseen.”

---

## Page 36

Section

This section serves as a kind of introduction to what follows: every human being forms intentions, acts, perceives, and moves by his own will. Indeed, every living being is likewise endowed with knowledge and acts by its own will. Will means volition and choice. Any voluntary, freely chosen action necessarily has an intended object—the thing sought. That object can be attained only through causes and means that bring it about. If it is attained through the servant’s own action, ability and strength are necessary; if through something external, another agent is necessary; and if through both his own action and something external, means such as tools and the like are necessary. Thus, every living being necessarily has a will, and everyone who wills something necessarily needs help to attain it. The servant is therefore naturally constituted to seek and desire something, and to seek help from something and rely upon it to attain what he desires. This is an inescapable necessity for every human being, which each finds within himself.

Yet both the object of desire and the source of help fall into two categories. Something may be desired for the sake of something else, or it may be desired for its own sake.

As for the source of help, it may be sought in its own right, or it may be subordinate to the source of help and serve as its instrument. Among the objects of desire is that which constitutes the ultimate end sought: it is what the seeker humbles himself before and loves, and it is the deity toward which he directs himself. Other things are desired for the sake of something else, so that the actual object of desire is that other thing; these are therefore indirect objects of desire.

Among the sources of help is that which constitutes the ultimate source upon which the servant relies, in which he places his trust, and from which he draws support. He recognizes no source beyond it from which to seek help. Other sources are subordinate to another, as the limbs are to the heart, property to its owner, and tools to the craftsman.

---

## Page 37

If a person reflects on his own condition and that of all people, he will find that these two things are inseparable from them: the soul must have something in which it finds reassurance and which is the ultimate object of its love; this is its deity. It must also have something it trusts and relies upon to attain what it seeks; this is its source of help, whether that is Allah or someone else.

This may take an all-encompassing form, which is disbelief, as with someone who worships other than Allah absolutely and appeals to other than Allah absolutely. Such are the worshippers of the sun, the moon, and other things, who ask them to fulfil their needs and turn to them in times of calamity. It may also take a particular form among Muslims, as with someone so overcome by love of wealth, love of a person, or love of leadership that he becomes its slave. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Wretched is the slave of the dirham! Wretched is the slave of the dinar! Wretched is the slave of the cloak! Wretched is the slave of the plush garment! If he is given something, he is pleased; if he is denied it, he is resentful. May he fall into misery and be brought low! If a thorn pierces him, may he fail to remove it!”

Likewise, someone may be overcome by confidence in his standing and wealth, believing that the leader or other person he serves, the assistant or soldier or other person who serves him, his friends, or his possessions are what bring him a particular benefit and ward off a particular harm. He therefore relies upon them and seeks their help. The source of help is the one called upon and asked.

How often worship entails seeking help! Whoever the heart relies upon for its provision, support, benefit, and protection from harm, it submits to him, humbles itself before him, obeys him, and loves him on that account, even if it does not love him for his own sake. Yet this state may come to dominate it until it loves him for his own sake and forgets what it originally sought from him. This happens to many who love wealth or love someone through whom they attain honour and power.

As for someone whom the heart loves, desires, and seeks, it may not seek his help or rely upon him unless it perceives his ability to secure what it desires—as when a lover perceives that the beloved is able to bring about their union.

---

## Page 38

so if he perceives that the beloved can secure what he seeks, he seeks his help; otherwise, he does not. There are thus three possibilities: someone may be loved without being sought for help, sought for help without being loved, or both.

If it is understood that, at every moment and in every circumstance, a servant must have an ultimate object that he seeks—his deity—and an ultimate source from which he seeks help—his source of help, the one to whom he turns in seeking help and in worship—then it becomes clear that His words:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

are comprehensive and all-encompassing, from beginning to end, leaving nothing outside their scope. The categories therefore become four.

A person may worship someone other than Allah and seek his help—even though he is a Muslim—for associating partners with Allah in this community is more hidden than the creeping of ants.

Or he may worship Allah while seeking help from someone else. This is the case with many religious people: they intend to obey Allah and His Messenger and to worship Him alone, without any partner, yet their hearts submit to those whom they perceive as the source of their support, provision, and guidance, whether kings, wealthy people, or shaykhs.

Or he may seek Allah’s help—even though he worships someone else. This is the case with many who experience spiritual states, possess power, or wield inward or outward authority, and with people of unveiling and extraordinary influence. They seek His help, rely upon Him, ask Him, and turn to Him for refuge; yet their aim is something other than what Allah and His Messenger have commanded, and other than following His religion and the revealed law with which Allah sent His Messenger.

The fourth category consists of those who worship none but Him and seek help from none but Him.

This fourfold classification is also mentioned later. At times, however, it is based on worship and seeking help, and at other times on the one whose help is sought. Here it is based on the one worshipped and the one whose help is sought, to show that every servant must have an object of worship and a source of help. Later, it is based on worshipping Allah and seeking His help, for people fall into four categories in this regard.

---

## Page 39

And Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section on the obligation to worship the Creator alone and place one’s trust in Him

One must act only for His sake and place one’s hope in Him alone. He, glorified be He, took the initiative in creating you and bestowing His blessings upon you through His own power over you, His will, and His mercy, without any cause whatsoever originating from you. No one else has the power to do what He has done for you.

Then, when you need Him to bring you provision or ward off harm, He alone brings that provision; no one else brings it. He alone wards off that harm; no one else wards it off (*).

As He, the Exalted, says:

“Or who is there that could be an army for you to aid you other than the Most Merciful? The disbelievers are only in delusion.”

“Or who is there that could provide for you if He withheld His provision? Yet they persist in insolence and aversion.”

He, glorified be He, Himself bestows blessings upon you and treats you with kindness. This necessarily follows from the names and attributes by which He has named and described Himself, for He is the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful, the Most Loving, the Most Glorious. His power is inherent in Him and inseparable from His Essence, as are His mercy, knowledge, and wisdom. He has no need of His creation in any respect; rather, He is free of all need of the worlds:

“Whoever is grateful is grateful only for his own benefit; and whoever is ungrateful—indeed, my Lord is Free of need and Generous.”

“And when your Lord proclaimed, ‘If you are grateful, I will surely increase you; but if you are ungrateful, indeed, My punishment is severe.’”

“And Moses said, ‘If you and everyone on earth were to disbelieve, indeed, Allah is Free of need and Praiseworthy.’”

In the authentic hadith conveying Allah’s words:

“O My servants, if the first of you and the last of you, and the humans among you
