## Page 40

and the jinn among you were as wicked at heart as the most wicked man among you, that would not diminish My dominion in the least. If they were as devout at heart as the most devout man among you, that would not increase My dominion in the least. If they stood together on a single plain and asked Me, and I granted each one what he requested, that would not diminish what I possess in the least”—and so on to the end of the hadith.

The Lord, glorified be He, is inherently free of all need. The attributes of perfection that are His due belong to Him inherently and necessarily, being inseparable from His Essence. In none of this does He depend upon anyone else.

Rather, His actions arise from His perfection: He is perfect, and therefore He acts. His kindness and generosity likewise arise from His perfection. He does nothing out of any need whatsoever for anyone else. Rather, whatever He wills, He does, for He always does whatever He wills.

He, glorified be He, accomplishes His purpose. Whatever He seeks, He attains, obtains, and reaches on His own. No one assists Him, and no one hinders Him. He needs no helper in any of His affairs; none of His creatures lends Him support, nor does He have a protector out of weakness.

---

## Page 41

Section

The more humble a servant is before Allah, and the greater his need of Him and submission to Him, the closer he is to Him, the dearer he is to Him, and the higher his standing. Thus, the happiest of all creatures are those whose servitude to Allah is greatest.

As for created beings, it has been said:

“Depend on whomever you wish, and you become his captive; be independent of whomever you wish, and you become his equal; do good to whomever you wish, and you become his master.”

The poet indeed spoke truly:

“Between abasement and presumptuous familiarity lies a dot …
Its removal leaves minds bewildered.
Such abasement is associating partners with Allah …
So understand, young man, by contradiction.” (1)

A servant enjoys the greatest standing and respect among people when he has no need of them whatsoever. If you do good to them while remaining independent of them, they will hold you in the highest esteem. Whenever you need them—even for a drink of water—your standing in their eyes diminishes in proportion to your need of them. This is part of Allah’s wisdom and mercy, so that all worship may be devoted to Allah and nothing associated with Him.

Hence, when Hātim al-Asamm was asked, “How can one be safe from people?” he replied, “Make what you possess freely available to them, while expecting nothing of what they possess.”

But if you compensate them for what they provide, and they too are in need, then, if your respective needs are equal, you stand as equals, like two parties to a sale: neither has an advantage over the other. If their need of you is greater, they will submit to you.

As for the Lord, glorified be He, you are dearest to Him when you are most in need of Him. And when you are most in need

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 42

of Him.

As for created beings, you are least esteemed by them when you are most in need of them, for they are all needy themselves. They do not know your needs or know how to secure what is good for you. Indeed, they are ignorant of what is good for themselves, so how could they know how to secure what is good for others? They cannot accomplish it, nor do they desire it of their own accord. Thus, they have neither the knowledge, the ability, nor the will.

The Lord, Most High, knows what is good for you, is able to secure it, and wills it out of His mercy and bounty. This is an attribute inherent in Him; nothing else has made Him willing and merciful. Rather, His mercy is inseparable from His very being: He has prescribed mercy for Himself, and His mercy encompasses all things. Created beings, by contrast, are all needy. They do nothing except to meet their own needs and serve their own interests. This is their duty and the course of wisdom; nothing else is fitting for them. Yet the happy among them are those who pursue what truly serves their interests, rather than what they imagine serves them but does not.

They therefore fall into three classes: the unjust, the just, and the beneficent.

The unjust person takes wealth or some benefit from you without giving you anything in return, or benefits himself by harming you.

The just person gives an equivalent in return, like a seller: the exchange is neither to your advantage nor to your disadvantage. Each helps sustain the order of life, and each of the two needs the other, as with spouses, buyers and sellers, and partners.

The beneficent person does good without seeking compensation from you. Yet this, too, is an act that serves his own need and interest: he benefits from doing good and obtains through it something his soul loves, such as reward, the praise and esteem of others, a closer relationship with you, or something else.

In any case, he has only done good to you because he hopes to benefit. All other created beings honor and esteem you only because they need you and benefit from you, either

---

## Page 43

through reciprocal exchange—for buyers and sellers, partners, and spouses each need one another; a master needs his slaves, and they need him; kings need their soldiers, and the soldiers need them; and this is the basis on which the affairs of the world are ordered—or through the good you do them.

When your relatives, friends, and others honor you for your own sake, they love and honor you only because of the honor they gain through you personally. If you were to turn away, they would turn away from you and abandon you. In reality, they love only themselves and their own interests.

Among all these people, from kings to those beneath them, you may find someone who is a master obeyed by others, yet in reality he is himself an obedient servant. If any of them suffers harm because of his master or someone he obeys, the relationship changes according to the circumstances. Whenever you need them, their love, honor, and esteem diminish in proportion to that need, even if they meet it.

As for the Lord, Most High, it is impossible for any created being to recompense Him or bestow a favor upon Him. This is why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would say when his meal was cleared away:

“All praise is due to Allah—abundant, good, and blessed praise. Our Lord needs no one to provide for Him; His favors are never denied, He is never forsaken, and we can never do without Him.”

Al-Bukhārī related this from Abū Umāmah.

Rather, Allah alone is always the One who bestows blessings and favors upon His servant, with no partner in this. Indeed, every blessing enjoyed by all created beings comes from Allah. The servant’s happiness lies in his complete dependence upon Allah and need of Him, and in recognizing and understanding this and conducting himself accordingly—that is, in accordance with his knowledge of it.

A person may become poor without knowing it: his wealth may be lost while he remains unaware, thinking that it is still there. Once he learns that it is gone, his state changes. Likewise, all created beings are in need of Allah. Yet the people of disbelief and hypocrisy are ignorant and heedless of this, turning away from remembering it and acting upon it. The believer, however, acknowledges it and acts in accordance with that acknowledgment. These are the servants of Allah.

---

## Page 44

Thus, the human being—and every created being—is inherently dependent upon Allah. This dependence is inseparable from his very being: he cannot be anything but dependent upon his Creator. None is self-sufficient except Allah alone. He is al-Samad (the One on whom all depend), who has no need of anything besides Himself, while everything besides Him needs Him. The servant needs Allah both as his Lord and as his God, as has been explained at length elsewhere. The human being continually sins; he is both needy and sinful. His Lord, Most High, shows him mercy and forgives him, for He is the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.

Were it not for His mercy and goodness, no good would exist at all, either in this world or in the Hereafter. Were it not for His forgiveness, the servant could not protect himself from the evil consequences of his sins. He constantly needs blessings to come to him and harm and evil to be averted. Blessings come only through His mercy, and evil is averted only through His forgiveness, for the sins of His servants are the sole cause of evil. As Allah, Most High, says:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

Here, “evil” means the calamities that distress the servant, and “good” means the blessings that bring him joy. As He says:

“We tested them with good things and bad.”

Blessings, mercy, and good all come from Allah through His grace and generosity, without anyone having, in his own right, any claim upon Him. Even if His servants have a right upon Him, Most High, that is a right He Himself has established as binding upon Himself. It does not originate with the created being, but with Allah, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Calamities occur because of the servants’ sins and deeds. As He says:

“Whatever calamity befalls you is because of what your own hands have earned, and He pardons much.”

Even when blessings result from acts of obedience that the servant performs and for which Allah rewards him, He, glorified be He, is the One who bestows the servant’s very being, his obedience, and his reward for it. For He, glorified be He, created the servant and made him an obedient Muslim. As Allah’s intimate friend said:

“The One who created me, and who guides me.”

And he said: “And make us both submissive

---

## Page 45

to You.” And he said:

“Make me one who establishes prayer.”

And He says:

“And We made from among them leaders guiding by Our command when they were patient and were certain of Our signs.”

Thus, he asked his Lord to make him submissive to Him and to make him one who establishes prayer.

And He says:

“But Allah has endeared faith to you and made it beautiful in your hearts.”

The verse concludes:

“As a bounty and blessing from Allah.”

In the Sahīh of Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Hibbān:

“Guide us to the paths of peace, deliver us from darkness into light, and make us grateful for Your blessing, praising You for it and accepting it; and complete it for us.”

And in al-Fātihah:

“Guide us to the straight path.”

In the supplication narrated by al-Tabarānī from Ibn ‘Abbās, he said: Among the supplications offered by the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, on the afternoon of ‘Arafah was:

“O Allah, You hear my words, see where I am, and know what I conceal and what I reveal. Nothing about me is hidden from You. I am wretched and needy, seeking help and protection, fearful and apprehensive, confessing my sin. I ask You as one in need asks; I implore You as a humbled sinner implores; and I call upon You as one who is fearful and afflicted calls—one whose neck bows before You, whose body is humbled before You, and whose pride is abased before You. O Allah, do not leave me disappointed in my supplication to You, my Lord. Be kind and merciful to me, O best of those who are asked, O best of those who give.”

The word “servant” in the Quran encompasses those who worship Allah. A servant who does not worship Him, however, is not called “His servant.” As He says:

“Indeed, you have no authority over My servants.”

As for His words:

“Except those of the misguided who follow you,”

the exception here is discontinuous, as most Quran commentators and scholars have stated.

And He says:

“A spring from which the servants of Allah will drink.”

“The servants of the Most Merciful are those who walk humbly upon the earth.”

“And remember Our servant David.”

“What an excellent servant! Indeed, he was ever turning to Allah.”

“And remember Our servant Job.”

“And remember Our servants Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.”

“Then they found a servant from among Our servants.”

“Glory be to

---

## Page 46

Him who took His servant by night.”

“Indeed, he was a grateful servant.”

“And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our servant.”

“Then He revealed to His servant what He revealed.”

“And when the servant of Allah stood up to call upon Him.”

“Blessed is He who sent down the Criterion upon His servant.”

There are many similar examples. The word “servant” may also be applied to all created beings, as in His words:

“Indeed, those whom you invoke besides Allah are servants like you.”

“Do those who disbelieve think that they can take My servants as protectors besides Me?”

It may be said here that the angels and prophets are meant: if He has forbidden taking them as protectors, then this applies all the more to others. For He has said:

“There is no one in the heavens and the earth but will come to the Most Merciful as a servant.”

In the authentic hadith concerning the Dajjāl, narrated by Muslim:

“Allah will then reveal to the Messiah: ‘I have servants whom no one has the strength to fight.’”

This is like His words:

“We sent against you servants of Ours.”

These people were not obedient to Allah, but they are held in servitude, humbled and subdued, and His decree takes effect upon them.

Their being servants may also refer to their acknowledgment of the Creator and their submission to Him, even though they are disbelievers, as in His words:

“And most of them do not believe in Allah without associating partners with Him.”

And His words:

“But will come to the Most Merciful as a servant,”

mean humbled and submissive. It is well known that this is the only condition in which they will come on the Day of Resurrection; their arrogant refusal to worship Allah occurred only in this world. Then He says:

“He has certainly accounted for them and numbered them precisely.”

“And each of them will come to Him on the Day of Resurrection alone.”

He thus mentions afterward that each will come alone, as in His words:

“And you have come to Us alone, just as We created you the first time.”

And He says:

“To Him has submitted whoever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly, and to Him they will be returned.”

“To Allah prostrates whoever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly,”

to the end of the verse.

And He says:

“Rather, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth; all are obedient to Him.”

What is meant by this is not merely that they are created, governed and subdued under His will and power, for this

---

## Page 47

cannot be described as occurring willingly or unwillingly, for willingness and unwillingness apply only to what an agent does willingly or unwillingly. As for something in which a person has no active part, he cannot on that account be described as prostrating or obedient, or even as submitting. Rather, all acknowledge the Maker by their innate nature, and they are humbled, submissive, obedient, and compelled in various respects.

These include their awareness of their need for Him and their utter dependence upon Him; their calling upon Him in dire necessity; and their submission and surrender to the decrees He brings upon them and to His will.

They also comply with much of what He has commanded in every matter. Other people do not allow a person to have whatever he wants; rather, they constrain him and compel him to abide by justice that he dislikes, although it is something Allah has commanded. Their disobedience to Him in some of what He has commanded—even if it concerns monotheism—does not prevent them from being obedient, humbled, and submissive unwillingly, like the disobedient among Muslims, those under a covenant of protection, and others. They are subject to the religion with which He sent His messengers, even though they disobey Him in certain matters.

The believer willingly submits to his Lord’s command, and likewise to the afflictions He decrees. When these occur, he willingly does what he has been commanded to do, such as exercising patience and the like. Thus, he willingly submits to Allah and willingly humbles himself before Him. The purpose of prostration is humble submission, and each thing prostrates according to its nature, in a manner suited to it and involving submission to the Lord.

As for created beings’ dependence upon Allah—meaning that they all need Him, and that neither they nor any of their attributes or actions can exist except through Him—this is the first degree of dependence. It is their dependence upon His lordship over them, His creation of them, and His perfecting of them. In this respect, they belong to Him, and to Him, glorified is He, belong dominion and praise. This is known to everyone who believes in Allah and His messengers with the required faith. Thus, coming into existence

---

## Page 48

is evidence that the prophets depend upon the One who brought them into being. Likewise, their continuing need for Him after He has brought them into being is evidence of their dependence, for the need for provision demonstrates the dependence of those receiving it upon the Creator and Provider.

The correct view is that things depend upon the Creator by their very nature, not because something else has made them dependent upon Him. Rather, their dependence is inseparable from them: they cannot be independent of Him. Likewise, the Lord’s self-sufficiency is inseparable from Him: He cannot be other than self-sufficient. He is self-sufficient in Himself, not through an attribute that has made Him so. Things are characterized by dependence upon the Creator both when they do not exist and when they exist. Thus, if rain whose arrival is awaited is described, before it exists, as dependent upon the Creator, this means that it can come into existence only through the Creator. This is the view of the majority of Muslim speculative thinkers and others.

This dependence is known through reason. What the Quran affirms concerning created beings’ submission, prostration, glorification, and devout obedience is something beyond this, according to Muslims generally among the early generations and the majority of later scholars. One group, however, claims that their dependence, submission, being created, and subjection to His will constitute their glorification and devout obedience, even if this is expressed through their condition and through their being evidence that bears witness to the Creator, exalted is His majesty.

Ask the earth who caused its rivers to flow, planted its trees, and brought forth its vegetation and fruits. If it does not answer you in speech, it will instead answer you through reflection. This is what al-Ghazālī and others say. It is also one of the interpretations mentioned by Abū Bakr ibn al-Anbārī concerning His words:

“All are devoutly obedient to Him.”

He said: “Every created being is obedient to Him. He has directly fashioned it and applied His decrees to it; this is evidence of its humble submission to its Lord.”

It is also the interpretation given by al-Zajjāj concerning His words:

“To Him submits whoever is in the heavens and the earth.”

He said: “The submission of all consists in their yielding to the operation of His command within their created nature. No one can resist the nature upon which Allah has fashioned him.” This meaning is sound, but the correct view

---

## Page 49

held by the majority of scholars among the early and later generations is that devout obedience, submission, and glorification are something beyond this. This resembles the statement made by some that an unwilling person’s prostration consists in his humble submission and yielding to whatever Allah wills for him, whether health or illness, wealth or poverty. Likewise, some have interpreted His words:

“There is not a thing except that it glorifies Him with praise.”

They say: “Its glorification consists in its pointing to its Maker, thereby prompting others to glorify Him.” The correct view, however, is that created things glorify Him and prostrate in ways appropriate to them.

The point is that created beings’ dependence upon the Creator, their pointing to Him, and their bearing witness to Him are matters known through the innate disposition upon which Allah has created His servants. Likewise, He has created them with an innate disposition to acknowledge Him even without these signs. This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where the distinction between the evidence furnished by signs and that furnished by syllogistic and analogical inference has been explained. Rational demonstrative inference may be formulated either in universal terms, as in the logical figures, or in analogical terms. It has also been explained that the common factor is the cause underlying the judgment, and that the judgment must hold wherever that factor is found. We have discussed the forms of these two kinds of inference at length elsewhere.

The precise account is that knowing that whatever comes into existence must have someone who brings it into existence is innate and immediate knowledge in the case of specific particulars, and is more forceful there than in the corresponding universal proposition. Universals become universals in the mind only after their particular instances are established in existence. The same applies to most universal propositions that many speculative theologians and philosophizers make the foundations of their knowledge, such as: “The whole is greater than the part”; “Two contradictories cannot both be true or both be false”; and “Things equal to the same thing are equal to one another,” and similar propositions. Whatever whole a person conceives of, he knows that it is greater than its part, even if the universal proposition does not occur to him. Thus, he knows that some parts of the human body are larger than others, that a dirham is larger than a portion of it, and that a city is larger than a part of it

---

## Page 50

and that a mountain is larger than a part of it. The same applies to the two contradictories, namely existence and nonexistence. When a person conceives of the existence and nonexistence of anything whatsoever, he knows that it cannot be both existent and nonexistent at the same time, nor can it be neither existent nor nonexistent. He makes this judgment about specific particulars even without bringing the universal proposition to mind. The same holds for similar cases.

Since universal syllogistic inference yields a general result rather than a specifically identified one, the existence of the Maker must be established through signs, as set forth in the Quran and as Allah has innately disposed His servants to do. Although the syllogistic method is valid, its benefit is incomplete. When the Quran employs inference in verses concerning divine matters, it uses a fortiori reasoning, not inference that establishes a common attribute. Whatever imperfections and defects contain no perfection, and from which a created being must be held free, the Maker, exalted is He, must all the more be held free of them. Likewise, whatever perfection containing no deficiency is established for a created being—such as life, knowledge, and power—the Creator has an even greater right to it. Thus, all created things are signs of the Creator.

The difference between a sign and syllogistic inference is that a sign points to the very object sought, of which it is a sign and an indication. Every created thing is therefore evidence and a sign of the Creator Himself, as we have explained at length elsewhere. Moreover, innate dispositions recognize the Creator without these signs, for they have been formed to do so. If they did not recognize Him without these signs, they would not know that a given sign points to Him.

For a thing to be a sign of Him and to point to Him is like a name pointing to what it names: one must already have conceived of what is named and recognized that this is its name. Likewise, for one thing to be evidence of another requires conceiving of what the evidence indicates and conceiving of that evidence as necessarily entailing it. One must therefore know that the evidence necessarily entails what it indicates. If what it indicates had not been conceived of, it would not be known that the evidence points to it,

---

## Page 51

for knowledge of a relation depends on conceiving of both the thing related and that to which it is related. Yet a person may be unaware of this relation or of the thing’s being evidence. Once he conceives of it, he recognizes what it indicates, provided he knows that it necessarily entails it. People know that these created things are signs and proofs of the Creator; they must therefore know Him in order to know that these proofs necessarily entail His existence. The point is that these rational methods, rooted in innate human disposition, are those presented by the Quran: reason and revelation agree, and rational reflection and transmitted revelation go hand in hand.

Those who espouse philosophy, such as Ibn Sīnā, al-Rāzī, and their followers, said:

“The way to establish His existence is to infer it from contingent beings, since a contingent being requires a necessary being.”

They said:

“Existence is either necessary or contingent, and contingent existence requires a necessary being. Thus, the existence of a necessary being follows on either supposition.”

Ibn Sīnā introduced this position, combining the statements of the speculative theologians with those of his philosophical predecessors. The speculative theologians divided existence into beginningless and originated existence, whereas he divided it into necessary and contingent existence. This was because, in his view, the celestial sphere had not come into being; nevertheless, he claimed that it was contingent. No philosopher before him had proposed this division. Indeed, the most accomplished among them recognized that it was erroneous and that he had departed from his predecessors, the majority of rational thinkers, and others.

We have explained elsewhere that beginningless existence and necessary existence mutually entail one another according to rational thinkers generally, both earlier and later. No group among them is known to have disputed this, apart from the position newly introduced by these people. We witness many things coming into existence after not having existed, and we witness their ceasing to exist after having existed. Whatever was nonexistent, or will become nonexistent, cannot be necessary in existence or eternal and without beginning.

Furthermore, even supposing that these people have established a necessarily existent being, nothing in their proof establishes that it is distinct from the heavens and celestial spheres. This is one of the points on which al-Ghazālī and others exposed the incoherence of their arguments, but

---

## Page 52

their main argument is that a body cannot be necessary in existence because it is composite, and a necessary being cannot be composite. This is the basis of their argument. We have demonstrated its invalidity in many ways. Thinkers have continually exposed the flaws in this position, each in his own way, just as al-Ghazālī did in his.

The problem is that the term “necessary” has come to bear several distinct meanings. It may refer to that which exists of itself and cannot admit nonexistence; in this sense, both the essence and the attributes are necessary. It may also refer to that which exists of itself and subsists in itself; in this sense, the essence is necessary, but not the attributes. It may further refer to the originator of contingent beings—that is, created things—whose originator is the Creator. In this sense, the necessary being is the essence endowed with those attributes. The essence, stripped of its attributes, was not created, nor were the attributes, stripped of the essence. Thus, those who followed them while claiming profound understanding and spiritual knowledge came to identify the necessary being with absolute existence, as has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Our purpose here, first of all, is to explain that a servant’s happiness lies in his complete dependence upon his Lord and his need for Him: that is, in perceiving and recognizing this dependence, and displaying the humility, submission, and reverence it requires. All created beings are in need in any case; yet a person may imagine himself to possess some measure of self-sufficiency and therefore transgress.

As Allah Most High says:

“No indeed! Man surely transgresses”

“because he sees himself as self-sufficient.”

And He says:

“When We bestow favor upon man, he turns away and draws aside; but when adversity touches him, he offers lengthy supplications.”

And in the other verse:

“he is ever despairing.”

---

## Page 53

Section

Happiness in dealing with others lies in dealing with them for Allah’s sake: placing your hope in Allah in your dealings with them, not in them in your dealings with Allah; fearing Him in your dealings with them, not them in your dealings with Allah; doing good to them in hope of Allah’s reward, not their repayment; and refraining from wronging them out of fear of Allah, not of them.

As a report states:

“Place your hope in Allah in your dealings with people, and do not place your hope in people in your dealings with Allah. Fear Allah in your dealings with people, and do not fear people in your dealings with Allah.”

That is, do not perform any act of worship or devotion for their sake, whether in hope of their praise or fear of their criticism. Rather, place your hope in Allah, and do not fear them when acting for Allah’s sake, whether in what you do or what you refrain from doing. Instead, do what you have been commanded, even if they dislike it.

A hadith states:

“It is a sign of weak certainty to please people by incurring Allah’s displeasure, or to blame them for what Allah has not given you.”

For certainty encompasses certainty in carrying out Allah’s commands and in what Allah has promised those who obey Him. It also encompasses certainty concerning Allah’s decree, His creation, and His governance. If you please people by incurring Allah’s displeasure, you lack certainty in both His promise and His provision. What drives a person to this is either a desire for the worldly possessions in their hands, leading him to neglect Allah’s commands in his dealings with them because of what he hopes to receive from them; or weak belief in the victory, support, and reward that Allah has promised those who obey Him, in this world and the Hereafter. If you please Allah, He will grant you victory, provide for you, and spare you the burden of dealing with them. Pleasing them by incurring His displeasure arises only from fearing them and placing your hope in them.

This is a sign of weak certainty. And if what you expect them to do for you has not been decreed for you, then the matter rests with Allah, not with them

---

## Page 54

for whatever He wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not. If you blame them for what has not been decreed, that is a sign of your weak certainty. So do not fear them, place your hope in them, or blame them on account of your own interests and desires. Rather, whoever is praised by Allah and His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, deserves praise; and whoever is condemned by Allah and His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, deserves condemnation.

When one of the delegation of Banū Tamīm said, “O Muhammad, give me something, for my praise brings honor and my blame brings disgrace,” the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, replied, “That is true of Allah, Mighty and Majestic.”

Ā’ishah wrote to Mu‘āwiyah—and it is also reported that she attributed this to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Whoever pleases Allah by incurring people’s displeasure, Allah will spare him the burden of dealing with them. Whoever pleases people by incurring Allah’s displeasure, they will be of no avail to him against Allah.”

This is the wording of the report traced back to the Prophet. The wording of the report attributed to Ā’ishah herself is:

“Whoever pleases Allah by incurring people’s displeasure, Allah will be pleased with him and cause people to be pleased with him. Whoever pleases people by incurring Allah’s displeasure, those who praised him will come to blame him.”

This is the wording transmitted from her, and it contains some of the profoundest understanding of religion. The wording traced back to the Prophet is more accurate and more truthful. Whoever pleases Allah by incurring their displeasure has indeed feared Him and become His righteous servant. Allah takes care of the righteous, and He is sufficient for His servant:

“And whoever fears Allah—He will make for him a way out”

“And will provide for him from where he does not expect.”

Thus, Allah will undoubtedly spare him the burden of dealing with people. As for all people being pleased with him, that may not happen. Yet they will be pleased with him if they are free of personal aims and the eventual outcome becomes clear to them. Whoever pleases people by incurring Allah’s displeasure, they will be of no avail to him against Allah. He is like the wrongdoer who bites his hand, saying:

“Oh, I wish I had taken a path with the Messenger.”

“Oh, woe to me! I wish I had not taken that person as a close friend.”

As for those who praised him turning to blame him, this happens often and comes about in the end, for the final outcome belongs to righteousness. This does not happen at the outset, when their desires hold sway. And He, glorified be He, knows best.

Monotheism is the opposite of associating partners with Allah. Thus, if a servant upholds monotheism, which is Allah’s right, and worships Him

---

## Page 55

without associating anything with Him, he is a monotheist.

Part of affirming Allah’s oneness and worshipping Him is placing one’s trust in Him, setting one’s hope on Him, and fearing Him. Through this, the servant frees himself from associating partners with Allah. By giving people their due rights and refraining from aggression against them, he frees himself from wronging them and from associating them as partners with Allah.

By obeying his Lord and avoiding disobedience to Him, the servant frees himself from wronging himself. Allah Most High has said in a sacred hadith:

“I have divided prayer between Myself and My servant into two halves.”

Both halves benefit the servant. Likewise, in the hadith narrated by al-Tabarānī in al-Duʿāʾ (Supplication):

“O My servants, there are only four matters: one for Me, one for you, one between Me and you, and one between you and My creation. What is for Me is that you worship Me without associating anything with Me. What is for you is your work: I will reward you for it when you are most in need of it. What is between Me and you is that you supplicate and I answer. What is between you and My creation is that you treat them as you would like them to treat you.”

Allah loves both halves, and He loves that they worship Him. The help and guidance Allah gives the servant are from His bounty and kindness, and they are a means to what He loves. He loves them only because they provide a path to worshipping Him. The servant first seeks what he needs: he needs help in worship and guidance to the straight path, and through these he attains worship. Thus, he first seeks what he needs so that he may use it to attain what his Lord loves, wherein lies his happiness.

The same applies to His words:

“Your work: I will reward you for it when you are most in need of it.”

For the servant loves the reward that is the recompense for his work. The servant therefore works only for himself:

“It will have the good it has earned, and bear the evil it has earned.”

Then, when he seeks to worship, he seeks it only insofar as it benefits him, brings him happiness, and protects him from his Lord’s punishment. A servant never seeks anything except that which holds some benefit for him. Even if his Lord loves it, he seeks it insofar as it suits him. Whoever worships Allah without associating anything with Him, Allah loves and rewards him. Thus there comes

---

## Page 56

to the servant what he desires in the way of blessings, as a consequence of what his Lord loves. This is like the seller and the buyer: the seller primarily wants payment from the buyer, which necessarily entails wanting to hand over the goods; the buyer wants the goods, which necessarily entails wanting to pay the price.

The Lord loves to be loved, and this necessarily entails loving those without whom worship cannot take place. The servant loves what he needs and benefits from, and this necessarily entails his love of worshipping Allah. Whoever worships Allah and treats people well thus fulfills Allah’s rights and the rights of His servants while devoting his religion sincerely to Him. Whoever seeks recompense from people—whether praise, supplication, or anything else—has not treated them well for Allah’s sake.

Whoever fears Allah in his dealings with people, but does not fear them when fulfilling his duty to Allah, does good both to creation and to himself. His fear of Allah prompts him to give them their rights and refrain from wronging them. Whoever fears them but does not fear Allah wrongs both himself and them by fearing someone other than Allah and placing his hope in that other. For when he fears them rather than Allah, he feels compelled to ward off their harm by every means: either by ingratiating himself with them and putting on a show for them, or by responding with harm equal to or greater than theirs. When he places his hope in them, he fails to fulfill Allah’s rights in his dealings with them. If he does not fear Allah, he chooses to act aggressively against them. The soul is naturally inclined to wrong those who do not wrong it—so how much more those who do?

You therefore find such a person greatly afraid of others, frequently unjust when he has power, and abased and humiliated when overpowered. His fear of people is proportionate to what he himself harbors of these traits. This is among the things that provoke strife between people. Likewise, when he places his hope in them and they do not give him what he hopes to receive, he inevitably comes to hate them and then wrongs them, unless he fears Allah, Mighty and Majestic.

This is widespread among people: you find them fearing one another and placing their hopes in one another. Each complains of being wronged by the other while seeking to wrong him. Thus they wrong one another; they violate Allah’s rights by fearing others and placing their hopes in others; and they wrong themselves. These are sins through which the soul suffers and for which it is punished. This also leads to committing particular sins, such as associating partners with Allah and unlawful sexual intercourse. For if a person does not fear

---

## Page 57

Allah, he follows his desires, especially when seeking something he has not obtained. His soul continues to seek something that will give it relief and dispel its distress and sorrow, yet it lacks the remembrance and worship of Allah in which it could find comfort and rest. He therefore seeks relief in forbidden things: acts of indecency, drinking forbidden substances, false speech, recounting personal happenings, jesting and play, keeping bad company, and other such things. Yet the heart finds sufficiency only through worshipping Allah Most High.

The human being was created needing to obtain what benefits him and ward off what harms him. His soul constantly desires something, and it must have an ultimate object of desire in which it can find rest and reassurance. This can be none other than Allah alone: hearts find reassurance only in Him, and souls find rest only in turning to Him. And:

“Had there been gods in the heavens and the earth besides Allah, both would have fallen into ruin.”

Thus, every object of worship besides Him brings corruption, and hearts can be set right only by worshipping Allah alone, without any partner.

When hearts do not devote their religion sincerely to Allah, they worship others besides Him—gods that most people worship and have chosen for themselves. They thereby associate partners with Allah by worshipping others and seeking their help. They worship and seek help from others because they do not know the happiness they can attain by worshipping their Creator and seeking His help. Through worshipping Him, they have no need of another object of worship; through seeking His help, they have no need to seek help from creation.

If the servant is not like this, he remains sinful and needy. His sufficiency lies only in obedience to his Lord. This is the human condition: he is poor and needy, and, at the same time, sinful and prone to error. He therefore cannot do without his Lord, for it is He who bestows his maghāfir; and he must seek forgiveness for his sins. Allah Most High says:

“So know that there is no god but Allah, and seek forgiveness for your sin.”

Through affirming Allah’s oneness, the servant gains strength and sufficiency. Whoever wishes to be the strongest of people should put his trust in Allah. Through seeking forgiveness, he receives Allah’s forgiveness, and Allah averts His punishment from him:

“Allah would not punish them while they seek forgiveness.”

Thus the servant’s poverty and need are not dispelled

---

## Page 58

except through affirming Allah’s oneness, for he cannot do without it. If he does not attain it, he remains poor, needy, and tormented in his pursuit of what he has not attained. And Allah Most High “does not forgive that partners should be associated with Him.”

When seeking forgiveness accompanies affirming Allah’s oneness, the servant attains sufficiency and happiness, and what torments him is removed. There is no power or strength except through Allah. The servant always needs to put his trust in Allah and seek His help, just as he needs to worship Him. He must therefore remain constantly aware of his dependence upon Allah and his need for Him as the One he worships and the One who helps him. There is no power or strength except through Allah, and there is no refuge from Allah except in Him.

Allah Most High says:

“It is only Satan who makes you fear his allies.”

That is, he frightens you by means of his allies. This is the correct interpretation held by the majority, including Ibn ‘Abbās and others, and by linguists such as al-Farrā’ and others.

Ibn al-Anbārī said: “Our preferred interpretation of the verse is: ‘He makes you fear his allies.’ The Arabs say, ‘I gave the wealth,’ meaning, ‘I gave the people the wealth’; thus they omit the first object.”

I say: This is because Satan makes people fear his allies without qualification; he has no need to frighten some people by means of others. The first object is therefore omitted because it is not the focus.

Some commentators said: “He frightens his allies, the hypocrites.” The first interpretation, however, is more evident, because the verse was revealed in connection with their being made to fear the disbelievers: it was revealed specifically concerning someone who made the believers fear other people. Allah says:

“He makes you fear his allies, so do not fear them.”

The pronoun refers to Satan’s allies, concerning whom the preceding verse says:

“So fear them.”

Those who offered the second interpretation explained the verse in terms of its meaning: Satan frightens his own allies because he has authority over them. He continually instils fears in them, even if they possess great numbers and ample equipment. The believers, by contrast, put their trust in Allah, and the disbelievers do not frighten them. Alternatively, they may have meant the first object; that is, he frightens

---

## Page 59

the hypocrites, his allies. He frightens the disbelievers just as he frightens the hypocrites. If the intended meaning were that he makes his allies afraid, the pronoun in His saying,

“So do not fear them,”

would have no referent. Moreover, Satan makes promises to his allies and fills them with hopes. As for the disbelievers, Allah casts terror of the believers into their hearts, and Satan does not choose this. Allah Most High says:

“You are indeed more feared in their hearts than Allah.”

And He says:

“I will cast terror into the hearts of those who disbelieve.”

However, those among the early generations who offered that interpretation meant that Satan frightens those who outwardly profess Islam while allying themselves with the enemy, thereby becoming hypocrites. It is the hypocrites who fear the disbelievers because Satan frightens them, as Allah Most High says:

“But they are a people who are afraid.”

And He says:

“But when fear comes…”

—to the end of the verse. Both interpretations are therefore correct in meaning. Yet the expression “his allies” refers to those whom Satan makes objects of fear, not those whom he makes afraid, as the context indicates. When he makes them objects of fear, only those whom Satan has frightened by means of them fear them. Thus, the verse indicates that Satan makes his allies objects of fear and makes other people afraid of them.

The verse also indicates that a believer must not fear Satan’s allies or fear people, as Allah says:

“So do not fear people, but fear Me.”

He commands fear of Allah and forbids fear of Satan’s allies. Allah Most High says:

“So that people may have no argument against you, except those among them who do wrong. So do not fear them, but fear Me.”

He thus forbids fearing the wrongdoer and commands fear of Himself. He also says:

“Those who convey Allah’s messages and fear Him, and fear none but Allah.”

And He says:

“And fear Me alone.”

Some people say: “My Lord, I fear You, and I fear those who do not fear You.” This is

---

## Page 60

worthless, impermissible speech. Rather, the servant must fear Allah alone and fear no one else. Anyone who does not fear Allah is too abject to be feared: he is a wrongdoer and one of Satan’s allies, and Allah has forbidden fearing him. If someone says, “He may harm me,” the reply is: “He can harm you only if Allah gives him power over you. If Allah wills to ward off his evil from you, He will do so. All authority belongs to Allah.”

Such a person is given power over a servant only because of that servant’s sins. If you fear Allah, remain mindful of Him, and place your trust in Him, He will protect you from every evil and will not give that person power over you. He has said:

“And whoever puts his trust in Allah, He is sufficient for him.”

That person is given power over you because of your sins and your fear of him. If you fear Allah, repent of your sins, and seek His forgiveness, he will not be given power over you, as Allah has said:

“Nor would Allah punish them while they seek forgiveness.”

It is related in transmitted reports that Allah says:

“I am Allah; there is no deity but Me, the King of kings. The hearts and forelocks of kings are in My hand. Whoever obeys Me, I make the hearts of kings merciful toward him; whoever disobeys Me, I make them a punishment for him. So do not occupy yourselves with reviling kings. Rather, repent to Me and obey Me, and I will make them kindly disposed toward you.”

When Allah gave the enemy power over the Companions on the day of Uhud, He said:

“When a calamity befell you…”

—to the end of the verse. He also said:

“How many a prophet fought with many companies alongside him…”

—and the verses that follow. Most recite qātala (fought).

According to the great majority of the early and later generations, the many ribbiyyūn are large companies. Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn ʿAbbās—in one narration from him—and al-Farrāʾ said: “Many thousands.” Ibn ʿAbbās, in another narration, Mujāhid, and Qatāda said: “Large companies.” The word has been recited with each of the three short vowels on its initial r. On this reading, the ribbiyyūn who fought alongside him are those who neither lost heart nor weakened.

As for the reading of Abū ʿAmr and others, it admits two interpretations:

The first agrees with the preceding interpretation: the ribbiyyūn are killed, yet they do not lose heart—that is, those of them who remained did not lose heart

---

## Page 61

because many of them had been killed. That is, they did not weaken on that account, lose their resolve, or submit to their enemy. Rather, they fulfilled Allah’s command to fight until Allah granted them victory over their enemies and His word became supreme.

The second interpretation is that the prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was killed along with many companies, yet those who remained did not lose heart because the prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had been killed. This accords with Satan’s cry that Muhammad had been killed. However, it does not accord with the wording of the verse. What accords with its wording is that, despite the magnitude of their calamity, they did not lose heart. Had the intended meaning been that the prophet was killed while those accompanying him were unafraid, there would have been no need to emphasize their great number. Rather, emphasizing how few they were would have been more fitting. If they were numerous, praising them for this would offer no lesson.

Nor would this constitute an argument against the Companions, for on the day of Uhud they were few, while their enemy outnumbered them several times over. They could say, “They did not lose heart because they numbered thousands, whereas we were few.”

Moreover, His words:

“How many a prophet…”

imply that this happened often. Yet it is not known that many prophets were killed in battle. This interpretation also implies that many companies were killed alongside each of those prophets, but this did not occur. The prophets before Moses did not engage in fighting, and Moses and the prophets of the Children of Israel were not killed on military campaigns. Indeed, no prophet is known to have been killed in battle. How, then, could this have happened often, with each prophet accompanied by a large army?

Allah, glorified is He, condemned those who turn back, whether the Prophet is killed or dies. He did not reproach them for being afraid if he died or was killed, but for turning back on their heels. This is why al-Siddīq, may Allah be pleased with him, recited the verse after the Prophet’s death—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and it was as though they had never heard it before.

Then, after that verse, He mentioned another point: those before you would fight, and some of them would be killed

---

## Page 62

in great numbers, yet those who remained would not lose heart. Mentioning their great number is therefore fitting, because many were killed alongside the prophets, and the killing of large numbers of one’s own people tends to make one lose heart. Yet they did not lose heart, despite the great number killed. Had they lost heart, that would have indicated weakness in their faith. He did not say here, “And they did not turn back on their heels.” Had the intended meaning been that their prophet was killed, He would have said, “Then they turned back on their heels,” for that is what He condemned when the Prophet dies or is killed. Thus, glorified is He, He condemned two things: apostasy if the Prophet dies or is killed; and losing heart, weakening, and submitting because of what befell them in Allah’s cause when the enemy gained the upper hand.

For this reason, He said:

“They did not lose heart because of what befell them…”

He did not say, “They did not lose heart because the Prophet was killed.” Had he been killed while they remained alive, He would have used wording appropriate to that circumstance, rather than saying:

“They did not lose heart because of what befell them in Allah’s cause.”

It is well known that what befalls people in Allah’s cause on most military campaigns is not the killing of a prophet.

Moreover, the fact that many companies fought or were killed with a prophet does not necessarily mean that the prophet was present with them on the campaign. Rather, anyone who follows a prophet and fights for his religion has fought with him. Likewise, anyone killed while following his religion has been killed with him. This is what the Companions understood. Their greatest campaigns took place after his death—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—until they conquered Syria, Egypt, Iraq, Yemen, Arab and non-Arab lands, Byzantine territories, and lands in both the west and the east. It was then that the great number of those killed with him became apparent, for many fought and suffered casualties while following the religion of the prophets.

Thus, this verse contains a lesson for all believers until the Day of Resurrection, for they all fight with the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for his religion, even though he has died. The Companions who went out on military expeditions without the Prophet accompanying them were nevertheless fighting with him. They are included in His words:

“Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, and those with him,”

and the rest of the verse, and in His words:

“And those who believed afterwards and emigrated

---

## Page 63

and strove alongside you,” and the rest of the verse. Being with someone whom one obeys does not require being physically present with him or seeing him.

It has also been said that “ribbiyyūn” (companies) here means scholars. Since these interpreters treated this as equivalent to the term rabbānī (religious teacher)—while Ibn Zayd said that they were followers, as though he regarded them as those being nurtured—the first interpretation is sounder for several reasons:

First: The religious teachers are the learned scholars themselves: those who nurture people and lead them in their religion. Such people can only be few in number.

Second: The command to fight and remain patient is not confined to them. The companions of the prophets were not all religious teachers, even though they had been granted knowledge and possessed fear of Allah, Mighty and Majestic.

Third: Using the word rabbānī in this sense is not established in the language.

Fourth: Nor is using the word ribbī in this sense established in the language. Rather, the established meaning is the first one. Those who proposed this interpretation said that the word denotes an association with al-Rabb (the Lord), without the letter nūn. Yet the well-known reading is (ribbī, with an i-vowel on the rā), whereas their explanation would apply only to the reading rabbī, with an a-vowel on the rā. It has also been read rubbī, with a u-vowel, showing that these are variant linguistic forms.

Fifth: Allah Most High commands everyone whom He commands to fight to remain patient and steadfast, whether or not they are religious teachers.

Sixth: There is no fitting reason to single these people out for mention here. Mentioning them is appropriate in passages such as His words:

“Why do the religious teachers and scholars not forbid them…”

and the rest of the verse; and His words:

“But be religious teachers.”

Mentioning them in those passages is appropriate.

Seventh: It has been said that rabbānī is derived from al-Rabb (the Lord), with the addition of alif and nūn, as in the formation lihyānī. It has also been said that it derives from nurturing people, or from rubbān (a ship’s captain). This last explanation is the soundest, for

---

## Page 64

the basic rule is that no extra letters are added when forming an adjective of affiliation. Their designation relates to nurturing, which is particular to them. As for their affiliation with the Lord, that is not exclusive to them: every servant of His is affiliated with Him, either generally or specifically. Allah has not called His God-fearing friends religious teachers, nor has He given this designation to His messengers and prophets. A rabbānī (religious teacher) guides and nurtures people just as a shipmaster directs a ship. Hence, religious teachers are sometimes censured and sometimes praised. Had their designation signified affiliation with the Lord, they would never have been censured.

This is the eighth reason: if the designation is taken as praise, they have nevertheless been censured in various passages; if it is not praise, then they have no distinguishing quality that sets them apart in this respect. If the designation derives from that of a shipmaster, the claim that rabbānī derives from al-Rabb (the Lord) is invalid. Deriving ribbiyyūn from al-Rabb is therefore even less tenable.

Ninth: Even supposing that their designation signifies affiliation with the Lord, this does not imply that they are scholars. It does, admittedly, imply faith, worship, and devotion to Allah, but these encompass all believers. Everyone who worships Allah alone, associating nothing with Him, is devoted to Allah and has knowledge of Him. All the Companions were such, yet they were not called rabbāniyyūn or ribbiyyūn. What has been reported is that, when Ibn al-Hanafiyya heard of Ibn 'Abbās’s death, he said: “Today the religious teacher of this community has died.” This was because he educated them through the knowledge Allah had granted him. The caliphs were superior to them, yet they were not called religious teachers, even if they themselves were religious teachers.

Ibrāhīm said: “'Alqama was one of the religious teachers.” Hence Mujāhid said: “They are those who nurture people by teaching them the elementary aspects of knowledge before its advanced aspects.” They are thus those who enjoin and forbid. Conveying information, however, includes anyone who imparts knowledge, transmits it from others, and relates it, even if he neither enjoins nor forbids. This is the meaning transmitted from the early generations concerning the religious teacher. It is reported that 'Alī said: “They are those who nourish people with wisdom

---

## Page 65

and nurture them in it.” Ibn 'Abbās said: “They are jurists who teach.”

I say: Those who enjoin and forbid are the jurists who teach. Qatāda and 'Atā’ said: “They are jurists, scholars, and wise men.” Ibn Qutayba said: “The singular is rabbānī (religious teacher), and they are scholars who teach.”

Abū 'Ubayd said: “I think the word is Hebrew or Syriac.” This is because Abū 'Ubayd maintained that the Arabs were unfamiliar with religious teachers.

I say: The word is Arabic, derived from rabbān (shipmaster), who stays aboard the ship and attends to its welfare. However, the Arabs in their pre-Islamic days had no religious teachers, because they did not follow a revealed law from Allah, Mighty and Majestic.

---

## Page 66

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Allah Most High said:

“Guide us to the straight path.”

“The path of those upon whom You have bestowed favor, not of those who have incurred wrath, nor of those who have gone astray.”

It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The Jews are those who have incurred wrath, and the Christians are those who have gone astray.”

The Book of Allah indicates this in several places, such as His words:

“Say: Shall I inform you of those whose recompense with Allah is worse than that? Those whom Allah has cursed and with whom He is angry.”

And His words:

“So they incurred wrath upon wrath.”

And His words:

“They incurred wrath from Allah and were stricken with destitution.”

Concerning the Christians, He said:

“Say: O People of the Book, do not go beyond the bounds of truth in your religion, and do not follow the desires of a people who went astray before, led many astray, and strayed from the right way.”

He also said:

“O People of the Book, do not go to excess in your religion, nor say anything about Allah except the truth. The Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, was only a messenger of Allah, His word which He conveyed to Mary, and a spirit from Him.”

Allah Most High said:

“The Jews say, ‘Ezra is the son of Allah,’ and the Christians say, ‘The Messiah is the son of Allah.’ That is what they say with their mouths, imitating the words of those who disbelieved before them. May Allah destroy them! How are they turned away from the truth!”

“They have taken their rabbis and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, son of Mary, although they were commanded only to worship one God. There is no god but Him. Glory be to Him, far above what they associate with Him!”

Allah Most High said:

“It is not for a human being

---

## Page 67

to whom Allah has given the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood, to then say to the people, ‘Be my servants rather than Allah’s.’ Rather, ‘Be devoted to the Lord through your teaching of the Scripture and your study of it.’”

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have become Muslims?”

Allah Most High said:

“Say: Call upon those you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to remove harm from you or to shift it elsewhere.”

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek the means of drawing near to their Lord, striving to be nearest to Him. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

Allah, glory be to Him, commanded us to ask Him in every prayer to guide us to the straight path: the path of those whom Allah has favored—the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous—who are distinct from those who have incurred wrath and those who have gone astray. This shows that there is reason to fear that a servant may stray onto either of these two paths. This has indeed happened, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, foretold when he said:

“You will surely follow the ways of those before you, as precisely as one arrow feather matches another. Even if they entered a lizard’s burrow, you would enter it.”

They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, do you mean the Jews and the Christians?”

He replied, “Who else?”

This is an authentic hadith.

The early generations held that a scholar who deviates from the straight path bears a resemblance to the Jews, while a worshipper who deviates bears a resemblance to the Christians. This can be seen among those who deviate from among the people of knowledge: they distort words from their proper places, their hearts become hard, they withhold knowledge, display arrogance, and command others to do good while forgetting themselves, among other things.

Likewise, among those who deviate from among people devoted to worship and spiritual states, one sees excess concerning righteous prophets and innovations in worship, monasticism, images, and sounds. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not praise me excessively as the Christians praised

---

## Page 68

Jesus, son of Mary. I am only a servant, so say, ‘Allah’s servant and His Messenger.’” For this reason, Allah affirmed his status as a servant in his loftiest stations, saying:

“Glory be to Him who took His servant by night.”

Allah Most High also said:

“Then He revealed to His servant what He revealed.”

And He said:

“When Allah’s servant stood up to call upon Him, they almost crowded upon him.”

For this reason, it is prescribed that, in the tashahhud (the testimony recited while seated in prayer) and in all prescribed sermons and addresses—such as the Friday and Eid sermons, and addresses for occasions such as marriage and other matters—we say:

“I bear witness that none is worthy of worship but Allah, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger.”

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, emphasized his servanthood so that the Muslim community would not fall into what the Christians had fallen into concerning the Messiah: claiming divinity for him. Thus, when a man said to him, “As Allah wills and you will,” he replied:

“Have you made me a rival to Allah? Rather, say: ‘As Allah alone wills.’”

He also said to his Companions:

“Do not say, ‘As Allah wills and Muhammad wills.’ Rather, say, ‘As Allah wills, then Muhammad wills.’”

And he said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings. Send blessings upon me wherever you are, for your blessings reach me.”

And he said:

“O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped. Allah’s wrath was severe against people who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

And he said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

Excess has arisen within the Muslim community among two groups: a group of misguided Shiites who believe in the divinity of the prophets and the imams of the Prophet’s household, and a group of ignorant Sufis who hold similar beliefs concerning the prophets and the righteous. Anyone who imagines that our Prophet, or any other prophet, possesses any divinity or lordship is of the same kind as the Christians. The rights due to the prophets are only those set forth concerning them in the Quran and Sunnah.

Allah Most High said, addressing the Children of Israel:

“And you believe in My messengers, support them, and lend Allah a good loan, I will surely remove your misdeeds from you and admit you into

---

## Page 69

gardens beneath which rivers flow.” Supporting the messengers means aiding, honoring, and strengthening them.

Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, We have sent you as a witness, a bearer of good tidings, and a warner.”

“So that you may believe in Allah and His Messenger, support him, and honor him.”

This concerns the Messenger. Then He said, concerning Allah Most High:

“And glorify Him morning and evening.”

Allah Most High said:

“My mercy encompasses all things. I shall ordain it for those who fear Me, give zakat, and believe in Our signs.”

“Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whom they find described in the Torah and the Gospel that they possess. He commands them to do what is right and forbids them from doing what is wrong, permits good things for them and forbids impure things, and relieves them of their burden and the shackles that were upon them. So those who believe in him, honor him, support him, and follow the light sent down with him—it is they who will prosper.”

Allah Most High said:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive your sins. Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.’”

“Say, ‘Obey Allah and the Messenger.’ But if they turn away, then Allah does not love the disbelievers.”

Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, Allah and His angels send blessings upon the Prophet. O you who believe, send blessings upon him and greet him with peace.”

Allah Most High said:

“Say, ‘If your fathers, your sons, your brothers, your spouses, your relatives, the wealth you have acquired, the trade whose decline you fear, and the homes you delight in are dearer to you than Allah, His Messenger, and striving in His cause, then wait.’”

He mentioned obedience to the Messenger in more than thirty places in the Quran, and said:

“O you who believe, respond to Allah and the Messenger when he calls you to that which gives you life.”

Allah Most High said:

“But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge in their disputes, then find no discomfort within themselves concerning your judgment and submit wholeheartedly.”

Allah Most High said:

“So let those who oppose his command beware lest a trial befall them or a painful punishment afflict them.”

Allah Most High said:

“The only response of the believers, when called to Allah and His Messenger so that he may judge between them, is to say, ‘We hear and obey.’ And those

---

## Page 70

are the ones who prosper.”

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, fears Allah, and is mindful of Him—it is they who will triumph.”

He thus made obedience due to Allah and the Messenger, while reserving fear and God-consciousness for Allah alone, as He said:

“So fear Me alone.”

And He said:

“And be mindful of Me alone.”

And He said:

“So do not fear people, but fear Me.”

And He said:

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah. The Hand of Allah is above their hands.”

Allah Most High said:

“Do not regard the calling of the Messenger among you as being like your calling one another.”

Allah Most High said:

“The Prophet has a greater claim upon the believers than they have upon themselves, and his wives are their mothers.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“None of you believes until I am dearer to him than his child, his father, and all people.”

Umar said to him, “By Allah, O Messenger of Allah, you are dearer to me than everyone except myself.” He replied, “No, Umar—not until I am dearer to you than your own self.” Umar said, “Then you are dearer to me than my own self.” He replied, “Now, Umar.”

Allah has thus explained in His Book the rights of the Messenger: obeying him, loving him, supporting and honoring him, aiding him, making him judge, being content with his judgment and submitting to it, following him, invoking blessings and peace upon him, giving him precedence over oneself, one’s family, and one’s wealth, referring disputed matters to him, and other rights.

He declared that obedience to the Messenger is obedience to Him, saying:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

And that pledging allegiance to the Messenger is pledging allegiance to Him, saying:

“Indeed, those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah.”

He coupled His name with the Messenger’s name in connection with love, saying:

“Dearer to you than Allah and His Messenger.”

And in connection with giving offense, saying:

“Indeed, those who offend Allah and His Messenger.”

And in connection with obedience and disobedience, saying:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger.”

“Whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger.”

And in connection with seeking approval, saying:

“Allah and His Messenger have a greater right that they should seek His approval.”

These and similar rights are what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is entitled to—may my father and mother be sacrificed for him.

As for worship and seeking help, these are for Allah alone, who has no partner, as He said:

“Worship Allah and associate nothing with Him.”

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

“They were commanded only to worship Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him, upright in faith.”

He has joined the two together in several passages, as in His saying:

“So worship Him and put your trust in Him.”

And His saying:

“Put your trust in the Ever-Living, who never dies, and glorify Him with praise.”

And His saying:

And He said:

And He said:

“Those to whom people said, ‘The people have gathered against you, so fear them,’ but this only increased them in faith, and they said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Trustee.’”

Invocation is for Allah alone, whether it is invocation through worship or invocation through petition and seeking help, as Allah Most High said:

“The mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone along with Allah.”

“When the servant of Allah stood up to invoke Him, they almost crowded around him.”

“Say, ‘I invoke only my Lord, and I associate no one with Him.’”

Allah Most High said:

“So invoke Allah, devoting your religion sincerely to Him, even if the disbelievers dislike it.”

And He said:

“So do not invoke another god along with Allah, lest you be among those who are punished.”

And He said:

“Do not drive away those who invoke their Lord morning and evening, seeking His Face.”

He condemned those who invoke the angels, the prophets, and others, saying:

“Say, ‘Invoke those whom you claim besides Him. They have no power to remove harm from you or to shift it elsewhere.’ Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of approach to their Lord, striving to be closest to Him. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

It is reported from Ibn Masʿūd that some people used to invoke the angels

---

## Page 72

and the Messiah and Ezra. Allah said:

“Those whom you invoke fear Allah, hope in Him, and seek nearness to Him, just as you yourselves fear Him, hope in Him, and seek nearness to Him.”

Allah Most High said:

“When harm befalls you at sea, all those you invoke vanish from your thoughts except Him.”

And He said:

“Who responds to the distressed when they call upon Him, removes their suffering, and makes you successors on earth? Is there another god besides Allah?”

And He said:

“Those who do not invoke another god besides Allah, do not kill the soul Allah has forbidden except by right, and do not commit adultery.”

The Quran speaks extensively of affirming Allah’s oneness and devoting religion sincerely to Him by worshipping Him and seeking His help. Indeed, this is the heart of faith and the beginning and end of Islam. As the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“I have been commanded to fight people until they testify that none is worthy of worship but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.”

And he said:

“I know a declaration which no one utters at the time of death without his soul finding comfort in it.”

And he said:

“Whoever’s last words are ‘None is worthy of worship but Allah’ is assured of Paradise.”

This is the heart of religion and faith; all other deeds are like its limbs. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Deeds are judged only by intentions, and each person will have only what they intended. Whoever migrates to Allah and His Messenger, their migration is to Allah and His Messenger. Whoever migrates for some worldly gain or to marry a woman, their migration is to whatever they migrated for.”

He thereby made clear that intention is an act of the heart and the foundation of deeds. Devoting religion sincerely to Allah, worshipping Allah alone, and following the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, in what he brought constitute the testimony that none is worthy of worship but Allah and that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger.

For this reason, we objected to Shaykh Yahyā al-Sarsarī’s appeals to the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, for relief in his poems praising him, such as his words:

“To you I appeal for relief; your help I seek, and your rescue I implore.”

And other expressions of this kind.

---

## Page 73

Likewise, I have denounced, in public and private gatherings, the practice of many people who appeal for rescue and assistance to the righteous and those who imitate them, whether living or dead. I have explained Allah’s oneness to people, and through this Allah has benefited as many of the elite and the general public as He willed. This is the universal religion of Islam with which Allah sent all His messengers.

As Allah Most High said:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every community, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’”

And He said:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him, ‘There is no god but Me, so worship Me.’”

And He said:

“Ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: did We appoint gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

And He said:

“O messengers, eat of the good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know all that you do.”

“And indeed, this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

And He said:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, that which We have revealed to you, and that which We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: uphold the religion and do not become divided in it. What you call the polytheists to is hard for them to accept.”

And He said:

“I did not create jinn and humankind except to worship Me.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to Muādh ibn Jabal:

“O Muādh, do you know Allah’s right over His servants?”

I replied, “Allah and His Messenger know best.”

He said, “His right over them is that they worship Him and associate nothing with Him. Do you know the servants’ right upon Allah if they do that? That He should not punish them.”

And he said to Ibn Abbās:

“When you ask, ask Allah; and when you seek help, seek help from Allah.”

Worship includes reverent fear, turning to Allah, submission, and repentance, as Allah Most High said:

“Those who convey Allah’s messages and fear Him, and fear no one but Allah.”

And He said:

“So do not fear people, but fear Me.”

And He said:

“Only those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakat, and fear none but Allah maintain Allah’s mosques.”

Abraham, Allah’s close friend, said:

“I do not fear what you associate with Him, unless my Lord wills something. My Lord encompasses all things in knowledge. Will you not then

“Those who believe and do not mix their faith with wrongdoing—it is they who will have security, and they are rightly guided.”

And He said:

“Will you not fight a people who broke their oaths?”

Up to His words:

Thus they called for reverent fear of Allah, and obedience to themselves.

“Fear Allah.”

And He said:

## Page 75

“And seek your Lord’s forgiveness, then turn to Him in repentance.”

Seeking provision and seeking victory, as in the prayer for rain and supplications against enemies during prayer. He said:

“So seek provision from Allah, worship Him, and give thanks to Him.”

And He said:

“If Allah helps you, none can overcome you; but if He forsakes you, who can help you after Him? So let the believers put their trust in Allah.”

Seeking help in distress, as He said:

“When you sought your Lord’s help, He answered you.”

Seeking protection, as He said:

“Say, ‘In whose hand is the dominion over all things, who grants protection while none can grant protection against Him, if you know?’ They will say, ‘Allah’s.’ Say, ‘How, then, are you deluded?’”

Seeking refuge, as He said:

“Say, ‘I seek refuge in the Lord of daybreak.’”

And:

“Say, ‘I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind.’”

And He said:

And He said:

“So when you recite the Quran”—and the rest of the verse.

Entrusting one’s affairs to Allah, as the believer from Pharaoh’s household said:

“And I entrust my affairs to Allah. Indeed, Allah sees His servants.”

In the hadith recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught this supplication to be said at bedtime:

“O Allah, I have surrendered myself to You, turned my face toward You, entrusted my affairs to You, and sought Your protection.”

And He said:

“And warn with it those who fear being gathered before their Lord: they have neither protector nor intercessor besides Him.”

And He said:

“Allah is the One who created the heavens and the earth and all that is between them in six days, then rose over the Throne. You have neither protector nor intercessor besides Him.”

The protector is the one who takes charge of all your affairs, while the intercessor assists in them—that is, serves as a helper. Thus, besides Allah, the servant has neither a protector who acts independently nor a supporting helper.

And He said:

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it except Him; and if He intends good for you, none can repel His bounty.”

And He said:

“Whatever mercy Allah opens up for people, none can withhold; and whatever He withholds, none can release after Him.”

And He said:
