“Or have they taken, besides

---

## Page 76

Allah, intercessors? Say, ‘Even though they possess nothing and have no understanding?’”

“Say, ‘All intercession belongs to Allah. To Him belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth.’”

And He said:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim besides Allah. They do not possess an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth, nor do they have any share in either, nor does He have any helper among them.’”

“Intercession does not benefit anyone with Him except those for whom He gives permission.”

And He said:

“Who can intercede with Him except by His permission?”

And He said:

“How many angels there are in the heavens whose intercession is of no avail, except after Allah gives permission to whom He wills and is pleased with.”

Thus, worship and seeking help, together with everything they encompass—supplication, seeking help in distress, reverent fear, hope, turning to Allah, trust, repentance, and seeking forgiveness—are all for Allah alone, without partner. Worship pertains to His divinity, while seeking help pertains to His lordship. Allah is the Lord of all worlds; there is no god but Him, and we have no lord other than Him—neither an angel, nor a prophet, nor anyone else. Indeed, the greatest of major sins is associating partners with Allah: setting up a rival to Him when it is He who created you. Associating partners with Him means giving someone other than Him a share—that is, a portion—in your worship, trust, and seeking of help, as those who said:

“We worship them only so that they may bring us nearer to Allah.”

And as He, Most High, said:

“We do not see with you your intercessors whom you claimed to be partners concerning you.”

And as He said:

“Or have they taken intercessors besides Allah? Say, ‘Even though they possess nothing and have no understanding?’”

And as He said:

“You have neither protector nor intercessor besides Him.”

The forms of worship include prayer with all its constituent parts taken together, as well as those parts that are acts of worship in their own right: prostration, bowing, glorification, supplication, recitation, and standing. These are appropriate only for Allah alone. Nor may any voluntary act be performed as worship for anyone but Allah alone—not for the sun or the moon

---

## Page 77

nor for an angel, a prophet, or a righteous person, nor for the grave of a prophet or a righteous person. This holds true in all the religions of the prophets. It is also stated in our revealed law, which goes so far as to prohibit even voluntary acts of this kind performed to greet or honour created beings. Thus, the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, forbade Mu‘ādh to prostrate before him. He said:

“Were I to command anyone to prostrate before another, I would command a wife to prostrate before her husband, because of the greatness of his right over her.”

He also forbade bowing in greeting and forbade them to stand behind him in prayer while he was seated.

Likewise, zakāh—both in its general sense, encompassing all charitable giving, and in its specific sense—must be given only for Allah. As He, Most High, said:

“He owes no one a favour that must be repaid,”

“But seeks only the Face of his Lord, the Most High.”

And He said:

“We feed you only for the Face of Allah.”

And He said:

“And the likeness of those who spend their wealth seeking Allah’s pleasure and out of firm conviction within themselves.”

And He said:

“But whatever you give in charity, seeking Allah’s Face—those who do so are the ones whose reward is multiplied.”

It is therefore impermissible to do this as a religious act for an angel, the sun, the moon, a prophet, or a righteous person, as some beggars and those who venerate such figures do in honour of so-and-so. They swear by various figures—prophets, Companions, or righteous people—naming, for example, Bakr, ‘Alī, Nūr al-Dīn Arslān, Shaykh ‘Adī, and Shaykh Jālīd.

Likewise, pilgrimage is made only to Allah’s House: only it is circumambulated, only there is the head shaved, and only in its environs is the standing rite performed. These acts are not performed for a prophet or a righteous person, at the grave of a prophet or a righteous person, or at an idol.

Likewise, fasting as an act of worship is only for Allah. One must not fast for the stars, the sun, the moon, the graves of prophets and righteous people, or anything of that kind.

---

## Page 78

All this elaborates upon the two testimonies that constitute the foundation of religion: the testimony that none is worthy of worship except Allah, and the testimony that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. A deity is one who deserves to be worshipped by His servants, which includes loving and fearing Him. Whatever pertains to divinity is therefore Allah’s exclusive right, while whatever pertains to messengership is the Messenger’s right.

Since the foundation of religion is the two testimonies, this community serves as the witnesses and is characterized by bearing witness. Priests practise worship without bearing witness; hence they said:

“Our Lord, we believe in what You have revealed and follow the Messenger, so count us among the witnesses.”

For this reason, discerning scholars hold that the two testimonies are the first religious obligations. This is the position of the true adherents of Ahl al-Sunnah, and was stated by Mansūr al-Sam‘ānī, Shaykh ‘Abd al-Qādir, and others.

And he made it the root of polytheism. They thereby altered the religion of monotheism, which is the foundation of religion, just as the philosophers of old did when they instituted religious practices that Allah had not authorized.

Among the causes of this is departing from the particular revealed law with which Allah sent Muhammad, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, in favour of common features involving resemblance to the Sabians, Christians, or Jews. This is a flawed analogy resembling that of those who said:

“Trade is just like usury.”

They wish to treat listening as a single category, and religion as a single category, without distinguishing what is prescribed from what is innovated, or what is commanded from what is forbidden. Religious listening prescribed by the revealed law consists in listening to Allah’s Book, beautifying one’s voice with it, and embellishing its recitation. As the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said:

“Beautify the Quran with your voices.”

Abū Mūsā said, “Had I known that you were listening, I would have made my recitation especially beautiful for you.”

And the forms, wives, and slave concubines that Allah Most High has permitted.

---

## Page 79

and worship is the worship of Allah alone, with no partner:

“In houses which Allah has permitted to be raised and in which His name is remembered, men glorify Him morning and evening.”

This establishes the principle that following the straight path requires differing from the people of Hell. It forbids equating what is religiously prescribed with what is natural and innovated merely because they share a common element. A beautiful voice, for example, is not in itself religiously prescribed unless a distinguishing element is added to it, such as the words of the Quran. The shared and distinguishing elements together then constitute beneficial religious practice.

---

## Page 80

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

Section: On the Servant Asking None but Allah

Allah Most High said:

“So when you have finished your duties, then stand up for worship.”

“And to your Lord direct your longing.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to Ibn ‘Abbās:

“When you ask, ask Allah; and when you seek help, seek help from Allah.”

It is related in al-Tirmidhī:

“Let each of you ask his Lord for all his needs, even the strap of his sandal when it breaks; for unless He makes it easy, it will not be easy.”

An authentic report relates that he said to ‘Adī ibn Mālik and the group who pledged allegiance along with him:

“Do not ask people for anything.”

Thus, if one of them dropped his whip, he would not ask anyone to hand it to him. In the authentic report concerning the seventy thousand who will enter Paradise without being brought to account, it is stated:

“They are those who do not request healing recitation, do not undergo cauterization, and do not take evil omens.”

Istirqā’ means requesting ruqyah (healing recitation), and it is a form of asking.

There are many hadiths prohibiting asking people for money, such as his saying:

“Begging is not lawful except for three kinds of people.”

And his saying, “For one of you to take his rope…”—to the end of the hadith; his saying, “A person continues to beg…”; his saying, “Whoever asks people when he has enough to suffice him…”; and other similar reports.

Also, his saying:

“Whoever is afflicted by poverty and turns to people to relieve it will not have his poverty relieved”—to the end of the hadith.

As for asking permissible questions concerning knowledge, this does not fall under this category, because the one who provides the information

---

## Page 81

loses none of his knowledge by answering; rather, his knowledge increases through answering, while the questioner needs the answer. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Why did they not ask when they did not know? The cure for ignorance is to ask.”

Some questions, however, are forbidden, as Allah Most High said:

“Do not ask about things…”—to the end of the verse.

Likewise, he forbade trick questions and the like.

As for asking someone else to supplicate for him, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to ‘Umar:

“Do not forget us in your supplications.”

He also said:

“When you hear the caller to prayer, repeat what he says, then invoke blessings upon me, for whoever invokes blessings upon me once, Allah will bless him ten times. Then ask Allah to grant me al-wasīlah, for it is a rank in Paradise befitting only one of Allah’s servants, and I hope to be that servant. Whoever asks Allah to grant me al-wasīlah will be entitled to my intercession on the Day of Resurrection.”

It may be said in this regard that he asked the Muslim community to supplicate for him because, by supplicating for him, they would receive a greater reward than if they supplicated for themselves. Thus, when someone asked him, “Shall I devote all my supplication to invoking blessings upon you?” he replied:

“Then Allah will take care of whatever concerns you in the affairs of this world and the Hereafter.”

His asking them to supplicate for him is therefore for their benefit, just like the other commands he gives them in accordance with what he has been commanded, because these are for their benefit. Indeed, it is authentically reported that he said:

“Whenever a man supplicates for his brother in his absence, Allah appoints an angel for him. Every time he offers a supplication, the appointed angel says, ‘Amen, and may you receive the same.’”

---

## Page 82

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Acts of worship are founded on revealed law and adherence to it, not on whims and religious innovation. Islam rests on two foundations:

First: that we worship Allah alone, without any partner.

Second: that we worship Him as He has prescribed through His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, not according to whims and innovations. Allah Most High said:

“Then We set you on a clear path in matters of religion, so follow it, and do not follow the desires of those who do not know.”

“They can be of no help to you against Allah”—to the end of the verse.

He also said:

“Or do they have partners who have prescribed for them a religion that Allah has not authorized?”

No one may therefore worship Allah except through the obligatory and recommended acts prescribed by His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him. One must not worship Him through innovated practices, as is established in the Sunan collections in the hadith of al-‘Irbād ibn Sāriyah. Al-Tirmidhī said: “A sound and authentic hadith.” It is also reported in Muslim that the Prophet used to say in his sermons:

“The best speech is the speech of Allah, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him. The worst matters are newly introduced practices, and every innovation is misguidance.”

No one may worship anyone but Allah alone: one must pray only to Allah and fast only for Allah

---

## Page 83

and make pilgrimage only to Allah’s House, rely only on Allah, fear only Allah, make vows only to Allah, and swear only by Allah.

It is reported in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Allah forbids you to swear by your fathers. Whoever wishes to swear should swear by Allah or remain silent.”

The Sunan collections report:

“Whoever swears by anyone other than Allah has associated partners with Him.”

Ibn Masʿūd said:

“I would rather swear by Allah while lying than swear by anyone else while speaking the truth.”

This is because swearing by anyone other than Allah is polytheism, whereas swearing by Allah affirms His oneness. Affirming His oneness while lying is better than associating partners with Him while speaking truthfully. Thus, lying at its worst is equated with associating partners with Allah, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“False testimony is equivalent to associating partners with Allah”—repeating this twice or three times.

He then recited the words of Allah Most High:

“Whoever associates partners with Allah is like one who has fallen from the sky and is snatched away by birds or swept by the wind to a distant place.”

If someone who swears by anyone other than Allah has associated partners with Him, what, then, of someone who makes a vow to anyone other than Allah? A vow is more serious than an oath. Thus, if someone makes a vow to anyone other than Allah, there is no obligation to fulfil it, by the agreement of the Muslims—for example, a vow to offer prayer, fasting, Hajj, ʿumrah, or charity to anyone other than Allah.

If someone swears to do something that he is not obligated to do, it has been said that he may make expiation for his oath without doing what he swore to do. As the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better should do what is better and make expiation for his oath.”

It is also established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade making vows and said:

“They bring no good; they only serve to extract something from the miserly.”

If vows bring no good, what, then, of vows made to a created being? However, a vow made to Allah must be fulfilled if it involves obedience to Him. If it involves disobedience, it must not be fulfilled, by the agreement of the scholars. They differ only over

---

## Page 84

whether a substitute or expiation for an oath is required or not. For al-Bukhārī reports in his Sahīh that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah should obey Him, and whoever vows to disobey Allah must not disobey Him.”

Whoever thinks that making vows to created beings will bring him benefit or ward off harm is among the misguided, like those who think that worshipping created beings will bring them benefit or ward off harm. Devils may appear to these polytheists, speak to them, carry one of them through the air, inform him of certain matters hidden from him, or bring him money, food, clothing, or other things. Similar things have happened to idol worshippers among Arabs and non-Arabs. Such occurrences are common, both in this age and in others, among misguided innovators who oppose the Quran and Sunnah, either by worshipping someone other than Allah or by practising worship that Allah has not prescribed.

When one of these people displays something extraordinary, it can only be a satanic phenomenon or a fraudulent one. Devils attach themselves to their elite, as happens to some of the intelligent among them. This may also happen to others, but devils attach themselves to people only when some form of religious innovation is present, whether disbelief, wickedness, or ignorance of the revealed law. Satan’s aim is to lead people astray as far as he can. If he can make them disbelievers, he does so; if he can only make them wicked or disobedient, he does so. And if all he can do is diminish their deeds and their religion through an innovation they practise, thereby opposing the revealed law with which Allah sent His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, he gains something from them in this way.

---

## Page 85

For this reason, the imams said:

“Even if you see a man flying through the air or walking on water, do not be taken in by him until you examine his observance of the commands and prohibitions.”

Thus, many people are found flying through the air, but it is devils that carry them; these are not miraculous gifts bestowed upon Allah’s pious allies.

Among these people are those whom a devil carries to Arafat, where they stand with the pilgrims, before carrying them back to their own city that same night. This ignorant man imagines that this is one of Allah’s allies, not realizing that he must repent of this. If he believes that this is an act of obedience and a means of drawing nearer to Allah, he is to be called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death. For the Hajj commanded by Allah and His Messenger necessarily includes entering the state of consecration, standing at Arafat, and subsequently performing tawāf al-ifādah (the obligatory circumambulation), which is a pillar without which Hajj is incomplete. He must also stop at Muzdalifah, throw pebbles at the prescribed pillars, and perform the farewell circumambulation. He must avoid the prohibited acts and enter the state of consecration at the designated boundary, along with fulfilling the other obligations of Hajj.

These misguided people, whom Satan leads astray, are carried by him through the air. He carries one of them in his ordinary clothes, so that he stands at Arafat and returns that same night. Thus, he is seen in his own town and at Arafat on the same day. In other cases, a devil takes on a person’s appearance and stands at Arafat. Someone who knows that person sees the figure standing there and assumes that the man himself has stood at Arafat.

When that shaykh tells him, “I did not go to Arafat this year,” he assumes that it was an angel created in the shaykh’s likeness. In fact, it was merely a devil that had assumed his appearance. Such things, and others like them, occur frequently; they are satanic phenomena. Allah Most High says:

“Whoever turns away from the remembrance of the Most Merciful, We appoint for him a devil who becomes his companion.”

The remembrance of the Most Merciful is the Reminder that He revealed to His Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, it is We who have sent down

---

## Page 86

the Reminder, and indeed We will preserve it.” Allah Most High also says:

“If guidance comes to you from Me…”

—through to His words—

“Thus did Our signs come to you, but you forgot them; and likewise today you will be forgotten.”

Forgetting them means abandoning faith in them and failing to act upon them, even if one has memorized their wording. Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“Allah has guaranteed that whoever recites the Quran and acts upon what it contains will neither go astray in this world nor suffer misery in the Hereafter.”

He then recited this verse. Whoever follows the Scripture and wisdom with which Allah sent His Messenger Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, will be guided by Allah and granted happiness. Whoever turns away from them will go astray and suffer misery, and Satan will lead him astray and bring him misery.

The phenomena bestowed by the Most Merciful and the miraculous gifts granted to His pious allies arise from faith, for this is the condition of His allies. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, the allies of Allah will have no fear, nor will they grieve—those who believe and are mindful of Him.”

These are blessings from Allah upon His believing servant in his religious and worldly affairs: they provide evidence in matters of religion and meet the believers’ worldly needs. Likewise, the miracles of our Prophet Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, provided evidence in matters of religion and met the Muslims’ needs. Examples include the blessing bestowed upon food and drink, such as water springing from between his fingers; rain falling in response to prayers for rain; the defeat of disbelievers and the healing of the sick through supplication; and truthful, beneficial accounts of matters hidden from those present. The reports given by the prophets are never false.

As for those who experience satanic phenomena, they are like soothsayers: sometimes they lie and sometimes they speak the truth, and their deeds necessarily involve disobedience to Allah’s commands. Allah Most High says in these two verses:

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend? They descend upon every sinful liar.”

For this reason, one finds such a person in contact with foul things—ritual impurities and filth

---

## Page 87

that the devils love—and committing acts of indecency or wronging people in their persons and property, and so on.

Allah Most High has forbidden:

“Acts of indecency, whether open or secret, sin, unjust aggression, and associating partners with Allah…”

—to the end of the verse.

The allies of Allah are those who seek His pleasure by doing what He has commanded, avoiding what He has forbidden, and patiently bearing what He has decreed. This is a summary whose detailed exposition would be lengthy and exceed the space available here. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 88

Shaykh al-Islām said:

A Comprehensive Section

I have written previously in several places—before some of the principles and at the end of the draft on jurisprudence—that justice encompasses all good deeds, and wrongdoing encompasses all evil deeds. This is a great, comprehensive principle.

To explain this in detail: Allah brought creation into being to worship Him. This is the intended purpose of all good deeds: to devote all religion sincerely to Allah. Any deed that fails to fulfil this purpose is not an unqualified good deed deserving Allah’s reward in the Hereafter, even though it may be good in some respects and earn a reward in this world. Everything He has forbidden is a departure and deviation from uprightness, placing something where it does not belong; it is therefore wrongdoing.

For this reason, He—glorified be He—joined the two together in His words:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Direct your faces toward Him at every place of worship, and call upon Him, devoting your religion sincerely to Him.’”

This verse is in Surah al-A‘rāf, which addresses the foundations of religion and holding fast to the Book. It condemns those who instituted religious practices that Allah did not authorize, such as associating partners with Him and forbidding wholesome things, or who opposed what Allah had prescribed in matters of their religion. These include Iblīs, those who opposed the messengers—from the people of Noah to the people of Pharaoh—and those among the People of the Book who altered the Book.

Thus, the surah condemns those who introduced a false religion, such as the disbelieving Arabs, and those who opposed the true religion, whether entirely, like those who disbelieved in the prophets, or partially, like the disbelievers among the People of the Book. In this surah, in al-An‘ām, and elsewhere, He—glorified be He—has grouped the sins of those who associate partners with Him into two categories.

---

## Page 89

One of them consists of commanding what Allah has not commanded, such as associating partners with Him, and forbidding what Allah has not forbidden, such as wholesome things. The former introduces into religion what Allah has not authorized; the latter forbids what Allah has not forbidden.

The same is stated in the authentic hadith narrated by ‘Iyād ibn Himār from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who related that Allah Most High said:

“I created My servants upright in faith, but the devils led them astray. They forbade them what I had made lawful for them and commanded them to associate with Me partners for which I had sent down no authority.”

For this reason, inventing false forms of worship, such as associating partners with Allah and similar practices, predominates among Christians and those who resemble them among deviant worshippers and Sufis. Inventing unfounded prohibitions predominates among Jews and those who resemble them among deviant students of jurisprudence. Indeed, the foundations of the Jewish religion include burdens and shackles in the form of prohibitions. Hence the Messiah said to them:

“And to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you.”

The foundations of the Christian religion include worshipful devotion expressed through ambiguous wording and actions whose significance is not clearly defined. Those whose hearts are inclined to deviate therefore followed what was ambiguous in it, seeking discord and seeking its interpretation.

I have established elsewhere that affirming Allah’s oneness—which means devoting religion sincerely to Him—and the justice we practise together encompass the whole of religion. This comes back to the same principle: devoting religion sincerely to Allah is the foundation of justice, just as associating partners with Allah is a tremendous wrong.

---

## Page 90

And Shaykh al-Islām said:

Know—may Allah have mercy on you—that associating partners with Allah is the gravest sin of disobedience to Him. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills.”

It is recorded in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, was asked, “Which sin is greatest?” He replied, “To set up a rival to Allah when He is the One who created you.” A rival is an equal. Allah Most High says:

“So do not set up rivals to Allah while you know.”

And He Most High says:

“He has set up rivals to Allah to lead others astray from His path. Say, ‘Enjoy your disbelief for a little while; you are indeed one of the inhabitants of the Fire.’”

Whoever makes any of Allah’s creatures His equal in the divinity and lordship that belong to Him, Mighty and Majestic, has disbelieved, by the consensus of the Muslim community. For Allah, glorified be He, deserves worship for His own sake: He is the God who is worshipped, whom hearts adore, to whom they turn in longing, and in whom they seek refuge in times of hardship. Everything besides Him is dependent and held in subjection as a servant. How, then, could it be fit to be a god?

Allah Most High says:

“They have made some of His servants a part of Him. Indeed, man is clearly ungrateful.”

And He Most High says:

“There is no one in the heavens and the earth who will not come to the Most Merciful as a servant.”

And Allah Most High says:

“The Messiah would never disdain to be a servant of Allah, nor would the angels brought near to Him.”

And He Most High says:

“Do not set up another god alongside Allah. Indeed, I am a clear warner to you from Him.”

And He Most High says:

“Say, ‘I have been commanded to worship Allah, sincerely devoting

---

## Page 91

my worship to Him.’” Thus Allah—glorified be He—is the One who deserves to be worshipped for His own sake.

He Most High says:

“All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

Here, the word for “praise” is preceded by alif and lām, the definite article, which encompasses every form of praise. This indicates that all praise belongs to Allah. He then expresses exclusivity in His words:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

This elaborates upon His words:

“All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds.”

It indicates that none is rightly worshipped except Allah and that no one besides Him deserves worship. His words:

“You alone we worship”

refer to worshipping Him as His divinity requires: with love, fear and hope, and in accordance with His commands and prohibitions. His words:

“And You alone we ask for help”

refer to what His lordship requires: reliance upon Him, entrusting one’s affairs to Him, and submission. For the Lord—glorified and exalted be He—is the Owner; the designation also conveys the meanings of lordship and setting things right. The Owner is the One who disposes of His dominion as He wills.

When the servant comes to perceive, through the inner reality of lordship, that all dominion and governance are in Allah’s hand—as He Most High says:

“Blessed is He in whose hand is dominion, and He has power over all things”—

he sees no benefit or harm, movement or stillness, contraction or expansion, lowering or raising, without recognizing Allah—glorified and exalted be He—as its doer and creator, the One who contracts and expands, raises and lowers. This inward witnessing is the inner meaning of the divine words governing creation. It is knowledge of the attribute of lordship. The former is knowledge of the attribute of divinity: the unveiling of the inner meaning of the divine words prescribing obligations. Thus, fully observing commands and prohibitions and realizing love, fear and hope arise from the unveiling of knowledge of divinity.

Fully realizing reliance upon Allah, entrusting one’s affairs to Him, and submission comes after the unveiling of knowledge of lordship

---

## Page 92

, which is knowledge of the governance that pervades all creation, as He—mighty and majestic—says:

“When We intend something, all We say to it is, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

When the servant fully realizes this inward witnessing, and Allah enables him to do so without its obscuring the first, he possesses true understanding of his servitude. These two forms of inward witnessing are the foundation upon which religion rests. Every inward witnessing of mercy, kindness, generosity and beauty falls within the witnessing of lordship.

For this reason, it has been said that this verse encompasses all the secrets of the Quran:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

Its first part entails worshipping Him by observing His commands and prohibitions, with love, fear and hope, as we have mentioned. Its latter part entails servitude to Him through entrusting one’s affairs to Him, submission and relinquishing one’s own choice. All forms of servitude fall within this.

Whoever loses sight of both this inward witnessing and the first, and sees Allah—mighty and majestic—watching over all things, watching over every soul and what it has earned, disposing of all things and ruling over them, then sees everything proceeding from Him through the execution of His decree and His all-powerful will, becomes so absorbed in what he perceives that he loses all discernment and distinction, nullifies commands, prohibitions and prophethood, and passes out of Islam as an arrow passes through its quarry.

If, however, this inward witnessing has overwhelmed him and deprived him of reason because of the overpowering force with which it comes upon him, and because his insight is too weak to bring the two forms of witnessing together, then he is excused, though deficient—unlike one who combines both: the command prescribing religious obligations and the witnessing of the command governing creation in accordance with the divine will.

Many who tread the spiritual path have stumbled in this inward witnessing because of their limited knowledge of what Allah sent His messengers with. This is because they worshipped Allah according to what they wanted from Him, becoming so absorbed in their own desire that they lost sight of what the Truth—mighty and majestic—wanted from them. For the Truth grants sufficiency through what He wills and loves. Had they worshipped Allah according to what He wills

---

## Page 93

—what He wills of them—none of this would have befallen them. For when the servant witnesses his own servitude and is not alert to his Master’s command, he does not become so absorbed in his worship that he loses sight of the One he worships, nor so absorbed in the One he worships that he loses sight of his worship. Rather, he has two eyes: with one he looks towards the One he worships as though he sees Him, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said when asked about excellence in worship:

“Worship Allah as though you see Him; and if you do not see Him, He surely sees you.”

With the other, he looks to his Master’s command, so that he may carry it out in accordance with the prescribed command that his Master loves and approves.

With this established, if the association of partners with Allah is of a kind that renders its perpetrator a disbeliever, it is of two types: associating partners with Him in His divinity, and associating partners with Him in His lordship.

As for associating partners with Him in His divinity, this means setting up a rival to Allah—that is, an equal to Him in one’s worship, love, fear, hope or turning to Him. This is the association of partners that Allah does not forgive except through repentance from it. He, Most High, says:

“Tell those who disbelieve that if they desist, their past will be forgiven.”

It was over this that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, fought the Arab polytheists, for they associated partners with Allah in His divinity. Allah, Most High, says:

“Among people are those who take others besides Allah as equals, loving them as they love Allah. But those who believe are stronger in their love for Allah.”

The verse continues.

“We worship them only so that they may bring us nearer to Allah.”

The verse continues.

“Has he made all the gods into one God? This is truly an astonishing thing!”

And He, Most High, says:

“Cast into Hell every stubborn disbeliever,”

up to His words:

“who set up another god alongside Allah. Cast him into the severe punishment.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, asked Husayn, “How many do you worship?”

He replied, “Six on earth and one in heaven.”

He asked, “Which one do you turn to in hope and fear?”

He replied, “The one in heaven.”

He said, “Will you not embrace Islam, so that I may teach you some words?”

He embraced Islam, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Say: O Allah, inspire me to right guidance and protect me from the evil of my own soul.”

As for His lordship, they acknowledged it. Allah, Most High, says:

“And if you ask them who created

---

## Page 94

the heavens and the earth and subjected the sun and the moon, they will surely say, ‘Allah.’” And He says:

“Say: To whom belong the earth and all who are in it, if you know? They will say, ‘To Allah.’”

Up to His words:

“How, then, are you deluded?”

None of them ever believed that the idols sent down rain, provided for the world or governed it. Rather, as we have explained, their association of partners consisted in taking others besides Allah as equals, loving them as they loved Allah. This shows that whoever loves something besides Allah as he loves Allah, Most High, has associated a partner with Him. This is illustrated by His words:

“They will say, as they dispute there: ‘By Allah, we were clearly astray when we made you equal to the Lord of all worlds.’”

The same applies to anyone who fears someone as he fears Allah, places hope in him as he places hope in Allah, or does anything similar.

As for the second type, it is associating partners with Allah in His lordship. For the Lord, glorified be He, is the One who owns and governs, gives and withholds, brings harm and benefit, lowers and raises, honours and humiliates. Whoever regards someone other than Him as the one who gives or withholds, brings harm or benefit, honours or humiliates, has associated a partner with Him in His lordship.

If, however, he wishes to free himself from this association of partners, let him look, for example, to the original Giver and thank Him for the blessings He has bestowed upon him. Let him also consider the person who has done him a kindness and repay him for it, in accordance with the Prophet’s words, peace be upon him:

“Whoever does you a kindness, repay him. If you cannot find anything with which to repay him, pray for him until you consider that you have repaid him.”

For all blessings come from Allah, Most High, as He says:

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah.”

And He, Most High, says:

“To all—these and those—We extend the gifts of your Lord.”

Allah, glorified be He, is therefore the true Giver, for He is the One who created all provision, determined it and brought it to whomever He wills among His servants. Thus, the Giver is the One who provided for the benefactor and moved his heart to give to others. He is the First and the Last.

---

## Page 95

Further support for this meaning is found in the Prophet’s words, peace be upon him, to Ibn ʿAbbās, may Allah be pleased with him:

“Know that if the entire community were to unite to benefit you, they could benefit you only with something Allah had already decreed for you. And if they were to unite to harm you, they could harm you only with something Allah had already decreed against you. The pens have been lifted and the pages have dried.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “This is an authentic hadith.”

This shows that, in reality, none but Allah brings benefit or harm. The same applies to everything we have mentioned concerning what His lordship entails. Whoever follows this great path finds relief from servitude to created beings and from looking to them for help. He also spares others his blame and condemnation. His heart becomes devoted exclusively to Allah’s oneness, his faith grows stronger, his breast expands, and his heart is illuminated. Whoever puts his trust in Allah, He is sufficient for him.

For this reason, al-Fudayl ibn ʿIyād, may Allah have mercy on him, said: “Whoever understands people finds relief.” What he means—and Allah knows best—is that they can neither benefit nor harm.

As for hidden association of partners with Allah, scarcely anyone escapes it. An example is loving someone else alongside Allah. If, however, that love is for Allah—as in love for the prophets, the righteous, and righteous deeds—it does not fall into this category. Rather, such love demonstrates the reality of love: to love the beloved and what he loves, and to hate what he hates. Whoever is true in his love cannot act contrary to his beloved, for such opposition arises only from a deficiency in following him. Allah’s words, Most High, show that this indicates deficient love:

“Say: If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive you your sins.”

The verse continues. This, then, is not the kind of love under discussion.

---

## Page 96

What is under discussion is love for others that is not for the sake of Allah, Most High. This is undoubtedly a deficiency in devoting one’s love exclusively to Allah, and it indicates deficient love for Him: if one’s love for Him were complete, one would love none besides Him. The first kind of love mentioned does not contradict this, for it is included in loving Him.

Here is a measure that does not wrong you: the stronger a servant’s love for his Lord becomes, the less significant and fewer the objects of his love become. The weaker that love becomes, the more numerous and varied its objects become. The same applies to fear, hope, and similar matters. If a servant’s fear of his Lord is complete, he fears nothing besides Him. Allah, Most High, says:

“Those who convey Allah’s messages and fear Him, and fear none but Allah.”

When his fear of Allah diminishes, he fears created beings. His fear of them varies according to the decrease or increase in his fear of Allah, as we have explained concerning love. The same applies to hope and other such matters. This is the hidden association of partners with Allah from which scarcely anyone escapes except those whom Allah, Most High, protects. It has been reported that the association of partners with Allah within this community is more hidden than the creeping of ants. The way to free oneself from all these maladies is sincere devotion to Allah, Mighty and Majestic. Allah, Most High, says:

“So whoever hopes to meet his Lord, let him do righteous deeds and associate no one in the worship of his Lord.”

Sincere devotion is attained only after renunciation, and there is no renunciation without piety. Piety consists in obeying the commands and observing the prohibitions.

---

## Page 97

Section

It is necessary to explain the principle by which hearts move towards Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and hold fast to Him, so that their maladies diminish or disappear altogether through Allah’s might and power.

Know that three things move hearts towards Allah, Mighty and Majestic: love, fear, and hope. The strongest of these is love. It is sought for its own sake, for it is desired both in this world and in the Hereafter, unlike fear, which ceases in the Hereafter. Allah, Most High, says:

“Indeed, the friends of Allah will have nothing to fear, nor will they grieve.”

The purpose of fear is to restrain a person and prevent him from straying from the path. Love moves the servant along his journey towards his beloved, and his progress towards Him corresponds to the weakness or strength of that love. Fear prevents him from straying from the path to his beloved, while hope leads him onward. This is a fundamental principle to which every servant must pay attention, for he cannot attain servitude to Allah without it. Everyone must be a servant of Allah, not of anyone else.

If it is asked, “At times, a servant may lack the love that impels him to seek his beloved. What, then, moves hearts?” we answer: two things move them.

The first is frequent remembrance of the beloved, for remembering Him frequently attaches hearts to Him. This is why Allah, Mighty and Majestic, commanded frequent remembrance, saying in the verse:

“O you who believe, remember Allah abundantly.”

“And glorify Him morning and evening.”

The second is contemplating His blessings and favors. Allah, Most High, says:

“So remember Allah’s favors so that you may

---

## Page 98

succeed.” Allah, Most High, also says:

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah.”

And He, Most High, says:

“He has lavished upon you His blessings, both outward and inward.”

And He, Most High, says:

“If you were to count Allah’s blessings, you could not enumerate them.”

When the servant remembers the blessings Allah has bestowed upon him—placing the sky and the earth, and the trees and animals within them, at his service, and lavishing upon him inward blessings, such as faith and others—this is bound to awaken a motivating impulse within him. Likewise, fear is stirred by contemplating the verses concerning threats of punishment, stern warnings, being presented before Allah, the reckoning, and similar matters. Hope, in turn, is stirred by contemplating His generosity, forbearance, and pardon, and what has been related concerning hope.

The discussion of Allah’s oneness is extensive. The purpose here is merely to point out, with the briefest indication, the freedom from need that it entails. Allah—Glorified and Exalted—is most knowing. May Allah send blessings and peace upon Muhammad, his family, and his Companions.

---

## Page 99

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Allah relates that our imam Abraham, His intimate friend, said to the unjust idolaters who disputed with him:

“How should I fear what you associate with Him, while you do not fear associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority to you? Which of the two parties has more right to security, if you know?”

“Those who believe and do not taint their faith with wrongdoing—it is they who will have security, and they are rightly guided.”

The authentic hadith collection relates, on the authority of ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—explained that “wrongdoing” here means associating partners with Allah. He said: “Have you not heard the words of the righteous servant: ‘Indeed, associating partners with Allah is a tremendous wrong’?”

Abraham thus rejected the notion that we should fear any of the created beings, above or below, whom they associate with Allah, while they themselves have no fear of associating with Allah a partner for whom He has sent down no authority. He made clear that those who do not associate partners with Allah are the ones who are secure and rightly guided.

This is a magnificent verse that benefits the believer devoted to Allah alone in various circumstances. Associating partners with Allah within this community can be more imperceptible than the creeping of ants, quite apart from its blatant forms. It includes associating partners in worship and devotion, in obedience and submission, and in belief and acceptance.

Thus, extremists among the Christians and the Rāfidah (rejectionists), along with misguided Sufis, fuqarā’ (ascetic devotees), and common people, associate partners with Allah: sometimes by invoking someone other than Allah, sometimes by offering that other being some form of worship, and sometimes by doing both. Whoever associates partners in this way also associates partners in obedience.

---

## Page 100

And many students of jurisprudence, soldiers in the service of kings, followers of judges, and ordinary people who follow them associate partners with Allah in obedience. When the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—recited to ‘Adī ibn Hātim:

“They have taken their scholars and monks as lords besides Allah, and also the Messiah, the son of Mary,”

‘Adī said, “O Messenger of Allah, they did not worship them.” He replied, “They did not worship them, but they made unlawful things lawful for them, and they obeyed them; and they made lawful things unlawful for them, and they obeyed them.”

Thus, you find a person who has gone astray treating as obligatory whatever his leader makes obligatory, as unlawful whatever he prohibits, as lawful whatever he permits, and as religion whatever he prescribes—whether for religious purposes, worldly purposes, or both. He then intimidates anyone who refuses this association of partners with Allah, while he himself has no fear of having associated something with Allah in obedience without any authority from Allah. This excludes those whom Allah has commanded people to obey, such as a messenger, a ruler, a scholar, a parent, a shaykh, and others.

As for the third form of associating partners with Allah, many followers of speculative theologians and philosophers—indeed, even some students of jurisprudence and Sufis, and even some followers of kings and judges—accept their leader’s statements concerning beliefs about matters of fact: his judgments that certain views are sound and others unsound, his praise of certain views and some who hold them, and his condemnation of others, without any authority from Allah. Such a person fears the one whom he has made a partner in belief and acceptance, yet does not fear having associated a person with Allah by believing in him and accepting his word without any authority from Allah. This excludes those whom Allah has prescribed that people believe, such as the messengers, scholars who convey their teachings, truthful witnesses, and others.

Thus, obedience to human beings and acceptance of their statements may be either prescribed or unprescribed. As for worship, seeking assistance, and adoration, human beings have no claim to any of these under any circumstances. For, as someone said, “I have never put my hand into anyone’s bowl without becoming subservient to him.” There is no doubt that whoever supports you and provides for you

---

## Page 101

has authority over you. The believer therefore wishes to be subject to no authority except that of Allah, His Messenger, and those who obey Allah and His Messenger. Accepting people’s money gives them authority over him. If his intention is to free himself from this authority and coercion, that is good and praiseworthy, and it safeguards his religious integrity. But if he intends to place himself above them, assume leadership over them, and make an ostentatious display of the former attitude, that is blameworthy. By refraining from accepting their money and leaving it with them, he may also intend to maintain his independence from them.

These, then, are four worthy aims: his own independence; his dignity, so that he neither depends on people nor abases himself before them; and the preservation of their wealth and their religion. Their wealth is thus not diminished: he neither deprives them of it nor, by accepting it from them, draws them into exercising a dominance over him that he would not wish for them. This benefits him by sparing him humiliation and dependence on them, and benefits them by preserving their wealth and religion.

There may also be benefit in winning their hearts by leaving their money with them, so that they accept what he tells them. Just as their hearts are won by giving to them, they may likewise be won by leaving their money with them. This may also safeguard their religion, for when money is accepted from them, they too may become emboldened to commit various sins and abandon various acts of obedience, refusing to accept commands to do what is right and prohibitions against what is wrong. There are other benefits and worthy aims in this as well.

If, however, accepting their money leads them to expect something of him, so that they enlist his help in sin or prevent him from an act of obedience, these are further harms. They are numerous, and all stem from his subservience to them and his dependence on them. They cannot prevent him from an act of obedience unless he is subservient to them or dependent on them, nor can they employ him in sin except under those same conditions. For giving calls for recompense and reciprocation. If no worldly repayment is forthcoming in the form of money or some benefit, all that remains is the benefit they expect to receive from him.

---

## Page 102

There are also objectionable and blameworthy reasons for refusing gifts. These include refusing for show, in imitation of someone whose aim is independence, dignity, and compassion for people in their religious and worldly affairs. Another is arrogance toward them and a desire for superiority, using this refusal to subjugate them and place oneself above them. This too is blameworthy. Another is stinginess toward them: if he accepts from them, he will need to benefit them and attend to their needs, so he may refrain from accepting because he begrudges them those benefits. Another is laziness about doing good to them. These, then, are four corrupt motives for refusing gifts: arrogance, ostentation, stinginess, and laziness.

In sum, a person may decline to accept money in order to secure a benefit for himself, ward off harm from himself, secure a benefit for others, or ward off harm from them. Refraining from accepting it preserves his independence and dignity, which benefit him, and safeguards his religious and worldly affairs from the various harms that may result from accepting it. It also benefits others by preserving their wealth and religion, and by averting the harm they may incur through giving something that could be detrimental to them.

He may, however, refuse it in order to harm others or withhold benefit from them. This is blameworthy, as explained above. Refusing may also harm him or deprive him of a benefit: he may need the money, so that refusing it harms him; or accepting and spending it may benefit him in his religious and worldly affairs, yet he forgoes that benefit without any countervailing consideration of equal weight.

This is why we have discussed this matter in detail, for it is a matter of great importance. Corresponding to it is the question of accepting gifts, which likewise requires a detailed treatment. As a rule, however, refraining from accepting has been better than accepting, and this is why people hold such conduct in greater esteem. Yet when accepting is proper, it is better—by which I mean accepting and distributing to people.

---

## Page 103

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about someone who said:

“It is permissible to seek relief from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in every matter in which relief is sought from Allah Most High, on the understanding that he is one of Allah Most High’s means of obtaining relief. Likewise, relief may be sought from the other prophets and righteous people in every matter in which relief is sought from Allah Most High.

“Whoever seeks to draw near to Allah Most High through His Prophet to have distress relieved has thereby sought relief from the Prophet, whether he uses the expression ‘seeking relief,’ ‘seeking a means of approach,’ or another expression with the same meaning. Saying, ‘O my God, I approach You through Your Messenger,’ or, ‘I seek relief through Your Messenger before You, asking that You forgive me,’ constitutes seeking relief from the Messenger in the literal sense, in the Arabic language and among all peoples.”

He said:

“People have always understood, in earlier times and today, what it means to seek relief from a person: that seeking relief can properly be attributed to created beings, that relief is sought from them by taking them as a means of approach, and that the expression applies to anyone who asks for distress to be relieved by taking a person as such a means. This is valid in the case of prophets and righteous people.”

He said:

“Regarding the report narrated by al-Tabarānī from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

‘Some of the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, said, “Seek relief from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, against this hypocrite.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Relief is not to be sought from me; relief is to be sought only from Allah.”’

---

## Page 104

If the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were to deny that relief could be sought from him, or make a similar statement, thereby pointing to monotheism and affirming that power belongs exclusively to the Creator, we ourselves would not be entitled to make that denial. We permit the unqualified statement that relief may be sought from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and from a righteous person—meaning in every matter in which relief is sought from Allah Most High—without needing to specify that he is a means and an intermediary. Anyone who says, ‘Relief is not to be sought from him,’ disparages him and is an unbeliever on that account, though he is excused if he is ignorant. Once he understands the meaning of seeking relief, if he then persists in his statement, he becomes an unbeliever. Seeking to draw near to Allah through him constitutes seeking relief from him, as stated earlier.”

Is any Muslim scholar known to have said that it is permissible to seek relief from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and from a righteous person in every matter in which relief is sought from Allah Most High? Is it permissible to state this without qualification, as this speaker has done? Does seeking to draw near to Allah Most High through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, a righteous person, or anyone else, in every matter, constitute seeking relief from the person taken as a means of approach, as this speaker has claimed concerning all languages?

Whether or not seeking to draw near to Allah through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or a righteous person constitutes seeking relief from him, is any scholar known to have said that it is permissible to seek to draw near to Allah through every prophet and righteous person?

Shaykh ‘Izz al-Dīn ibn ‘Abd al-Salām ruled in his well-known fatwas that it is not permissible to seek to draw near to Allah Most High through anyone except the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, provided that the hadith concerning this is authentic. Has anyone expressed a view contrary to this shaykh’s ruling?

Assuming that there is disagreement on this issue, does someone who says, ‘The other prophets and righteous people are not to be taken as a means of approach,’ in accordance with Shaykh ‘Izz al-Dīn’s ruling, become an unbeliever, as this speaker has declared him to be? Would the shaykh’s ruling itself then constitute unbelief?

Indeed, even concerning taking him as a means of approach, if someone were to say, ‘He is not to be taken as a means of approach;

---

## Page 105

nor is relief to be sought from him, except during his lifetime and in his presence, not after his death or in his absence,’ would that constitute unbelief? Or would it constitute disparagement?

And if someone were to say, ‘In matters that none but Allah Most High can accomplish, relief is to be sought only from Allah—that is, such things are to be requested only from Allah Most High,’ would that constitute unbelief, or would it be true? If the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, denies something of himself because it belongs exclusively to divine lordship, is it forbidden to deny it of him, or is such a denial obligatory or permissible? Give us a satisfying and sufficient answer—may Allah have mercy on you, grant you success, and reward you—if Allah Most High wills.

THE ANSWER

The answer:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

No Muslim scholar has said that relief may be sought from any created being in every matter in which relief is sought from Allah Most High—not from a prophet, an angel, a righteous person, or anyone else. Rather, it is necessarily known in the religion of Islam that making such an unqualified statement is impermissible.

No one has said that seeking to draw near to Allah through a prophet constitutes seeking relief from him. Indeed, ordinary people who invoke various things as means of drawing near to Allah in their supplications—for example, saying, ‘I seek to draw near to You by the right of Shaykh so-and-so, or by his sanctity,’ or, ‘I seek to draw near to You through the Tablet and the Pen, or through the Kaaba,’ or other such expressions they use in their supplications—know that they are not seeking relief from these things.

Someone who seeks relief from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is requesting something from him and asking him for it. By contrast, the one taken as a means of drawing near to Allah is neither invoked nor asked for anything; rather, a request is made through him. Everyone distinguishes between the one addressed in supplication and the means through which the supplication is made.

Seeking relief means requesting deliverance, that is, the removal of hardship, just as seeking support means requesting support, and seeking assistance means requesting help. A created being may be asked for such things as he is able to provide

---

## Page 106

as Allah Most High says:

“If they seek your help in religion, it is your duty to help them.”

And as He says:

“The man from his own people sought his help against the one from his enemies.”

And as Allah Most High says:

“Cooperate in righteousness and piety.”

As for what none but Allah can accomplish, it must be requested only from Allah. For this reason, the Muslims did not seek relief from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, but sought rain through him and sought to draw near to Allah through him. Thus, Sahīh al-Bukhārī records that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, sought rain through al-‘Abbās, saying:

“O Allah, whenever we suffered drought, we would seek to draw near to You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek to draw near to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.”

They would then be granted rain.

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd:

“A man said to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ‘We seek Allah’s intercession with you, and we seek your intercession with Allah.’ He replied, ‘Allah is far too exalted for that. His intercession is not to be sought with any of His creation.’”

He thus approved the man’s statement, “We seek your intercession with Allah,” but rejected his statement, “We seek Allah’s intercession with you.”

The Muslims agree that our Prophet will be an intercessor on the Day of Resurrection and that people will ask him to intercede. According to Ahl al-Sunnah, his intercession for those who have committed major sins will be accepted. According to the Wa‘īdiyya (those who insist that divine threats of punishment must be fulfilled), however, his intercession will be accepted only for an increase in reward.

The claim, “Whoever seeks to draw near to Allah through a prophet, saying, ‘I seek to draw near to You through Your Messenger,’ has actually sought relief from His Messenger, according to the language of the Arabs and of all nations,” is a false attribution to those peoples. Such usage is unknown in the language of any of Adam’s descendants. Rather, everyone knows that the one from whom relief is sought is someone through whom a request is made and who is invoked. They distinguish between the one who is asked and the one through whom a request is made, whether relief is sought from the Creator

---

## Page 107

or from a created being, for it is permissible to seek relief from a created being in matters in which that being is able to help. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the best created being from whom relief may be sought in such matters.

If someone were to say to the person from whom he seeks relief, “I ask you through so-and-so, or by the right of so-and-so,” no one would say that he had sought relief from the one through whom he sought to draw near. Rather, he sought relief only from the person he called upon and asked.

For this reason, those who have written commentaries on Allah’s Most Beautiful Names say:

“Al-Mughīth (the One who grants relief) means al-Mujīb (the One who answers), but granting relief pertains more specifically to actions, and answering more specifically to words.”

As for seeking to draw near to Allah through anyone other than our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—whether this is called seeking relief or not—we know of no one among the early generations who did so or transmitted any report concerning it. We know of nothing concerning it except the shaykh’s ruling prohibiting it. As for seeking to draw near to Allah through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there is a hadith in the Sunan collections, narrated by al-Nasā’ī, al-Tirmidhī, and others:

“A Bedouin came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, my eyesight has been afflicted, so pray to Allah for me.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told him, ‘Perform ablution, pray two units of prayer, then say: “O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad. O Muhammad, I seek your intercession for the restoration of my sight. O Allah, accept Your Prophet’s intercession on my behalf.”’ He also said, ‘If you have a need, do likewise.’ Allah then restored his sight.”

Because of this hadith, the shaykh made an exception for seeking to draw near to Allah through him.

There are two views concerning the meaning of this. The first is that this seeking to draw near is what ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, mentioned when he said:

“Whenever we suffered drought, we would seek to draw near to You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek to draw near to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.”

‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, thus mentioned that they used to seek to draw near to Allah through him during his lifetime when seeking rain, and that after his death they sought to draw near through his uncle al-‘Abbās; and their seeking to draw near to Allah

---

## Page 108

through him consisted in seeking rain through him: he would supplicate, and they would supplicate with him, so that he was their means of drawing near to Allah. The Companions did not do this after his death or in his absence. In such cases, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, interceded for them and supplicated on their behalf. Hence the words in the hadith of the blind man:

“O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf.”

This shows that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had interceded for him, and he therefore asked Allah to accept that intercession on his behalf.

The second view is that seeking to draw near through him may be done during his lifetime and after his death, in his absence and in his presence. No one has said that anyone who holds the first view has disbelieved, nor is there any basis for declaring him an unbeliever. This is a subtle question whose evidence is not clear and manifest. Disbelief consists only in denying what is known by necessity to be part of the religion, denying rulings established by mass transmission and consensus, or the like. People’s disagreement over which supplications are prescribed and which are not is comparable to their disagreement over whether invoking blessings upon him when slaughtering an animal is prescribed. No Muslim regards this as an issue of insulting the Prophet.

As for someone who says that anyone who rejects seeking to draw near—which he has called seeking relief through another—has disbelieved, and declares those who adopt the position of Shaykh ‘Izz al-Dīn and others like him unbelievers, the error in this is too obvious to require a response. Rather, someone who declares others unbelievers over such matters deserves the severe punishment and disciplinary measures deserved by others like him who fabricate lies about the religion, especially in light of the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever calls his brother an unbeliever, that charge falls upon one of them.”

As for someone who says, “In matters that none but Allah can accomplish, relief must be sought from Him alone,” he has spoken the truth. Indeed, he would have spoken well even if he had said, as Abū Yazīd did, “A created being seeking relief from another created being is like a drowning person seeking relief from another drowning person”; or, as Shaykh Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Qurashī said, “A created being seeking relief from another created being is like a prisoner seeking relief from another prisoner.” For this statement, taken without qualification, conveys the sense of seeking relief

---

## Page 109

in an absolute sense, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Ibn ‘Abbās:

“When you ask, ask Allah; and when you seek help, seek help from Allah.”

When the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, denies something concerning himself, he speaks the truth and is worthy of belief in that regard, just as he speaks the truth and is worthy of belief in everything he reports, whether denying or affirming. We must believe everything he reports, whether denying or affirming. Whoever rejects his report out of reverence for him resembles the Christians who, out of reverence for Christ, disbelieved him when he declared himself a servant. We may deny what he denied, and no one may contradict his denial under any circumstances. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 110

Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked: (*)

“What do the eminent scholars, the leaders of the religion—may Allah enable them to obey Him—say concerning someone who states, ‘Relief is not to be sought from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace’? Is it forbidden for him to say this, and is it disbelief or not? If he supports his statement with verses from the Book of Allah and hadiths of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, does his evidence avail him or not? When proof is established from the Book and the Sunnah, what is incumbent upon someone who opposes it? Give us your ruling, and may you be rewarded.”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

It has been established through the widely transmitted—indeed, mass-transmitted—Sunnah and the agreement of the Muslim community that our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the intercessor whose intercession is accepted; that his intercession on behalf of creation will be accepted on the Day of Resurrection; that people will seek his intercession, asking him to intercede for them with their Lord (*); and that he will intercede for them.

Furthermore, the people of the Sunnah and the community agree that his intercession for those guilty of major sins will be accepted, and that none of those who affirm Allah’s oneness will remain in the Fire forever.

As for the Khawārij and the Mu‘tazilah, they denied his intercession for those guilty of major sins, but did not deny his intercession for the believers. These are misguided innovators; whether they are to be declared unbelievers is a matter of disagreement and requires qualification.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (pp. 16–17):
> This is the fatwa to which al-Bakrī al-Shāfi‘ī objected and against which he wrote a refutation. The shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, responded with al-Istighātha (Seeking Relief), known as al-Radd ‘alā al-Bakrī (Refutation of al-Bakrī). This fatwa appears in Talkhīs Kitāb al-Istighātha (Abridgment of the Book on Seeking Relief), pp. 211–216. Three points should be noted:
> 
> First: The version here in al-Fatāwā contains scribal misreadings and minor differences in approximately twenty places. These do not affect the meaning, and most result from changes made by copyists.
> 
> Second: In al-Istighātha, the shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, omitted nearly a page of this fatwa. He stated there, p. 215 (1/420): “They said: ‘The difference between someone seeking relief and someone making an invocation is that the former calls for relief, whereas the latter calls upon the one invoked’—an account of this has already been given in full, so this is not the place to examine it exhaustively—and it includes: ‘Seeking relief from the Messenger, in the sense of asking him for what befits his station, is not disputed by any Muslim…’”
> 
> His words, “An account of this has already been given in full, so this is not the place to examine it exhaustively,” indicate two things:
> 
> One: He omitted part of the fatwa, namely the passage in al-Fatāwā from 1/111, line eleven, to 1/112, line seven.
> 
> The other: This fatwa had already been mentioned earlier in al-Istighātha. The printed abridgment, however, contains no mention of it before this point. Perhaps it was among the passages abridged by Ibn Kathīr, may Allah have mercy on him. Allah knows best.
> 
> Third: The shaykh did not include in al-Istighātha the last five lines of this fatwa as it appears here (1/113). Perhaps these, too, belonged to the previously mentioned material abridged by Ibn Kathīr. Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 111

As for anyone who denies what has been established by mass transmission and consensus, he is an unbeliever once the proof has been established against him, whether or not he calls this “seeking relief.”

As for someone who acknowledges his intercession but denies the Companions’ practice of seeking nearness to Allah through him and seeking his intercession: al-Bukhārī related in his Sahīh, from Anas, that whenever they suffered drought, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb would seek rain through al-‘Abbās ibn ‘Abd al-Muttalib, saying:

“O Allah, we used to seek nearness to You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek nearness to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.” They would then be granted rain.

In the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd and elsewhere, it is related:

“A Bedouin said to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ‘People are suffering hardship, families are hungry, and possessions have been ruined. So supplicate to Allah for us, for we seek your intercession with Allah, and we seek Allah’s intercession with you.’

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then glorified Allah, until the effect of this was apparent on his Companions’ faces. He said, ‘Woe to you! Allah is not sought as an intercessor with any of His creation. Allah’s majesty is far greater than that.’”

He then related the remainder of the hadith. Thus, the Prophet rejected his statement, “We seek Allah’s intercession with you,” but did not reject his statement, “We seek your intercession with Allah.” Rather, he approved it, thereby establishing its permissibility. Whoever denies this is misguided, mistaken, and an innovator; whether he is to be declared an unbeliever is a matter of disagreement and requires qualification.

As for someone who acknowledges what the Book, the Sunnah, and consensus have established concerning his intercession, seeking nearness to Allah through him, and similar matters, but says that none but Allah should be invoked, and that matters within Allah’s power alone should be sought from Him alone—such as forgiveness of sins, guidance of hearts, sending down rain, causing plants to grow, and similar matters—he is correct in this. Indeed, this too is a matter concerning which there is no disagreement among Muslims.

As Allah Most High says:

“And who forgives sins except Allah?”

And He says:

“Indeed, you do not guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills.”

And as He Most High says:

“O mankind, remember the favor of Allah upon you. Is there any creator other than Allah who provides for you from heaven

---

## Page 112

and earth?” And as He Most High says:

“Allah made it only as good tidings for you, and so that your hearts might be reassured by it. Victory comes only from Allah.”

And He says:

“If you do not support him, Allah has already supported him when those who disbelieved drove him out, one of two, when they were in the cave and he said to his companion, ‘Do not grieve; indeed, Allah is with us.’”

The meanings affirmed by the Book and the Sunnah must be affirmed, and those negated by the Book and the Sunnah must be negated. If expressions conveying these meanings, whether by affirmation or negation, occur in the words of Allah and His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they must be accepted. If they occur in someone else’s words and his intended meaning is clear, he is judged accordingly; otherwise, he must be asked to clarify his meaning. An expression in the words of Allah and His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, may have a sound meaning, yet some people may understand it differently from what Allah and His Messenger intended. Such an understanding must be rejected.

For example, al-Tabarānī related in his al-Mu‘jam al-Kabīr:

“During the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there was a hypocrite who harmed the believers. Abū Bakr al-Siddīq said, ‘Come with us so that we may seek relief from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, against this hypocrite.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Relief is not to be sought from me; relief is to be sought only from Allah.’”

By this statement, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, meant only the second sense: asking him for something that Allah alone can do. Otherwise, the Companions used to ask him to supplicate and seek rain through him. Thus, in Sahīh al-Bukhārī, Ibn ‘Umar said:

“At times I would recall the poet’s words as I looked at the face of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, while he prayed for rain. He would not come down before a gutter was gushing with rainwater:

‘And a fair-faced man, through whose countenance rain is sought from the clouds,
A refuge for orphans, a protector of widows.’”

These are the words of Abū Tālib. Accordingly, scholars who have written about the names of Allah Most High have said: Everyone subject to religious obligations must know that there is no source of relief and no giver of relief in the absolute sense except Allah, and that every

---

## Page 113

act of relief comes from Him, even when He brings it about through someone else. It is therefore truly attributable to Him, glorified and exalted is He, and only figuratively to others.

They said:

“Among His names, Most High, are al-Mughīth (the Giver of Relief) and al-Ghiyāth (the Source of Relief). The name al-Mughīth is mentioned in the hadith of Abū Hurayrah.” They also said that the Muslim community unanimously agreed on this.

Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Halīmī said:

“Al-Ghiyāth is the same as al-Mughīth. The expression most commonly used is ‘the Source of Relief for Those Who Seek It.’ It means the One who comes to His servants’ aid in adversity when they call upon Him, answers them, and delivers them. In the account of the prayer for rain in the two Sahīh collections, we find:

‘O Allah, grant us relief! O Allah, grant us relief!’

“The verb aghāthahu (‘he brought him relief’) has the verbal nouns ighāthah, ghiyāth, and ghawth (‘relief’). This name conveys the meaning of ‘the One Who Answers’ and ‘the One Who Responds.’ Allah Most High says:

‘When you sought help from your Lord, He answered you.’

“However, providing relief applies more properly to actions, and responding to words, though either may be used in place of the other.”

They said: “The difference between someone seeking relief and someone making a supplication is that the former cries out for relief, whereas the latter calls upon the one addressed and the giver of relief.”

This, however, is open to question, for one form of seeking relief is: “O Allah, help the Muslims!” It is reported that Ma‘rūf al-Karkhī would frequently cry, “Help!” and say, “I heard Allah say:

‘When you sought help from your Lord, He answered you.’”

A transmitted supplication also says:

“O Ever-Living, O Sustainer of all, there is no deity but You. Through Your mercy I seek relief. Set all my affairs right for me, and do not leave me to myself for the blink of an eye, nor to anyone among Your creation.”

Seeking relief through His mercy is, in reality, seeking relief from Him, just as seeking refuge in His attributes is, in reality, seeking refuge in Him, and swearing by His attributes is, in reality, swearing by Him. Thus, a hadith says:

“I seek refuge in the perfect words of Allah from the evil of what He has created.”

Another says:

“I seek refuge in Your pleasure from Your wrath, in Your pardon from Your punishment, and in You from You. I cannot adequately praise You; You are as You have praised Yourself.”

---

## Page 114

For this reason, among the proofs the imams cited that Allah’s speech is uncreated were the words:

“I seek refuge in the perfect words of Allah.”

They said: “Seeking refuge in a created being is not permissible.”

The same applies to oaths. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath should swear by Allah or remain silent.”

Another wording states:

“Whoever swears by anything other than Allah has committed shirk.”
