show that the prescribed means of drawing near to Allah, as they understood it, was through his supplication and intercession, not by asking Allah by his person. Had the latter been prescribed, ʿUmar, the Emigrants and the Helpers would not have turned from asking Allah by the Messenger to asking Him by al-ʿAbbās.

Disagreement became widespread over asking Allah by the prophets and the righteous, but not over adjuring Him by them, because there is a distinction between asking and adjuring. One who asks is humble and imploring, making his request on grounds conducive to its being answered. One who adjures, however, adopts a higher stance: he makes a request and reinforces it with an oath. He adjures only someone whom he believes will fulfil his oath. Having one’s oath fulfilled is therefore particular to certain servants, whereas having one’s requests answered is general.

Allah answers the supplication of someone in desperate need and the supplication of someone who has been wronged, even if that person is an unbeliever. In an authentic report, the Prophet said:

“No one supplicates to Allah with a supplication that involves neither sin nor the severing of kinship ties without Allah granting him one of three things in return: either He promptly grants what he has asked for, or He stores up an equivalent good for him

---

## Page 226

or He wards off an equivalent evil from him. They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, then we shall supplicate more.’ He replied, ‘Allah has still more to give.’”

As for seeking to draw near to Allah through the prophets in the sense of asking Him by them—which Abū Hanīfa, his companions and others held to be impermissible—nothing in the known teachings of Mālik’s school contradicts that position, let alone provides a basis for treating this as a matter of insulting the Prophet. Whoever attributes to Mālik’s school permission to seek to draw near to Allah through the Prophet by adjuring Allah by him or asking Allah by him has no transmitted statement from Mālik or his companions to support this, much less a statement from Mālik that “This is an insult to the Messenger or a disparagement of him.”

Rather, what is known of Mālik is that he disliked a supplicant saying, “O my Master, my Master,” and said: “Say as the prophets said: ‘O my Lord, O my Lord, O Generous One.’” He also disliked saying, “O Compassionate One, O Bestower of Favours,” as this is not transmitted from him.

If Mālik disliked such supplications because he did not consider them prescribed, how could he consider it permissible to ask Allah by a created being, whether a prophet or anyone else? He knew that when the Companions suffered drought in the Year of Ashes, they did not ask Allah by any created being, whether a prophet or anyone else. Rather, ʿUmar said:

“O Allah, when we suffered drought, we used to seek to draw near to You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek to draw near to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.”

They would then be granted rain. Likewise, it is established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of Ibn ʿUmar, Anas and others, that when they suffered drought, they sought to draw near to Allah only through the Prophet’s supplication and his prayer for rain, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is not reported of any of them that, during his lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he asked Allah Most High by a created being

---

## Page 227

—neither by him nor by anyone else, whether when praying for rain or otherwise. We shall discuss the hadith of the blind man, if Allah Most High wills.

Had asking Allah by him been a known practice among the Companions, they would have said to ʿUmar:

“Asking Allah and seeking to draw near to Him through the Prophet is preferable to asking Him and seeking to draw near to Him through al-ʿAbbās. Why, then, should we abandon the prescribed practice we followed during his lifetime—seeking to draw near to Allah through the best of creation—in favour of seeking to draw near to Him through one of his relatives? This would mean abandoning the prescribed sunnah, turning away from what is better, and asking Allah Most High through the weaker of two means when the stronger is available to us—all while we are in the direst need during the Year of Ashes, which has become proverbial for drought.”

Muʿāwiya did as ʿUmar had done, in the presence of the Companions and Successors who were with him: they sought to draw near to Allah through Yazīd ibn al-Aswad al-Jurashī, just as ʿUmar had done through al-ʿAbbās.

Likewise, jurists among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others stated that, when praying for rain, one seeks to draw near to Allah through the supplications of good and righteous people. They said: “If they are relatives of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that is preferable, following ʿUmar’s example.” No scholar said that Allah Most High should, in this matter, be asked by a prophet or by anyone else.

Likewise, whoever reports that Mālik permitted asking the Messenger or anyone else after their deaths—or attributes this to any other imam of the Muslims, such as al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad or others—has lied about them. Yet some ignorant people attribute this to Mālik, relying on a fabricated account about him. Even if that account were authentic, the seeking to draw near to Allah described in it would not be of this kind; rather, it would be through the Prophet’s intercession on the Day of Resurrection. Some people, however, distort the account when transmitting it, and the original account itself is weak, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.

---

## Page 228

And al-Qādī ʿIyād did not mention this account in the chapter of his book on visiting the Prophet’s grave. Rather, he mentioned there what is well known from Mālik and his followers. He cited the account only in the context of explaining that respecting, honouring and venerating the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, remains obligatory after his death, just as during his lifetime. The same applies when he, his hadith or his sunnah is mentioned, and when his name is heard.

It is reported that Mālik was asked about Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī and said:

“There is no one from whom I have narrated to you whom Ayyūb did not surpass in excellence.”

He also said:

“He performed Hajj twice, and I would watch him without hearing anything from him. But whenever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was mentioned, he would weep until I felt compassion for him. When I witnessed this and saw his reverence for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, I began recording hadith from him.”

Musʿab ibn ʿAbd Allāh said:

“Whenever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was mentioned, Mālik’s colour would change and he would bend forward, so much that those sitting with him found it distressing.”

One day he was asked about this, and he replied:

“If you had seen what I have seen, you would not disapprove of what you see in me. I used to see Muhammad ibn al-Munkadir—who was the foremost of the reciters—and we could scarcely ever ask him about a hadith without his weeping until we felt compassion for him. I also used to see Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad, who often joked and smiled. Yet whenever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was mentioned in his presence, his face would turn pale. I never saw him narrate from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, except in a state of ritual purity.

“I visited him regularly for a time, and whenever I saw him, he was doing one of three things: praying, remaining silent or reciting the Quran. He would not speak about matters that did not concern him, and he was among the scholars and devout worshippers who fear Allah.

---

## Page 229

And whenever ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn al-Qāsim mentioned the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his complexion would look as though the blood had drained from him, and his tongue would grow dry in his mouth out of awe for the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. I used to visit ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, and whenever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was mentioned in his presence, he would weep until no tears remained in his eyes. I also saw al-Zuhrī—who was among the most amiable and approachable of people—but whenever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was mentioned in his presence, it was as though he did not know you and you did not know him. I used to visit Safwān ibn Sulaym, who was among those devoted to diligent worship. Whenever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was mentioned, he would weep and continue weeping until the people rose, leaving him alone.”

Al-Qādī ʿIyād transmitted all of this from the well-known books of Mālik’s followers. He then cited an account with a singular, disconnected chain of transmission, which he related from several people who had authorized him to transmit it. They said:

Abū al-ʿAbbās Ahmad ibn ʿUmar ibn Dilhāt narrated to us, saying: Abū al-Hasan ʿAlī ibn Fihr narrated to us: Abū Bakr Muhammad ibn Ahmad ibn al-Farah narrated to us: Abū al-Hasan ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Muntāb narrated to us: Yaʿqūb ibn Ishāq ibn Abī Isrā’īl narrated to us: Ibn Humayd narrated to us, saying:

Abū Jaʿfar, the Commander of the Faithful, debated with Mālik in the mosque of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Mālik said to him:

“Commander of the Faithful, do not raise your voice in this mosque, for Allah instructed some people in proper conduct, saying:

‘Do not raise your voices above the voice of the Prophet’

—and the rest of the verse. He praised others, saying:

‘Indeed, those who lower their voices before the Messenger of Allah’

—and the rest of the verse. And He censured others, saying:

‘Indeed, those who call out to you

---

## Page 230

from behind the chambers’

—and the rest of the verse. The reverence due to him after his death is the same as that due to him during his lifetime.”

Abū Jaʿfar yielded to these words and said:

“Abū ʿAbd Allāh, should I face the qiblah and supplicate, or should I face the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?”

He replied:

“Why would you turn your face away from him, when he is your means of approach to Allah on the Day of Resurrection, and that of your father Adam, peace be upon him? Rather, face him and seek his intercession, so that Allah may grant your request for intercession. Allah Most High says:

‘If, when they wronged themselves, they had come to you and sought Allah’s forgiveness, and the Messenger had sought forgiveness for them, they would have found Allah ever Accepting of repentance, Merciful.’”

I say: This account has a disconnected chain of transmission. Muhammad ibn Humayd al-Rāzī did not meet Mālik, much less during the time of Abū Jaʿfar al-Mansūr. Abū Jaʿfar died in Mecca in 158 AH, while Mālik died in 179 AH.

Muhammad ibn Humayd al-Rāzī died in 248 AH. He did not leave his hometown to travel in pursuit of knowledge until he was grown, accompanying his father. Moreover, most hadith scholars considered him weak. Abū Zurʿah and Ibn Wāra declared him a liar. Sālih ibn Muhammad al-Asadī said:

“I have never seen anyone more brazen toward Allah or more adept at lying than he.”

Yaʿqūb ibn Shabība said:

“He transmitted many objectionable reports.”

Al-Nasā’ī said:

“He is not trustworthy.”

Ibn Hibbān said:

“He is alone in transmitting inverted reports from trustworthy narrators.”

The last person to transmit al-Muwatta’ from Mālik was Abū Musʿab, who died in 242 AH. The last person to transmit anything from Mālik was Abū Hudhayfah Ahmad ibn Ismāʿīl al-Sahmī, who died in 259 AH. The chain also includes narrators whose standing is unknown. None of Mālik’s followers known to have studied under him mentioned this account.

---

## Page 231

And Muhammad ibn Humayd is considered weak by hadith scholars when he supplies a chain of transmission. How much more so when he relates, without a connected chain, an account known only through him—and that is assuming it is established that he related it at all. Mālik’s followers agree that a report of this kind cannot establish any opinion attributed to Mālik on a question of jurisprudence. Indeed, when Syrian narrators such as al-Walīd ibn Muslim and Marwān ibn Muhammad al-Tātārī transmit from him, they regard their reports as weak, relying instead on the transmissions of the Medinan and Egyptian narrators. How, then, could they accept an account that contradicts Mālik’s established position in several respects, transmitted by a Khurasanian who never met him and whom hadith scholars consider weak?

Moreover, the statement,

“He is your means of approach to Allah on the Day of Resurrection, and that of your father Adam, peace be upon him,”

indicates only that Adam and his descendants will seek to draw near to Allah through him on the Day of Resurrection. This means seeking to draw near to Allah through his intercession on that Day, and this is true, as the authentic hadiths relate. On the Day of Resurrection, people will come to Adam to ask him to intercede for them. Adam will direct them to Noah, Noah to Abraham, Abraham to Moses, Moses to Jesus, and Jesus to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. For, as he said:

“I am the foremost of Adam’s descendants on the Day of Resurrection, and I say this without boasting. Adam and all those below him will be under my banner on the Day of Resurrection, and I say this without boasting.”

Nevertheless, this account contradicts Mālik’s established position in several respects:

(First: the question,

“Should I face the qiblah and supplicate, or should I face the Messenger of Allah and supplicate?”

and the reply,

“Why would you turn your face away from him, when he is your means of approach to Allah, and that of your father Adam?”

The established position of Mālik, the other imams, and the rest of the early generations—the Companions and their successors—is that, after greeting the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, anyone wishing to supplicate for himself should face the qiblah and supplicate in his mosque. He should not face the grave while supplicating for himself. Rather, he faces only

---

## Page 232

the grave when greeting the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and supplicating for him. This is the position of most scholars, including Mālik according to one of the two reports from him, al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad, and others. According to Abū Hanīfah’s followers, however, one should not face the grave even when greeting him.

Some of them said that one should keep the chamber to one’s left and greet him—a position also transmitted by Ibn Wahb from Mālik. Others said that one should instead turn one’s back to the chamber and greet him. This is their well-known position. Nevertheless, Mālik disapproved of standing at the grave for a prolonged period for this purpose.

Qādī 'Iyād said, citing Mālik’s statement in al-Mabsūt:

“I do not consider it appropriate for someone to stand at the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, supplicating. Rather, he should offer greetings and leave.”

He continued: Nāfi' said:

“Ibn 'Umar used to offer greetings at the grave. I saw him a hundred times or more come to the grave and say, ‘Peace be upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Peace be upon Abū Bakr. Peace be upon my father.’ Then he would leave. He was also seen placing his hand on the Prophet’s seat on the pulpit, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and then placing it on his face.”

He also said:

“Ibn Abī Qusayt and al-Qa'nabī reported that, when the mosque was empty, the Prophet’s Companions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would touch with their right hands the knob of the pulpit facing the grave, then turn towards the qiblah and supplicate.”

He continued:

“In al-Muwatta', as transmitted by Yahyā ibn al-Laythī, he—meaning Ibn 'Umar—would stand at the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and invoke blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and upon Abū Bakr and 'Umar. In Ibn al-Qāsim’s version, as cited by al-Marwadhī, the wording is: ‘And he would supplicate for Abū Bakr and 'Umar.’”

Mālik said, in Ibn Wahb’s transmission:

“He says, ‘Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and Allah’s mercy and blessings.’”

He also said in al-Mabsūt:

“And he offers greetings to Abū Bakr and 'Umar.”

---

## Page 233

Abū al-Walīd al-Bājī said:

“In my view, one should supplicate for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, using words that invoke blessings, and for Abū Bakr and 'Umar using words of peace, in view of the differing versions of Ibn 'Umar’s report.”

This supplication explains the supplication mentioned in Ibn Wahb’s transmission. Mālik said, as transmitted by Ibn Wahb:

“When greeting the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and supplicating, one should stand facing the grave, not the qiblah. He should draw near and offer greetings, without touching the grave.”

This, then, means greeting him and supplicating for him by invoking blessings upon him, as explained above. The same applies to every supplication mentioned by Mālik’s followers, such as those cited by Ibn Habīb in al-Wādiha and by others. He continued:

Mālik said in al-Mabsūt:

“Residents of Medina are not required to stop at the grave whenever they enter or leave the mosque; that is only for visitors from elsewhere.”

He also said in the same work:

“There is no harm in someone returning from a journey or setting out on one standing at the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, invoking blessings upon him and supplicating for him, Abū Bakr, and 'Umar.”

He was asked:

“Some residents of Medina who have neither returned from a journey nor intend to undertake one do this once or more each day. Sometimes, on Friday or on other days, they stand at the grave once, twice, or more, offering greetings and supplicating for a while.”

Mālik replied:

“I have not heard of the scholars of our town doing this. One is free to refrain from it. The later generations of this community will be set right only by what set its earliest generations right. I have not heard that the earliest generations of this community, its first members, used to do this. It is disliked except for someone who has returned from a journey or intends to undertake one.”

Ibn al-Qāsim said:

“I saw the people of Medina come to the grave and offer greetings whenever they left the city or entered it.” He said: “And for that reason he considered . . .”
(1)

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 234

Abū al-Walīd al-Bājī said:

“He thus distinguished between the residents of Medina and visitors from elsewhere, because the visitors came for that purpose, whereas the residents live there and did not come to Medina for the sake of the grave and offering greetings.”

He said:

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, do not let my grave become an idol that is worshipped.”

“Allah’s wrath was severe against people who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He said:

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“Do not make my grave an ʿīd (festival).”

He continued:

“From the book of Ahmad ibn Shuʿba: Whoever stands at the grave should not press himself against it, touch it, or remain standing beside it for long. In al-ʿUtbiyya—meaning the report from Mālik—it states:

‘In the Prophet’s mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, one should begin with prayer before offering greetings. The preferred place for voluntary prayer there is where the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to pray, beside the perfumed pillar. For obligatory prayer, however, one should move forward to the front rows.’”

He said:

“For visitors from elsewhere, I prefer voluntary prayer in that mosque to voluntary prayer in their lodgings.”

These statements of Mālik and his followers, together with what they transmitted from the Companions, show that they went to the grave only to greet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and supplicate for him. Mālik disliked standing there for a prolonged period for that purpose. He also disliked the residents of Medina doing this whenever they entered or left the mosque. Rather, this is done by visitors from elsewhere and by those returning from a journey or setting out on one, for it is a greeting to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

If, however, a person intends to supplicate for himself, he should do so in the Prophet’s mosque, facing the qiblah, as they reported from the Prophet’s Companions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It has not been transmitted that any Companion did this at the grave, or even stood there for a prolonged period supplicating for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. How much less, then, would he do so to supplicate for himself?

---

## Page 235

As for invoking the Messenger, asking him to meet one’s needs, and seeking his intercession at his grave or after his death, none of the early generations did this. It is well known that, had deliberately supplicating at the grave been prescribed, the Companions and Successors would have done so. The same applies to making requests through him; how much more, then, to invoking him and asking him directly after his death?

This shows that the statement in the account with a disconnected chain of transmission—“Face him and seek his intercession”—is falsely attributed to Mālik. It contradicts his statements and those of the Companions and Successors, as well as their practices, which Mālik and his followers observed and which the other scholars transmitted. None of them faced the grave to supplicate for himself, let alone faced it and sought his intercession, saying to him, “O Messenger of Allah, intercede for me,” or “Supplicate for me”; complained to him of afflictions concerning religious or worldly matters; asked him, other deceased prophets and righteous people, or angels whom they could not see to intercede for them; or complained to them of their afflictions.

All of this belongs to the practices of Christians and other polytheists, and of those innovators within this community who imitate them. It is not among the practices of the earliest forerunners among the Emigrants and Helpers, or of those who followed them in righteousness, nor is it anything that any of the imams of the Muslims instructed people to do. They did, however, offer greetings to him, since he heard the greetings of those nearby and the greetings of those far away were conveyed to him.

Ahmad and others cited as evidence the hadith reported by Ahmad and Abū Dāwūd with a good chain of transmission, through Haywa ibn Shurayh al-Misrī, who said: Abū Sakhr narrated to us, from Yazīd ibn Qusayt, from Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whenever anyone greets me, Allah restores my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

The imams relied on this hadith as the basis for greeting him at his grave—may Allah’s blessings

---

## Page 236

and peace be upon him—for all the hadiths concerning visiting his grave are weak, and none may be relied upon in matters of religion. This is why the compilers of the Sahīh and Sunan collections did not transmit any of them. They are transmitted only by those who narrate weak reports, such as al-Dāraqutnī, al-Bazzār, and others.

[The strongest hadith among them is that narrated by Abd Allāh ibn Umar al-Umarī—he is a weak narrator, and the report is manifestly false—such as the saying:

“Whoever visits me after my death, it is as though he visited me during my lifetime.”

This is manifestly false and contrary to the religion of the Muslims.] (*)

For whoever visited him during his lifetime while believing in him was one of his Companions, especially if he was among those who emigrated to him and fought alongside him. It is authentically established that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not revile my Companions. By Him in whose hand is my soul, if one of you spent gold equal to Mount Uhud, it would not equal a double handful given by one of them, nor even half of that.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded it in their Sahīh collections.

Someone who comes after the Companions cannot equal them even through commanded, obligatory deeds, such as Hajj, jihad, the five daily prayers, and sending blessings upon him. How, then, could he do so through an act that, by the agreement of the Muslims, is not obligatory? Indeed, travelling to his grave is not even prescribed; rather, it is prohibited.

As for travelling to his mosque to pray there, or to al-Aqsā Mosque to pray there, this is recommended, whereas travelling to the Kaaba for Hajj is obligatory. Even if someone undertook an obligatory or recommended journey, he would not equal any of the Companions who travelled to him during his lifetime. How, then, could he do so through a prohibited journey?

The imams have agreed that if someone vowed to travel to his grave—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him—or to the grave of another prophet or righteous person, he would not be obliged

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 17):
> The compiler, may Allah have mercy on him, placed the phrase “—he is weak, and the falsehood is apparent—” between two dashes, giving the impression that the Shaykh’s words “the falsehood is apparent” also refer to al-Umarī. This is incorrect: al-Umarī is a weak narrator, not a liar. By weakness, the Shaykh meant al-Umarī; by falsehood, he meant the hadith’s matn (text).

---

## Page 237

to fulfil his vow; rather, he would be prohibited from doing so.

If, however, someone vowed to travel to his mosque or to al-Aqsā Mosque to pray, al-Shāfiʿī has two opinions on the matter. The more firmly established opinion attributed to him is that fulfilling the vow is obligatory; this is also the position of Mālik and Ahmad. The second is that it is not obligatory; this is also the position of Abū Hanīfa, whose governing principle is that a vow makes binding only what is obligatory under Islamic law. Since going to these two mosques is not obligatory under Islamic law, it does not become obligatory through a vow according to him.

The majority, however, maintain that this is an act of obedience to Allah. It is authentically established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

As for travelling to visit the graves of prophets and righteous people, none of these imams holds that it becomes obligatory through a vow, because it is not an act of obedience. How, then, could someone who does this be equal to one of his Companions?

Indeed, Mālik disapproved of someone saying, “I visited the grave of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,” and regarded it as a grave matter. It has been suggested that this was akin to his disapproval of visiting graves; another explanation offered is that the visitor is superior to the one visited. Both explanations are considered weak by Mālik’s followers.

The correct explanation is that the expression “visiting the grave” is ambiguous: it includes the religiously innovated form of visitation that is of the nature of polytheism. Visiting the graves of prophets and all other believers takes two forms, as previously mentioned:

---

## Page 238

a form of visitation prescribed by Islamic law and a religiously innovated form of visitation.

The prescribed visitation is intended to convey greetings of peace to them and supplicate for them, just as one offers the funeral prayer for someone who has died. This is the prescribed form of visitation.

The second is to visit their graves as the polytheists and people of religious innovation do: to invoke the dead and ask them to fulfil one’s needs; or because one believes that supplication at the grave of one of them is better than supplication in mosques and homes; or that adjuring Allah by them and petitioning Him, glorified be He, by them is prescribed and entails one’s supplication being answered. Such visitation is a prohibited religious innovation.

If, therefore, the term “visitation” is ambiguous and admits both a true and a false meaning, it is set aside in favour of an unambiguous expression, such as “greeting him with peace.” No one could challenge Mālik on the basis of reports about visiting the Prophet’s grave or visiting him after his death, for all these hadiths are weak—indeed, fabricated—and none may be used as evidence for rulings of Islamic law.

What is authentically established from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is that he said:

“Between my house and my pulpit is a garden from the gardens of Paradise.”

This is the wording established in the Sahīh. Some, however, narrated it by meaning and said, “my grave.” When he uttered these words, he had not yet been buried; may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him. For this reason, none of the Companions cited this as evidence when they disagreed over where he should be buried. Had they known such a wording, it would have been an explicit text settling the disputed point. Rather, he was buried in ʿĀʾisha’s chamber, in the place where he died—may my father and mother be his ransom, and may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon him.

Then, when the mosque was expanded during the caliphate of al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, and his governor in Medina was

---

## Page 239

Umar ibn Abd al-Azīz, he ordered him to purchase the chambers and incorporate them into the mosque. The chambers stood on its eastern side and the side facing the qiblah (direction of prayer), and were incorporated into the mosque. From that time onward, Ā’isha’s chamber was within the mosque, and they built the outer wall with a pointed, angled shape. For it is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim, in the hadith of Abū Marthad al-Ghanawī, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not sit on graves, and do not pray facing them.”

Doing so resembles prostrating to them, even if the worshipper intends only to pray to Allah Most High. Just as taking graves as places of worship is forbidden, deliberately choosing to pray beside them is also forbidden, even if the worshipper intends only to pray to Allah, glorified be He, and supplicate to Him. Whoever seeks out the graves of the prophets and the righteous in order to pray and supplicate beside them has sought the very forbidden act whose avenues Allah and His Messenger have blocked. This differs from the prescribed greeting of peace, as explained above.

Sufyān al-Thawrī narrated from Abd Allāh ibn al-Sā’ib, from Zādhān, from Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has angels who travel throughout the earth, conveying to me the greetings of peace from my community.”

This was narrated by al-Nasā’ī and by Abū Hātim in his Sahīh. A similar report was narrated from Abū Hurayrah. This shows that greetings of peace from those far away are conveyed by the angels.

In the well-known hadith narrated by Abū al-Ash‘ath al-San‘ānī from Aws ibn Aws, he said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Send abundant blessings upon me every Friday, for the blessings sent by my community are presented to me that day. Whoever among them sends the most blessings upon me will be nearest to me in rank.”

In the Musnad of Imam Ahmad: Shurayh narrated to us: Abd Allāh ibn Nāfi‘ narrated to us, from Ibn

---

## Page 240

Abī Dhi’b, from al-Maqburī, from Abū Hurayrah, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings, and do not turn your homes into graves. Send blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your blessings reach me.”

Abū Dāwūd also narrated it.

Judge Iyād said that Abū Bakr ibn Abī Shaybah narrated from Abū Hurayrah, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sends blessings upon me at my grave, I hear him; whoever sends blessings upon me from afar, I am informed of it.”

This was narrated by Muhammad ibn Marwān al-Suddī, from al-A‘mash, from Abū Sālih, from Abū Hurayrah. This is al-Suddī the Younger, who is not a trustworthy narrator, and this is not one of al-A‘mash’s hadiths.

Abū Ya‘lā al-Mawsilī narrated in his Musnad from Mūsā ibn Muhammad ibn Hibbān, from Abū Bakr al-Hanafī, who said: Abd Allāh ibn Nāfi‘ narrated to us: al-Alā’ ibn Abd al-Rahmān narrated to us: I heard al-Hasan ibn Ali say: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Pray in your homes, and do not turn them into graves. Do not make my house a place of recurring gatherings. Send blessings and greetings of peace upon me, for your blessings and greetings of peace reach me.”

Sa‘īd ibn Mansūr narrated in his Sunan that Abd Allāh ibn Hasan ibn Hasan ibn Ali ibn Abī Tālib saw a man frequently visiting the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said to him: “O man, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings. Send blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your blessings reach me.’

Thus, in this respect, you and a man in al-Andalus are equal.”

A report conveying this meaning was also narrated from Ali ibn al-Husayn, Zayn al-Ābidīn, from his father, from Ali ibn Abī Tālib. The hadith scholar Abū Abd Allāh Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wāhid al-Maqdisī included it in his selected collection, which

---

## Page 241

is more authentic than al-Hākim’s Sahīh.

Judge Iyād related that al-Hasan ibn Alī said:

“When you enter, greet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘Do not make my house a place of recurring gatherings, and do not turn your homes into graves. Send blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your blessings reach me wherever you may be.’”

Another point that weakens this account is the statement it contains:

“Why turn your face away from him when he is your means, and the means of your father Adam, of access to Allah on the Day of Resurrection?”

This indicates only that, on the Day of Resurrection, people will seek access to Allah through his intercession. This is true, as established by mass-transmitted hadiths. Yet if people seek access to Allah through his supplication and intercession on the Day of Resurrection, just as his Companions did during his lifetime, this amounts only to asking him to supplicate and intercede. The corresponding practice—if the account were authentic—would therefore be to ask him for supplication and intercession at his grave in this world.

It is well known that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, neither commanded this nor instituted it for his community. None of the Companions or those who followed them in righteousness practised it, and none of the leading scholars of the Muslims regarded it as recommended—neither Mālik nor any other imam. How, then, can such words be attributed to Mālik, given his lofty standing, immense virtue, scholarly leadership, and wholehearted commitment to following the sunnah and condemning religious innovations and their adherents? Only an ignorant person, unfamiliar with scriptural evidence and with rulings whose scriptural proofs are known, would say such a thing. Would anyone but an innovator command or prescribe this? Even if no statement contradicting this had been transmitted from Mālik, it would still be known that he would not say such a thing.

The account then states:

“Face him and seek his intercession, and Allah will accept your intercession.”

And seeking his intercession

---

## Page 242

in its linguistic sense means asking him to intercede, just as people will seek his intercession on the Day of Resurrection and as his Companions used to seek it. An example is the hadith recorded in the Sunan:

“A Bedouin said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, we are suffering hardship, our dependants are hungry, and our wealth has perished. Supplicate to Allah for us. We seek Allah’s intercession with you, and your intercession with Allah.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, glorified Allah until its effect could be seen on his Companions’ faces, then said, ‘Woe to you! Do you know what you are saying? Allah’s majesty is far greater than that. His intercession is not sought with any of His creatures.’”

The remainder of the hadith was then related. He thus rejected the man’s words, “We seek Allah’s intercession with you.” It is well known that there is no objection to asking a created being in Allah’s name or adjuring him by Allah. What he rejected was treating Allah as an intercessor with a created being. For this reason, he did not reject the words, “We seek your intercession with Allah,” for he is the intercessor whose intercession is accepted.

If the account were authentic, they would come to him only to seek his intercession, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Hence, the account concludes by citing:

“If, when they wronged themselves, they had come to you”—and the rest of the verse.

If it were prescribed for these people to ask him, after his death, to intercede and seek forgiveness for them, then, if he answered them, he would seek forgiveness for them. His seeking forgiveness for them would be a supplication and intercession on his part that Allah forgive them.

Since seeking his intercession means asking him to intercede, the appropriate wording would be, “Seek his intercession, and Allah will accept his intercession on your behalf,” not, “Allah will accept your intercession on his behalf.”

This is ordinary usage and the language of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his Companions, and all the scholars. One says, “So-and-so interceded on behalf of so-and-so, and his intercession on that person’s behalf was accepted.” Thus, the person whose intercession is accepted by the one to whom it is addressed is the intercessor whose intercession is sought.

---

## Page 243

not the petitioner who asks someone else to intercede for him, for he is not the one who has interceded. Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the intercessor whose intercession is accepted, not the accepted intercessor whose intercession is sought.

For this reason, he says in his supplication:

“O Lord, accept my intercession.”

Allah then accepts his intercession. Thus, he asks Allah, glorified be He, to accept his own intercession, not that of those seeking his intercession. How, then, could he say, “Seek his intercession, and Allah will accept your intercession”?

Furthermore, asking him to intercede, supplicate, or seek forgiveness after his death and at his grave is not prescribed according to any of the imams of the Muslims. Nor did any of the four imams or their early disciples mention this. Only some later scholars mentioned it: they related an account from al-Utbī in which he saw a Bedouin come to the Prophet’s grave and recite this verse, and then saw in a dream that Allah had forgiven the man.

None of the authoritative jurists qualified to exercise independent legal reasoning within the schools of law—those whose opinions form the basis of fatwas issued to people—mentioned this. Those who related the account provided no evidence from the revealed law to support it. It is well known that, had asking him to supplicate, intercede, and seek forgiveness at his grave been prescribed, the Companions and those who followed them faithfully would have known this better than others and would have been the first to practise it. The imams of the Muslims would also have mentioned it. How fitting are Mālik’s words:

“Nothing will set right the latter generations of this community except what set right its earliest generations.”

He said, “No report has reached me that the earliest generations and pioneers of this community used to do this.”

How, then, could an imam such as this prescribe a religious practice that has not been transmitted from any of the early generations, and instruct the community to ask the prophets and the righteous, after their deaths and at their graves, to supplicate, intercede, and seek forgiveness—something none of the early generations of this community ever did?

---

## Page 244

However, the wording in this account resembles that of many ordinary people who use the term “intercession” to mean seeking an approach to Allah through someone. One of them says:

“O Allah, we seek intercession before You through so-and-so and so-and-so.”

He means that he seeks an approach to Allah through that person. Of someone who invokes a prophet or another person as a means in his supplication, they say, “He has sought intercession through him,” even though the person whose intercession is sought has neither interceded nor supplicated for him. Indeed, that person may be absent, having neither heard his words nor interceded for him. This is not the usage of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his Companions, or the scholars of the community; indeed, it is not even Arabic usage. Seeking intercession means requesting intercession. An intercessor joins the petitioner in his plea, requesting on his behalf what he seeks from the one being asked and supplicated, to whom the intercession is addressed.

As for seeking intercession through someone who has neither interceded for the petitioner nor requested that his need be fulfilled—and who may not even know of his request—this is not seeking intercession, either in Arabic usage or in the speech of anyone who understands what he is saying. Certainly, this is asking by means of him; such supplication is not seeking his intercession. Yet when these people altered the language—as they altered the revealed law—and called this “seeking intercession,” meaning asking by means of the intercessor, they began saying:

“Seek his intercession, and Allah will accept your intercession.”

They mean that He will answer your request made by means of him. This is among the indications that this account was fabricated by someone ignorant of both the revealed law and the Arabic language, and that its wording is not Mālik’s.

Nevertheless, the account may have an authentic basis: Mālik may have forbidden raising one’s voice in the Messenger’s mosque in accordance with the Sunnah, just as Umar used to forbid raising one’s voice in his mosque. Mālik may also have enjoined what Allah commanded concerning supporting and honouring the Messenger, and other such things that it would be fitting for Mālik to enjoin.

---

## Page 245

Whoever does not understand the language in which the Companions spoke to one another and in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed them, nor their customary manner of speaking, will distort words from their proper meanings. Many people grow up accustomed to their community’s terminology and use of words. When they then encounter those same words in the speech of Allah, His Messenger, or the Companions, they assume that Allah, His Messenger, or the Companions mean what those words mean within their own customary usage and terminology, whereas the intended meaning is different.

This occurs among various groups: speculative theologians, jurists, grammarians, ordinary people, and others. Others deliberately assign the words of the prophets and their followers meanings contrary to those they intended. They then use those words with their own meanings in mind and say:

“We agree with the prophets.”

This is found in the writings of many heretical philosophers and Ismā'īlīs, and of those heretics among speculative theologians and Sufis who resemble them. An example is someone who applies the terms “originated,” “created,” and “made” to whatever is an effect of a cause, even though he considers it eternal and without beginning. He calls this “intrinsic origination,” then says:

“We say that the world is originated.”

This is what he means by it. Yet it is well known that no people use the word “originated” in this sense. In their usage, something originated is only that which comes into existence after not existing.

Likewise, they apply the word “angels” to the intellects, souls, and faculties of the soul whose existence they affirm, and the words “jinn” and “devils” to certain faculties of the soul. They then say:

“We affirm the existence of the angels, jinn, and devils reported by the prophets and acknowledged by most people.”

---

## Page 246

Whoever understands what the prophets mean and what these people mean necessarily recognizes that the two are not the same. For example, he understands what they mean by the First Intellect: that, in their view, it coexists with the Lord of the worlds eternally, without beginning or end, and that it originates everything besides itself, or that everything besides it comes into being through its mediation. In their view, everything beneath the lunar sphere proceeds from the Active Intellect. Yet it is necessarily known from the religion of the prophets that none of the angels, as the prophets understand them, is the lord of everything besides Allah, or the lord of everything beneath the lunar sphere. Nor is any angel beginningless and everlasting, having always existed and never ceasing to exist.

It is also known that the report attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

“The first thing Allah created was the intellect”—

is a false hadith. Even if it were true, however, it would constitute evidence against them. Its wording is:

“When Allah first created the intellect”—

with awwal (first) in the accusative, functioning as an adverb of time:

“He said to it, ‘Come forward,’ and it came forward. Then He said to it, ‘Turn back,’ and it turned back. He then said, ‘By My might, I have created nothing dearer to Me than you. Through you I take, and through you I give; through you comes reward, and through you comes punishment.’”

Another version reads:

“When Allah created the intellect.”

Thus, if the hadith were established as authentic, its meaning would be that He addressed the intellect at the very beginning of its creation, that it was created before anything else, and that these four matters occur through it—not that all created things do.

In Muslim usage, the word 'aql (intellect) is the verbal noun of 'aqala (he reasoned), ya'qilu (he reasons): 'aqlan (reasoning). It denotes the faculty through which one reasons, as well as the knowledge and actions resulting from it. In no linguistic usage does it ever denote a self-subsisting substance. This meaning therefore cannot be intended by the word “intellect.”

Moreover, elsewhere we have demonstrated, on the basis of sound reason, the invalidity of their claims. Their assertions concerning immaterial entities and entities separate from matter ultimately amount to affirming the soul that separates from the body at death, and affirming the intelligible concepts that the soul abstracts and that subsist within it. This is the full extent of the truth they establish in this matter.

---

## Page 247

The point here is that those who follow their path use many expressions from the speech of Allah and His Messenger, intending by them what these people intend, not what Allah and His Messenger intend. This occurs in the writings of the author of al-Kutub al-Madnūn bihā (The Withheld Books) and others. Examples include his discussion of the “Preserved Tablet,” which he identifies with the celestial soul; the “Pen,” which he identifies with the First Intellect; and the terms malakūt, jabarūt, and mulk, which he takes to refer to the soul and the intellect. He likewise interprets “intercession” as an emanation flowing from the intercessor to the person seeking intercession, even though the intercessor may be unaware of it. In these and similar matters, he follows the approaches of Ibn Sīnā, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

The point here, however, is to discuss those who fall into this without carefully considering the language of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. One example is the term qadīm (old). In the language of the Messenger, in which the Quran was revealed, it means the opposite of new, even if something else preceded it. Thus Allah Most High says:

“Until it returns like an old date stalk.”

Allah Most High also relates the words of Joseph’s brothers:

“By Allah, you are indeed in your old error.”

And He says:

“Have you considered what you have been worshipping—you and your ancestors of old?”

Among the speculative theologians, however, qadīm denotes that which has always existed, or that which was not preceded by the existence of anything else, provided that it was not preceded by its own nonexistence. When this is what is meant by it, they classify its use as figurative. In the language of the Quran, the term muhdath (new) stands in contrast to the Quranic term qadīm (old).

Likewise, in the Quran, hadith, and Arabic usage generally, kalima (word) denotes a complete sentence, as in the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Two utterances are beloved to the Most Merciful,

---

## Page 248

light on the tongue and heavy on the scales: ‘Glory be to Allah and praise be to Him’ and ‘Glory be to Allah, the Magnificent.’” And his saying:

“The truest utterance spoken by a poet is the saying of Labīd: ‘Indeed, everything other than Allah is vain.’”

Another example is the saying of Allah Most High:

“Grave is the word that comes out of their mouths; they speak nothing but a lie.”

And His saying:

“Say, ‘O People of the Scripture, come to a common word between us and you,’”

and the remainder of the verse. And His saying:

“He made the word of those who disbelieved the lowest, while the word of Allah is the highest.”

There are other similar examples. In Arabic usage, the term kalām (speech) occurs only in this sense.

Grammarians, however, have adopted the convention of calling a noun on its own, a verb, or a particle a “word.” Some of them then say, “A ‘word’ may also mean complete speech.” Someone accustomed to this supposes that this is how the Arabs use the term.

Likewise, the expression “blood relatives” in the Quran and Sunnah means relatives on both parents’ sides. It therefore includes residuary heirs and heirs entitled to prescribed shares, although it also encompasses those who inherit neither a prescribed share nor as residuary heirs. In the terminology of jurists, however, it came to designate this last group alone. Someone familiar only with this usage therefore supposes that this is what the expression means in the speech of Allah and His Messenger and in the speech of the Companions. There are many similar examples.

The meanings of the expressions tawassul (seeking a means of approach), istishfā‘ (seeking intercession), and others like them have undergone changes from the usage of the Messenger and his Companions. These changes have caused people to misunderstand their religion and their language.

Knowledge requires reliable transmission and sound reasoning. Reports transmitted from the early generations and the scholars require us to establish the authenticity of their wording and understand what it signifies, just as reports transmitted from Allah and His Messenger require this. This concludes what pertains to this account.

---

## Page 249

The texts of the Quran and Sunnah consistently affirm that Allah has commanded us to invoke blessings and peace upon the Prophet wherever we may be. This is a matter on which Muslims agree. Likewise, in the authentic hadith, he encouraged and urged us to ask Allah to grant him al-wasīla and distinction, and to raise him to the praised station He has promised him. This al-wasīla, which we have been instructed to ask Allah Most High to grant him—just as we have been instructed to invoke blessings and peace upon him—is his right, just as invoking blessings and peace upon him is his right, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The means of nearness that Allah has commanded us to seek towards Him is drawing near to Allah through obedience to Him. This encompasses everything Allah and His Messenger have commanded us to do. We have no way of attaining this means of nearness except by following the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through faith in him and obedience to him. Seeking nearness through him in this sense is obligatory upon everyone.

As for seeking nearness through his supplication and intercession—as people will ask him on the Day of Resurrection to intercede for them, and as the Companions used to seek nearness through his intercession when seeking rain and on other occasions, such as the blind man’s seeking nearness through his supplication until Allah restored his sight through the Prophet’s supplication and intercession—this is a third type. It falls under Allah’s acceptance of his supplication and intercession because of the honour in which He holds him. Someone for whom the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intercedes and supplicates is therefore unlike someone for whom he neither supplicates nor intercedes.

Some people, however, supposed that the Companions’ seeking nearness through him meant that they adjured Allah by him and asked Allah by him. They therefore supposed that this was prescribed without qualification for everyone, during his lifetime and after his death, and they supposed

---

## Page 250

that this was prescribed with respect to prophets and angels, and even righteous people and those thought to be righteous, even if they were not righteous in reality. Yet no hadith traced back to the Prophet concerning this is found in any of the Muslim collections relied upon for hadith—neither in the two Sahīh collections, nor in the Sunan collections, nor in authoritative Musnad collections such as the Musnad of Imam Ahmad and others. Such reports are found only in books known to contain many fabricated, falsely attributed hadiths invented by liars. These liars are distinct from those who may make mistakes in transmitting hadith without deliberately lying: reports from the latter are found in the Sunan collections, the Musnad of Imam Ahmad, and similar works. As for those who deliberately lie, Ahmad did not transmit from any of them in his Musnad.

For this reason, the hadith master Abū al-‘Alā’ al-Hamdānī and Shaykh Abū al-Faraj Ibn al-Jawzī disagreed over the question: Does the Musnad contain any fabricated hadith? Abū al-‘Alā’ denied that it contained any fabricated hadith, while Abū al-Faraj affirmed that it did, explaining that it contained hadiths known to be false. There is no contradiction between the two positions. In Abū al-Faraj’s terminology, a “fabricated” hadith is one for which there is evidence establishing that it is false, even if its transmitter did not deliberately lie but merely made a mistake. For this reason, he included many hadiths of this kind in his book on fabricated hadiths.

A number of scholars disputed many of the reports he included, saying that there was no evidence establishing their falsity. Indeed, they demonstrated the authenticity of some of them. Nevertheless, most of what he included in his book on fabricated hadiths is false by scholarly agreement.

---

## Page 251

As for the hadith master Abū al-‘Alā’ and others like him, by “fabricated” they mean only a report deliberately invented by someone who knowingly lied. Lying was rare among the early generations.

As for the Companions, none of them is known—praise be to Allah—to have deliberately attributed a lie to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Likewise, none is known to have followed the well-known religious innovations, such as those of the Khawārij, Rāfidah, Qadariyyah, and Murji’ah. No Companion is known to have belonged to any of these sects.

Nor was there anyone among them who claimed that al-Khidr had visited him. The al-Khidr who accompanied Moses died, as has been explained elsewhere. The “al-Khidr” who visits many people is merely a jinn who has assumed human form, or a human liar. He cannot be an angel while claiming, “I am al-Khidr,” for angels do not lie; it is jinn and humans who lie. I know of cases in which people were visited by “al-Khidr,” who was in fact a jinn; recounting them here would take too long. The Companions were too knowledgeable to be taken in by such deception.

Likewise, none of them was carried by jinn to Mecca and taken to ‘Arafāt to perform the standing there, as jinn have done with many ignorant people, devout worshippers, and others. Nor was there anyone among them to whom jinn brought people’s wealth and food that they had stolen, leading him to suppose that this was a miraculous favor. These matters have been discussed at length elsewhere.

As for the Successors, deliberate lying was not known among those of Mecca, Medina, Syria, and Basra, unlike the Shī‘ah, among whom lying is known. After these generations, lying became known among various groups.

---

## Page 252

As for error, most people are not free of it. Indeed, some Companions occasionally made mistakes, as did some of those who came after them. Thus, collections of authentic hadith contain some reports known to be erroneous, although the majority of the hadith texts in the two Sahīh collections are known to be true. The hadith master Abū al-‘Alā’ knows that these reports are erroneous. Imam Ahmad himself explained this and stated that he transmitted them so that they could be recognized, unlike reports whose narrators deliberately lied.

For this reason, Ahmad kept his Musnad free of reports from certain narrators whose reports are transmitted by compilers of the Sunan, such as Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī. An example is the mashyakha of Kathīr ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr ibn ‘Awf al-Muzanī, transmitted from his father, from his grandfather. Although Abū Dāwūd includes some of these reports in his Sunan, Ahmad’s criteria for his Musnad are better than Abū Dāwūd’s criteria for his Sunan.

The point is that the hadiths transmitted on this subject are like other singular and rejected reports—indeed, fabricated reports—transmitted by those who indiscriminately collect the good and the bad concerning merits and distinctions. Similar material is found in works on the merits of particular times, acts of worship, prophets and Companions, places, and the like. These subjects contain authentic hadiths, sound hadiths, weak hadiths, and fabricated lies.

It is not permissible to rely in matters of the Sharia on weak hadiths that are neither authentic nor sound. However, Ahmad ibn Hanbal and other scholars permitted the transmission, concerning the merits of deeds, of reports not known to be established, provided they are not known to be lies.

---

## Page 253

This is because, when an act is known to be prescribed on the basis of evidence from the Sharia, and a hadith concerning its merit is transmitted that is not known to be a lie, the reward mentioned may indeed be real. None of the imams said that something may be declared obligatory or recommended on the basis of a weak hadith. Whoever says this has contradicted the consensus.

Likewise, nothing may be declared forbidden without evidence from the Sharia. However, when something is known to be forbidden, and a hadith threatening its perpetrator with punishment is transmitted that is not known to be a lie, it may be narrated. Thus, reports not known to be lies may be narrated to encourage good and warn against evil—but only concerning matters that Allah is known to have encouraged or warned against on the basis of evidence other than this hadith whose status is unknown.

The same applies to reports from the Israelite tradition. Those not known to be lies may be narrated to encourage good and warn against evil concerning matters that Allah Most High is known to have commanded or forbidden in our Sharia. As for establishing a rule of our Sharia solely on the basis of unverified reports from the Israelite tradition, no scholar advocates this. Neither Ahmad ibn Hanbal nor other imams like him relied on such hadiths in matters of the Sharia.

Anyone who reports that Ahmad used weak hadiths that were neither authentic nor sound as evidence has misrepresented him. Rather, in the terminology of Ahmad ibn Hanbal and the scholars before him, hadiths were divided into two categories: authentic and weak. They further divided weak reports into those that were abandoned and could not be used as evidence, and those that were weak yet sound. Similarly, weakness caused by illness may take the form of a potentially fatal illness, which precludes unrestricted gifts from one’s estate, or mild weakness, which does not prevent this.

---

## Page 254

The first person known to have divided hadiths into three categories—authentic, sound, and weak—was Abū ‘Īsā al-Tirmidhī in his Jāmi‘. In his terminology, a sound hadith is one transmitted through multiple chains, whose narrators include no one under suspicion, and which is not anomalous. Ahmad calls hadiths of this kind weak, yet uses them as evidence. Thus, Ahmad illustrated the category of weak hadiths admissible as evidence with the reports of ‘Amr ibn Shu‘ayb, Ibrāhīm al-Hajarī, and others like them. This is discussed at length elsewhere.

The hadiths narrated on this subject—namely, supplicating by invoking created beings themselves—are feeble, weak reports, indeed fabricated ones. None of the imams of Islam used them as evidence or relied upon them. One example is the hadith narrated from ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Hārūn ibn ‘Antarah, from his father, from his grandfather:

“Abū Bakr al-Siddīq came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘I am learning the Quran, but it slips from my memory.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him, ‘Say: O Allah, I ask You by Muhammad, Your Prophet; by Abraham, Your intimate friend; by Moses, with whom You spoke in confidence; by Jesus, Your spirit and Your word; by the Torah of Moses, the Gospel of Jesus, the Psalms of David, and the Criterion of Muhammad; and by every revelation You have given and every decree You have decreed.’”

He then related the remainder of the hadith. Razīn ibn Mu‘āwiyah al-‘Abdarī included this hadith in his Jāmi‘, and Ibn al-Athīr reproduced it in Jāmi‘ al-Usūl. Neither attributed it to any book by Muslim scholars. It was, however, narrated by those who compiled works on daily and nightly practices, such as Ibn al-Sunnī and Abū Nu‘aym. Books of this kind contain many fabricated hadiths which, by scholarly consensus, may not be relied upon in matters of the Sharia. Shaykh al-Asbahānī also narrated it in his book Fadā’il al-A‘māl (The Merits of Deeds), and in this

---

## Page 255

book there are many false, fabricated hadiths.

Abū Mūsā al-Madīnī also narrated it through Zayd ibn al-Hubāb, from ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Hārūn ibn ‘Antarah, and said, “This is a sound hadith, although its chain is not continuous.” Abū Mūsā said:

“Muhriz ibn Hishām also narrated it from ‘Abd al-Malik, from his father, from his grandfather, from al-Siddīq, may Allah be pleased with him. ‘Abd al-Malik is not particularly strong as a narrator. He lived in Rayy, and his father and grandfather are both trustworthy.”

I say: ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Hārūn ibn ‘Antarah is among those known for lying. Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn said, “He is a liar.” Al-Sa‘dī said, “An impostor and a liar.” Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān said, “He fabricates hadiths.” Al-Nasā’ī said, “His narrations are abandoned.” Al-Bukhārī said, “His hadiths are rejected.” Ahmad ibn Hanbal said, “He is weak.” Ibn ‘Adī said, “He narrates hadiths in which no other narrator corroborates him.” Al-Dāraqutnī said, “Both he and his father are weak.” Al-Hākim said in al-Madkhal (The Introduction): “‘Abd al-Malik ibn Hārūn ibn ‘Antarah al-Shaybānī narrated fabricated hadiths from his father.”

Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī included this hadith in al-Mawdū‘āt (Fabricated Hadiths). When the hadith scholar Abū Mūsā says, “It is disconnected,” he means that even if its narrators were trustworthy, its chain would still be disconnected.

‘Abd al-Malik also narrated those other related hadiths concerning the People of the Book seeking victory through him, as will be discussed later. In doing so, he contradicted the general body of reports transmitted by Quran commentators and scholars of prophetic biography, as well as what the Quran indicates. This supports the scholars’ assessment that his narrations are abandoned, whether because he deliberately lied or because of his poor memory. It is thus clear that neither this report nor those reports provide any basis for proof.

Similar to this is the hadith narrated by ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam, from his father

---

## Page 256

from his grandfather, from ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, both as a report traced back to the Prophet and as one attributed to ‘Umar himself:

“When Adam committed the sin, he said, ‘My Lord, I ask You, by the right of Muhammad, to forgive me.’ He said, ‘How did you know of Muhammad?’ Adam replied, ‘When You created me with Your hand and breathed into me of Your spirit, I raised my head and saw written on the supports of the Throne: “There is no god but Allah; Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.” I thus knew that You would place beside Your name only the one most beloved to You among Your creation.’ He said, ‘You have spoken the truth, Adam. Were it not for Muhammad, I would not have created you.’”

Al-Hākim narrated this hadith in his al-Mustadrak (The Supplement), through ‘Abd Allāh ibn Muslim al-‘Awfī, from Ismā‘īl ibn Salamah, from him. Al-Hākim said, “This is the first hadith of ‘Abd al-Rahmān that I have mentioned in this book.” Al-Hākim also said, “It is sound.”

Shaykh Abū Bakr al-Ājurrī narrated it in Kitāb al-Sharī‘ah (The Book of Divine Law) as a report attributed to ‘Umar, through ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Maryam, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam, without tracing it back to the Prophet. Al-Ājurrī also narrated it through another chain, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī al-Zinād, from his father, with the attribution stopping at him. He said:

“Hārūn ibn Yūsuf al-Tājir narrated to us; Abū Marwān al-‘Uthmānī narrated to us; Abū ‘Uthmān ibn Khālid narrated to me, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī al-Zinād, from his father, who said: ‘Among the words through which Allah accepted Adam’s repentance were his words: “O Allah, I ask You by Muhammad’s right upon You.” Allah Most High said, “What do you know of Muhammad?” He replied, “My Lord, I raised my head and saw written upon Your Throne: ‘There is no god but Allah; Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.’ I thus knew that he was the most honoured of Your creation.”’”

I say: Al-Hākim’s narration of this hadith is among the things for which he was criticized, for he himself said in Kitāb al-Madkhal ilā Ma‘rifat al-Sahīh min al-Saqīm (Introduction to Distinguishing Sound from Unsound Reports): “‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam

---

## Page 257

narrated fabricated hadiths from his father; any hadith specialist who examines them will readily see that the responsibility for them lies with him.”

I say: ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam is weak by their unanimous agreement and makes frequent mistakes. Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Abū Zur‘ah, Abū Hātim, al-Nasā’ī, al-Dāraqutnī and others declared him weak. Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān said: “He would unknowingly distort reports, until such errors became frequent in his narrations: presenting disconnected reports as fully traced back to the Prophet and attributing reports that stopped at Companions to the Prophet. He therefore deserved to have his narrations abandoned.”

As for al-Hākim’s declaring this hadith and others like it sound, this is among the things for which the leading hadith scholars criticized him. They noted that al-Hākim declares hadiths sound which hadith experts regard as fabricated lies. For example, he declared sound the hadith of Zarīb ibn Barthamlī, which mentions the Messiah’s appointed trustee. The experts unanimously agree that it is false, as al-Bayhaqī, Ibn al-Jawzī and others have explained. Likewise, he declares many hadiths in his al-Mustadrak sound although the leading hadith scholars regard them as fabricated. Some are reports whose attribution stops at a Companion, yet he traces them back to the Prophet.

For this reason, hadith scholars do not rely merely on al-Hākim’s declaration that a hadith is sound, even though most of the reports he declares sound are indeed sound. Among scholars who authenticate hadiths, he is like a trustworthy narrator who makes frequent mistakes, although he is more often correct than mistaken. No scholar who authenticates hadiths has weaker judgments of soundness than his. This is unlike Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān al-Bustī, whose judgments of soundness are superior to al-Hākim’s and carry greater weight. The same applies to the judgments of al-Tirmidhī, al-Dāraqutnī, Ibn Khuzaymah, Ibn Mandah and others like them among scholars who authenticate hadiths.

---

## Page 258

For although some of what these scholars transmit is disputed, they are more proficient in this field than al-Hākim. Yet none of their judgments of soundness carries the same weight as Muslim’s, nor do Muslim’s carry the same weight as al-Bukhārī’s. Indeed, al-Bukhārī’s book is the finest work compiled in this field. Al-Bukhārī was among the most knowledgeable of Allah’s creation concerning hadith and their defects, and he also possessed a profound understanding of them. Al-Tirmidhī stated that he had never seen anyone more knowledgeable about hadith defects than al-Bukhārī.

For this reason, whenever al-Bukhārī narrated a hadith whose chain of transmission or some of whose wording was disputed, his practice was to mention that disagreement, lest anyone be misled by his inclusion of the report: he included it only alongside an explanation of the disagreement concerning it. Thus, in most cases where his authentication of a hadith was challenged, his position was stronger than that of his critics.

This was unlike Muslim ibn al-Hajjāj, whose inclusion of several hadiths was challenged, with his critics being correct in those cases. For example, in the reports concerning the eclipse prayer, he narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed with three bowings and with four bowings, just as he narrated that he prayed with two bowings. The correct view is that he prayed only with two bowings, and that he performed the eclipse prayer only once, on the day Ibrāhīm died. Al-Shāfi‘ī explained this, and it is also the position of al-Bukhārī and of Ahmad ibn Hanbal according to one of the two narrations from him. The hadiths mentioning three and four bowings also state that he performed this prayer on the day Ibrāhīm died. It is well known that Ibrāhīm did not die on two different days of eclipse, nor did the Prophet have two sons named Ibrāhīm. Anyone who reports that he died on the tenth day of the month has lied.

Likewise, Muslim narrated:

“Allah created the soil on Saturday.”

Scholars more knowledgeable than he, such as Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn, al-Bukhārī and others, challenged him concerning this report. They explained that this was an error and that these were not the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 259

And the evidence is on their side, for it is established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus that Allah Most High created the heavens and the earth in six days, that the last of His creation was Adam, and that Adam was created on Friday. This disputed hadith, however, implies that He created all this in seven days. It has also been narrated through a sounder chain of transmission that creation began on Sunday.

Likewise, Muslim narrated that, when Abū Sufyān embraced Islam, he asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to marry Umm Habībah and appoint Mu‘āwiyah as a scribe. A number of hadith masters judged him mistaken in this. Nevertheless, the great majority of the hadith texts in the two Sahīh collections are agreed upon by the leading hadith scholars. They have accepted them and reached consensus concerning them, and they know with absolute certainty that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, uttered them. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

A number of compilers cite this aforementioned hadith concerning Adam without a chain of transmission, along with similar reports containing further additions. Qādī ‘Iyād, for example, wrote:

Abū Muhammad al-Makkī, Abū al-Layth al-Samarqandī, and others related:

“When Adam disobeyed, he said, ‘O Allah, by the right of Muhammad, forgive my sin’—he said: another version reads, ‘Accept my repentance.’ Allah then said to him, ‘How did you come to know of Muhammad?’ He replied, ‘I saw written everywhere in Paradise: “There is no god but Allah; Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah”’—he said: another version reads, ‘Muhammad is My servant and My Messenger’—‘so I knew that he was the most honored of Your creation in Your sight.’ Then He accepted his repentance and forgave him.”

By the consensus of the Muslims, reports such as this may not serve as a foundation for the Sharia or be cited as evidence in religion. This belongs to the category of Isrā’īliyyāt and similar reports, whose authenticity can only be established through transmission

---

## Page 260

soundly traced back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Even if these reports were transmitted by someone like Ka‘b al-Ahbār, Wahb ibn Munabbih, or others who relate accounts of origins and stories of earlier peoples from the People of the Book, they could not, by the consensus of the Muslims, be cited as evidence in the Muslims’ religion. How much more so when their transmitter relates them neither from the People of the Book nor from trustworthy Muslim scholars? Rather, he relates them only from someone whom the Muslims regard as an impugned, weak narrator whose hadith cannot serve as evidence. His transmission of these reports is so inconsistent that it is clear he did not retain them accurately. No trustworthy Muslim scholar whose transmission can be relied upon relates this or anything like it. These reports are merely of the kind transmitted by Ishāq ibn Bishr and others like him in books concerning origins.

Even if these reports were authentically established from the prophets, they would constitute religious law for those prophets. Citing them as evidence would then depend on whether the religious law of those before us is also binding upon us. The disagreement over this is well known. However, the position of the leading imams and most scholars is that it is binding upon us unless our own religious law provides otherwise. This applies only to what has been established as the religious law of those before us through a sound report from our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or through mass transmission from them—not to reports transmitted in this manner. No Muslim may cite such reports as evidence in Islamic law.

Another example of this is a hadith cited by Mūsā ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-San‘ānī, the author of the Quran commentary, through his chain of transmission from Ibn ‘Abbās, attributing the following words to the Prophet:

“Whoever wishes Allah to enable him to retain the Quran and the various branches of knowledge should write this supplication in a clean vessel or on glass sheets, using honey, saffron, and rainwater, and drink it on an empty stomach. He should fast for three days and break his fast with it, and recite this supplication after his prayers: ‘O Allah, I ask You, for You are One who is asked, and none has been asked

---

## Page 261

like You, nor will anyone like You ever be asked. And I ask You by the right of Muhammad, Your Prophet; Abraham, Your close friend; Moses, Your confidant; and Jesus, Your spirit and Your word, honoured in Your sight.’” He then cited the remainder of the supplication.

This Mūsā ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān is among the liars. Abū Ahmad ibn ‘Adī said of him:

“He narrates rejected hadith.”

Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān said:

“He is an impostor who fabricates hadith. He fabricated a book of Quran commentary, falsely attributing it to Ibn Jurayj, from ‘Atā’, from Ibn ‘Abbās, compiling it from the statements of al-Kalbī and Muqātil.”

Something similar—without the fasting—is transmitted from Ibn Mas‘ūd through Mūsā ibn Ibrāhīm al-Marwazī, who said: “Wakī‘ narrated to us, from ‘Ubayda, from Shaqīq, from Ibn Mas‘ūd.” Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn said of this Mūsā ibn Ibrāhīm: “A liar.” Al-Dāraqutnī said: “His narrations are abandoned.” Ibn Hibbān said: “He was heedless: he would accept what was prompted to him, and thus deserved to have his narrations abandoned.” This is also transmitted from ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, from Mujāhid ibn Jabr, from Ibn Mas‘ūd, through a chain weaker than the first.

Abū al-Shaykh al-Asbahānī transmitted it through Ahmad ibn Ishāq al-Jawharī, who said: “Abū al-Ash‘ath narrated to us; Zuhayr ibn al-‘Alā’ al-‘Utbī narrated to us; Yūsuf ibn Yazīd narrated to us, from al-Zuhrī.” He traced the hadith back to the Prophet, who was reported to have said:

“Whoever wishes to retain what he learns should fast for seven days, and let him break his fast at the end of the seven days with these words.”

I say: These chains of transmission are obscure; nothing can be established through them. Abū Mūsā al-Madīnī transmitted it in his dictated collections, as did Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Maqdisī, following the practice of scholars like them who transmit whatever is reported on a subject, whether authentic or weak. This is also the customary practice of most later hadith scholars: they transmit reports concerning virtues and place responsibility

---

## Page 262

for these reports on the transmitter, as is the customary practice of those who compile works on the merits of particular times, places, people, and acts of worship. Such are the reports transmitted by Abū al-Shaykh al-Asbahānī in Fadā’il al-A‘māl (The Merits of Deeds) and other works. Drawing on his extensive body of transmitted material, he gathers numerous hadith, including many strong reports—authentic and good—as well as many weak, fabricated, and feeble reports.

The same applies to what Khaythama ibn Sulaymān transmits concerning the merits of the Companions; what Abū Nu‘aym al-Asbahānī transmits in Fadā’il al-Khulafā’ (The Merits of the Caliphs), a separate work, and at the beginning of Hilyat al-Awliyā’ (The Adornment of Allah’s Friends); and what Abū al-Layth al-Samarqandī, ‘Abd al-‘Azīz al-Kinānī, Abū ‘Alī ibn al-Bannā’, and similar scholars transmit. It also applies to what Abū Bakr al-Khatīb, Abū al-Fadl ibn Nāsir, Abū Mūsā al-Madīnī, Abū al-Qāsim ibn ‘Asākir, the hadith master ‘Abd al-Ghanī, and others with knowledge of hadith transmit. In their writings, they frequently transmit whatever has been reported, without restriction, following their usual practice.
