Their purpose is to make known what has been transmitted on a given subject, not to establish every transmitted report as evidence. One of them may comment on a hadith, describing it as singular, rejected, or weak; or he may leave it without comment.

This differs from the practice of the leading hadith scholars who use hadith as evidence and base their religion upon it, such as Mālik ibn Anas, Shu‘ba ibn al-Hajjāj, Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Qattān, ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī, Sufyān ibn ‘Uyayna, ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, Wakī‘ ibn al-Jarrāh, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, ‘Alī ibn al-Madīnī, al-Bukhārī, Abū Zur‘a, Abū Hātim, Abū Dāwūd, Muhammad ibn Nasr al-Marwazī, Ibn Khuzayma, Ibn al-Mundhir, Dāwūd ibn ‘Alī, Muhammad ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī, and others. For these are the scholars who

---

## Page 263

base rulings on hadith and therefore need to strive to identify which are authentic and which are weak, and to assess their transmitters. The same applies to those who critically examine hadith and their transmitters, making the distinctions necessary for assessing hadith, as do Abū Ahmad ibn ʿAdī, Abū Hātim al-Bustī, Abū al-Hasan al-Dāraqutnī, and Abū Bakr al-Ismāʿīlī, and as Abū Bakr al-Bayhaqī, Abū Ismāʿīl al-Ansārī, Abū al-Qāsim al-Zanjānī, Abū ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Barr, Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm, and others like them may also do. A detailed discussion of these matters belongs elsewhere. We have not mentioned those who transmit without chains of transmission—as in Wasīlat al-Mutaʿabbidīn (The Devout Worshippers’ Means) by ʿUmar al-Mallā al-Mawsilī, al-Firdaws (Paradise) by Shahriyār al-Daylamī, and similar works—for they rank below these groups, and what they relate contains a great deal of falsehood.

The point here is that, according to the consensus of those versed in the Prophet’s hadith, there is not a single hadith on this subject traced back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that can be relied upon in a matter of Islamic law. Rather, those versed in hadith recognize the reports transmitted on this subject as fabrications, whether produced deliberately by their originators or through error. There are also reports from the early generations on this subject, most of which are weak.

Among them is the account of the four men who gathered at the Kaaba and made supplications: ʿAbd Allāh and Musʿab, the two sons of al-Zubayr; ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar; and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. Ibn Abī al-Dunyā mentioned it in Mujābī al-Duʿāʾ (Those Whose Supplications Were Answered), transmitting it through Ismāʿīl ibn Abān al-Ghanawī

---

## Page 264

from Sufyān al-Thawrī, from Tāriq ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, from al-Shaʿbī, who said:

“I witnessed something remarkable. We were in the courtyard of the Kaaba: I, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, Musʿab ibn al-Zubayr, and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. When they had finished their conversation, they said: ‘Let each of you rise, take hold of the Yemeni Corner, and ask Allah for what he needs, for he will be granted abundantly.’

“Then they said: ‘Rise, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, for you were the first child born in Islam after the Hijrah.’ He rose, took hold of the Yemeni Corner, and said: ‘O Allah, You are indeed great, and it is in You that hope is placed in every great matter. I ask You by the sanctity of Your Face, the sanctity of Your Throne, and the sanctity of Your Prophet not to let me die before You grant me rule over the Hijaz and I am saluted as caliph.’ Then he returned and sat down.

“Then Musʿab rose, took hold of the Yemeni Corner, and said: ‘O Allah, You are the Lord of all things, and to You all things return. I ask You by Your power over all things not to let me die before You grant me rule over Iraq and grant me marriage to Sukayna bint al-Husayn.’

“Then ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān rose, took hold of the Yemeni Corner, and said: ‘O Allah, Lord of the seven heavens and Lord of the earth that bears vegetation after being barren, I ask You by that with which Your servants who obey Your command have petitioned You. I ask You by Your right over Your creation and by the right of those who circle Your Throne’”—and so on.

I say: Ismāʿīl ibn Abān, who narrated this from Sufyān al-Thawrī, is a liar. Ahmad ibn Hanbal said: “I recorded hadith from him, but then he narrated fabricated hadith, so we abandoned him.”

Yahyā ibn Maʿīn said: “He fabricated a hadith about the seventh descendant of al-ʿAbbās wearing green”—meaning al-Maʾmūn.

---

## Page 265

And al-Bukhārī, Muslim, Abū Zur‘a, and al-Dāraqutnī said: “His narrations are abandoned.” Al-Jūzajānī said: “He was found to be lying.” Abū Hātim said: “A liar.” Ibn Hibbān said: “He fabricates reports and attributes them to trustworthy narrators.”

The identity of Tāriq ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, from whom al-Thawrī is said to have narrated, is unknown. He said: “For the known Tāriq ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, from whom Ibn ‘Ajlān narrated, does not belong to this generation of narrators.”

A different version of this account was also transmitted. Abū Nu‘aym narrated it from al-Tabarānī: “Ahmad ibn Zayd ibn al-Jarīsh narrated to us; Abū Hātim al-Sijistānī narrated to us; al-Asma‘ī narrated to us, saying: ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī al-Zinād narrated to us, from his father, who said:

“Mus‘ab, ‘Urwa, and ‘Abd Allāh, the sons of al-Zubayr, gathered in the Hijr enclosure with ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar. They said: ‘Express your wishes.’

“‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr said: ‘As for me, I wish for the caliphate.’ ‘Urwa said: ‘As for me, I wish that knowledge be transmitted from me.’ Mus‘ab said: ‘As for me, I wish to govern Iraq and to be married to both ‘Ā’isha bint Talha and Sukayna bint al-Husayn.’ ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar said: ‘As for me, I wish for forgiveness.’

“He said: ‘They all attained what they wished for, and perhaps Ibn ‘Umar has been forgiven.’”

I say: The scholars agree that this chain of transmission is better than the former one, and it contains no petition invoking created beings.

On this subject, there are also accounts of people who saw a dream in which they were told: “Supplicate with such-and-such words.” The scholars agree that something of this kind cannot serve as evidence. Some compilers of supplications have included these accounts. A report concerning this has also been transmitted from one of the early generations, such as the one narrated by

---

## Page 266

Ibn Abī al-Dunyā in his book Mujābī al-Du‘ā’ (Those Whose Supplications Were Answered). He said:

Abū Hāshim narrated to us: I heard Kathīr ibn Muhammad ibn Kathīr ibn Rifā‘a say:

“A man came to ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Sa‘īd ibn Abjar, who examined his abdomen and said, ‘You have an illness that cannot be cured.’

“The man asked, ‘What is it?’

“He replied, ‘An internal abscess.’

“The man then turned and said, ‘Allah, Allah, Allah is my Lord; I associate nothing with Him. O Allah, I turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. O Muhammad, I turn through you to your Lord and mine, that He may have mercy on me for what afflicts me.’

“He then examined the man’s abdomen and said, ‘You have recovered; you have no illness.’”

I say: It has been reported that some of the early generations used this supplication and others like it. It has also been transmitted from Ahmad ibn Hanbal, in al-Marwadhī’s manual of pilgrimage rites, that one may seek a means of approach to Allah through the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in supplication. Others, however, prohibited this. If those seeking such a means intend to approach Allah through faith in the Prophet, love for him, allegiance to him, and obedience to him, then there is no disagreement between the two groups. If they intend to approach Allah through his person, then this is the point of dispute. Whatever they dispute must be referred back to Allah and the Messenger.

The mere fact that a supplication achieves its intended purpose does not establish that it is permissible under Islamic law. Many people invoke celestial bodies and created beings besides Allah, and some of what they desire comes about. Some deliberately supplicate at idols, churches, and other such places, and invoke the statues in churches, and some of what they desire comes about. Others use supplications that Muslims unanimously agree are forbidden, and some of what they desire comes about. Thus, achieving a purpose by some means does not necessarily make that means permissible, even if the purpose itself is permissible.

---

## Page 267

for that act may involve harm that outweighs its benefit. Islamic law came to secure and perfect benefits, and to eliminate and reduce harms. Otherwise, all forbidden acts—associating partners with Allah, drinking wine, gambling, indecency, and injustice—may bring their perpetrators benefits and fulfil some of their aims. Yet because their harms outweigh their benefits, Allah and His Messenger prohibited them. Likewise, many practices, such as acts of worship, jihad, and spending wealth, may entail harm; but because their benefits outweigh their harms, the Lawgiver commanded them.

This is a principle that must be taken into account. Nothing may be deemed obligatory or recommended without evidence from Islamic law establishing that status. Acts of worship can only be obligatory or recommended; whatever is neither obligatory nor recommended is not an act of worship. Supplication to Allah Most High is an act of worship if what is sought through it is permissible.

In sum, asking Allah through him has been reported from some of the early generations and scholars. This is unlike invoking the dead or absent among the prophets, angels, and righteous, seeking their aid, and voicing complaints to them. None of the early generations—the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness—did this, nor did any of the leading Muslim scholars permit it.

The hadith of the blind man, narrated by al-Tirmidhī and al-Nasā’ī, belongs to the second category: seeking a means of approach to Allah through the Prophet’s supplication:

“The blind man asked the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to supplicate for Allah to restore his sight. He told him, ‘If you wish, you may remain patient; and if you wish, I will supplicate for you.’ He replied, ‘Rather, supplicate to Him.’ So he instructed him to perform ablution, pray two units of prayer, and say:

‘O Allah, I ask You

---

## Page 268

through Your Prophet, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, O Messenger of Allah, I turn through you to my Lord concerning this need of mine, that He may fulfil it. O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf.’”

This is seeking a means of approach to Allah through the Prophet’s supplication and intercession—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—supplicated for him, which is why he said:

“And accept his intercession on my behalf.”

He thus asked Allah to accept His Messenger’s intercession for him, namely, his supplication. Scholars have cited this hadith when discussing the Prophet’s miracles—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—his answered supplications, and the extraordinary occurrences and cures of infirmities that Allah brought about through the blessing of his supplication. Through the blessing of his supplication for this blind man, Allah restored the man’s sight.

This hadith—the hadith of the blind man—was narrated by authors of works on the evidences of prophethood, such as al-Bayhaqī and others. Al-Bayhaqī narrated it through ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Umar, from Shu‘bah, from Abū Ja‘far al-Laythī, who said that he heard ‘Umārah ibn Khuzaymah ibn Thābit narrating from ‘Uthmān ibn Hunayf:

“A blind man came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and said, ‘Ask Allah to heal me.’ He told him, ‘If you wish, I will defer that, which is better for you; and if you wish, I will supplicate.’ He replied, ‘Then supplicate to Him.’ He instructed him to perform ablution thoroughly, pray two units of prayer, and offer this supplication:

‘O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I turn through you to my Lord concerning this need of mine, that He may fulfil it for me. O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf, and accept my intercession concerning him.’

He said, ‘The man then stood up, his sight restored.’”

Al-Tirmidhī narrated it through this same chain, from ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Umar. Versions of it were also narrated by al-Nasā’ī and Ibn Mājah. Al-Tirmidhī said: “This is a good and sound hadith, singular in its transmission. We know it only through this chain, from Abū Ja‘far, who is not al-Laythī.” This is how it appears in al-Tirmidhī’s work. The other scholars said that he is Abū Ja‘far al-Khatmī, and that is correct.

---

## Page 269

Moreover, al-Tirmidhī and the others who narrated it with him did not give its wording in full, as the other scholars did. Rather, they narrated it only as far as:

“O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: Mahmūd ibn Ghaylān narrated to us; ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Umar narrated to us; Shu‘bah narrated to us, from Abū Ja‘far, from ‘Umārah ibn Khuzaymah ibn Thābit, from ‘Uthmān ibn Hunayf:

“A blind man came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and said, ‘Ask Allah to heal me.’ He replied, ‘If you wish, you may remain patient; that is better for you.’ The man said, ‘Then supplicate to Him.’ He instructed him to perform ablution thoroughly and offer this supplication: ‘O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I have turned through you to my Lord concerning this need of mine, that it may be fulfilled. O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf.’”

Al-Bayhaqī said:

“We narrated it in the Book of Supplications with a sound chain of transmission from Rawh ibn ‘Ubādah, from Shu‘bah, who said: ‘The man did so and was cured.’” He said: “Hammād ibn Salamah likewise narrated it from Abū Ja‘far al-Khatmī.”

I say: Imam Ahmad also narrated it in his Musnad from Rawh ibn ‘Ubādah, as al-Bayhaqī mentioned. Ahmad said: Rawh ibn ‘Ubādah narrated to us; Shu‘bah narrated to us, from Abū Ja‘far al-Madīnī, who said: I heard ‘Umārah ibn Khuzaymah ibn Thābit narrating from ‘Uthmān ibn Hunayf:

“A blind man came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and said, ‘O Prophet of Allah, ask Allah to heal me.’ He replied, ‘If you wish, I will defer that, which is better for your Hereafter; and if you wish, I will supplicate for you.’ The man said, ‘No; rather, ask Allah for me.’ He instructed him to perform ablution, pray two units of prayer, and offer this supplication: ‘O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I turn through you to Allah concerning this need of mine, that it may be fulfilled for me, and that You accept my intercession concerning him and his intercession on my behalf.’ He said: ‘The man did so and was cured.’”

---

## Page 270

Al-Bayhaqī also narrated it through Shabīb ibn Sa‘īd al-Habatī, from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim, from Abū Ja‘far al-Madīnī—who is al-Khatmī—from Abū Umāmah ibn Sahl ibn Hunayf, from ‘Uthmān ibn Hunayf, who said:

“I heard the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when a blind man came to him complaining of the loss of his sight. He said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I have no one to guide me, and this has become difficult for me.’

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Go to the ablution place and perform ablution, then pray two units of prayer, then say: “O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I turn through you to my Lord so that my blindness may be removed. O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf, and accept my intercession on my own behalf.”’

“‘Uthmān ibn Hunayf said: ‘By Allah, we had neither dispersed nor been talking long before the man came in, as though he had never suffered any affliction.’”

Thus, Shabīb’s narration from Rawh, from Abū Ja‘far al-Laythī, differed from the narration of Shu‘bah and Hammād ibn Salamah in both its chain of transmission and its wording. In the latter, Abū Ja‘far narrated it from ‘Umārah ibn Khuzaymah, whereas in this narration he narrated it from Abū Umāmah Sahl. In the former narration, he said, “Accept his intercession on my behalf, and accept my intercession concerning him,” whereas this narration has, “Accept my intercession on my own behalf.” This chain of transmission, however, has further corroboration in a narration of Hishām al-Dastawā’ī from Abū Ja‘far.

Al-Bayhaqī narrated it through this route. It includes an account that those who seek access to Allah through him after his death may cite as evidence—if it is authentic. He narrated it through Ismā‘īl ibn Shabīb ibn Sa‘īd al-Habatī, from Shabīb ibn Sa‘īd, from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim, from Abū Ja‘far al-Madīnī, from Abū Umāmah Sahl ibn al-‘Utbiyyah: a man used to approach ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān concerning a need of his, but ‘Uthmān would pay him no attention and would not look into his need. The man then met ‘Uthmān ibn Hunayf

---

## Page 271

and complained to him about this. Uthmān ibn Hunayf said to him:

“Go to the ablution place and perform ablution, then go to the mosque and pray two units of prayer. Then say: ‘O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through our Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I turn through you to my Lord so that He may fulfill my need.’ Then mention your need, and then set out so that I may go with you.”

He said: The man went and did this. Afterwards, he came to Uthmān ibn Affān. The doorkeeper took him by the hand and brought him before Uthmān, who seated him beside himself on the rug and said, “Tell me what you need.” He mentioned his need, and Uthmān fulfilled it for him.

The man then left and met Uthmān ibn Hunayf. He said to him, “May Allah reward you with good. He would neither look into my need nor pay me any attention until you spoke to him on my behalf.”

Uthmān ibn Hunayf replied: “I did not speak to him. Rather, I heard the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—speak when a blind man came to him complaining of the loss of his sight. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said to him, ‘Would you be patient?’

“He replied, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I have no one to guide me, and this has become difficult for me.’ He said, ‘Go to the ablution place, perform ablution, and pray two units of prayer. Then say: “O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I turn to my Lord so that He may remove the affliction from my sight. O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf, and accept my intercession on my own behalf.”’”

Uthmān ibn Hunayf said: “By Allah, we had neither dispersed nor been talking long before the man came in, as though he had never suffered any affliction.”

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Ahmad ibn Shabīb ibn Saīd also narrated it in full from his father.” He then presented it through the narration of Yaqūb ibn Sufyān from Ahmad ibn Shabīb ibn Saīd.

He said: “Hishām al-Dastawā’ī also narrated it from Abū Jafar, from Abū Umāmah ibn Sahl, from his uncle—namely, Uthmān ibn Hunayf.” He did not mention the chains of transmission for these routes.

---

## Page 272

I say:

Al-Nasā’ī also narrated it in his book (Amal al-Yawm wa-l-Laylah—Deeds of the Day and Night) through this route, from Muādh ibn Hishām, from his father, from Abū Jafar, from Abū Umāmah Sahl ibn Hunayf, from his uncle Uthmān ibn Hunayf. He also narrated it from Shubah and Hammād ibn Salamah, both from Abū Jafar, from Umārah ibn Khuzaymah. None of these—neither al-Tirmidhī, al-Nasā’ī, nor Ibn Mājah—narrated it through that singular route containing the addition: the route of Shabīb ibn Saīd from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim.

Al-Hākim, however, narrated it in his Mustadrak through both routes. He narrated it from Uthmān ibn Umar: Shubah narrated to us from Abū Jafar al-Madanī, who said: I heard Umārah ibn Khuzaymah relate from Uthmān ibn Hunayf:

“A blind man came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and said, ‘Pray to Allah to restore my health.’ He replied, ‘If you wish, I will postpone that, which would be better for you; or, if you wish, I will pray.’ He said, ‘Then pray to Him.’

“He instructed him to perform ablution thoroughly, pray two units of prayer, and make this supplication: ‘O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I have turned through you to my Lord concerning this need of mine. O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf, and accept my intercession concerning him.’”

Al-Hākim said: “It meets the criteria of al-Bukhārī and Muslim.”

He then narrated it through Shabīb ibn Saīd al-Habatī and Awn ibn Umārah, from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim, from Abū Jafar al-Laythī al-Madanī, from Abū Umāmah Sahl ibn Hunayf, from his uncle:

“Uthmān ibn Hunayf heard the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when a blind man came to him and complained of the loss of his sight, saying, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I have no one to guide me, and this has become difficult for me.’ He said, ‘Go to the ablution place and perform ablution, then pray two units of prayer. Then say: “O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through Your Prophet

---

## Page 273

Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I turn through you to my Lord, that He may restore my sight. O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf, and accept my intercession for myself.”’ Uthmān said: ‘By Allah, we had neither dispersed nor been speaking for long before the man entered, as though he had never suffered any affliction.’”

Al-Hākim said: “It meets al-Bukhārī’s criteria.”

This Shabīb is truthful, and al-Bukhārī narrated reports from him. However, objectionable hadiths on the authority of Rawh ibn al-Faraj have also been transmitted from him, as narrated by Ibn Wahb, and mistakes in reporting from him have been suspected. Yet one may say that if a narrator like this alone reports an addition absent from the accounts of trustworthy narrators whose memories are more accurate than his—such as Shubah, Hammād ibn Salamah, and Hishām al-Dastawā’ī—this would count against his narration. This is especially so since this version reads:

“Accept his intercession on my behalf, and accept my intercession for myself,”

whereas those narrators reported:

“Accept his intercession on my behalf, and accept my intercession concerning him.”

The words “accept my intercession concerning him” mean concerning his supplication and request on my behalf. They thus correspond to the words “accept his intercession on my behalf.”

Abū Ahmad ibn Adī said in his book entitled Al-Kāmil fī Asmā’ al-Rijāl (The Comprehensive Work on Narrators’ Names)—a work unmatched in its field:

“Shabīb ibn Saīd al-Habatī, Abū Saīd al-Basrī al-Tamīmī: Ibn Wahb transmitted objectionable reports from him. He also transmitted sound reports from Yūnus, from al-Zuhrī, in the collection of al-Zuhrī’s hadiths.”

He cited Alī ibn al-Madīnī as saying: “He is a trustworthy narrator from Basra and was one of Yūnus’s associates. He used to travel to Egypt on business and brought back an accurate written collection.” He said: “His son Ahmad ibn Shabīb copied those reports from him.”

He also narrated from Adī two hadiths from Ibn Wahb, from this Shabīb, from Rawh ibn al-Faraj:

The first was from Ibn Aqīl, from Sābiq ibn Nājiyah, from Ibn Salām, who said: “A man passed by us, and they said, ‘This man served the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.’”

---

## Page 274

And the second was transmitted from him, from Rawh ibn al-Faraj, from Abd Allāh ibn al-Husayn, from his mother Fātimah: the hadith concerning entry into the mosque. Ibn Adī said:

“That is how the chain is given in the hadith: from Abd Allāh ibn al-Husayn, from his mother Fātimah bint al-Husayn, from Fātimah, the daughter of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Ibn Adī said:

“Shabīb ibn Saīd possessed a collection of al-Zuhrī’s hadiths, transmitted through Yūnus from al-Zuhrī, and these are sound reports. Ibn Wahb, however, transmitted objectionable hadiths from him. The two hadiths of Rawh ibn al-Faraj that I dictated are transmitted by Ibn Wahb from Shabīb. When his son Ahmad ibn Shabīb transmitted al-Zuhrī’s collection from him, Shabīb ibn Saīd was not the same Shabīb ibn Saīd from whom Ibn Wahb transmitted those objectionable reports. Perhaps, while Shabīb was in Egypt on business, Ibn Wahb recorded reports from him as he related them from memory, and he made mistakes and misremembered. I hope that Shabīb did not deliberately utter this falsehood.”

I say:

These two hadiths, which Ibn Adī found objectionable in his transmissions, were narrated by him from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim. Likewise, he narrated this hadith—the hadith of the blind man—from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim. This hadith was also transmitted from him by Ibn Wahb, just as it was transmitted from him by his two sons. Ibn Wahb, however, did not preserve its wording as accurately as his two sons did. This bears out what Ibn Adī said, establishing that the report was indeed preserved from Shabīb.

Ibn Adī attributed the error to Shabīb, not to Ibn Wahb. This is correct if Shabīb did make an error. If he made errors in transmitting those two hadiths from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim, it is possible that he also made an error in transmitting this hadith from him. Rawh ibn al-Qāsim is a well-known, trustworthy narrator whose reports were included by all six compilers of the principal hadith collections. For this reason, they did not attribute the error to him.

---

## Page 275

A narrator may accurately preserve what he transmits from one teacher, yet fail to do so with what he transmits from another. Such is the case with Ismā‘īl ibn Ayyāsh: he makes mistakes in what he transmits from narrators of the Hijaz, unlike what he transmits from Syrian narrators. Another example is Sufyān ibn Husayn in his transmissions from al-Zuhrī. There are many such cases. Thus, this narrator may have made mistakes in what he transmitted from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim—if matters were as Ibn Adī stated. This, however, requires further examination.

Al-Tabarānī narrated this hadith in al-Mu‘jam through Ibn Wahb, from Shabīb ibn Saīd. He also narrated it through Asbagh ibn al-Faraj, who said: Abd Allāh ibn Wahb narrated to us, from Shabīb ibn Saīd al-Makkī, from Rawh ibn al-Qāsim, from Abū Ja‘far al-Laythī al-Madanī, from Abū Umāmah Sahl ibn Hunayf, from his uncle Uthmān ibn Hunayf:

“A man used to visit Uthmān ibn Affān repeatedly concerning a need of his. He met Uthmān ibn Hunayf and complained to him about this. Uthmān ibn Hunayf told him: ‘Go to the place for ablution and perform ablution. Then go to the mosque and pray two units of prayer there. Then say: “O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through our Prophet Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I turn through you to your Lord, Mighty and Majestic, so that He may fulfill my need.” Mention your need, then set out so that I may go with you.’

The man went and did as he had been told. He then came to Uthmān ibn Affān’s door, and Uthmān seated him beside him on the rug and said: ‘What is your need?’ He stated his need, and Uthmān fulfilled it for him. Then he told him: ‘I had not remembered your need until this very moment.’ He also said: ‘Whenever you have a need, come to us.’

The man then left and met Uthmān ibn Hunayf. He said to him: ‘May Allah reward you with good! He would neither attend to my need nor pay any attention to me until you spoke to him on my behalf.’

Uthmān ibn Hunayf said to him:

---

## Page 276

‘By Allah, I did not speak to him. But I was present with the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when a blind man came to him and complained of losing his sight. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—asked him, “Will you bear it patiently?”

‘He replied, “O Messenger of Allah, I have no one to guide me, and this has become difficult for me.” The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—told him, “Go to the place for ablution and perform ablution, then pray two units of prayer, then offer these supplications.”’

Uthmān ibn Hunayf said: ‘By Allah, we had neither dispersed nor spoken for long before the man came in to us, as though he had never suffered any affliction.’”

Al-Tabarānī said: “Shu‘bah narrated this hadith from Abū Ja‘far, whose name is Umayr ibn Yazīd and who is trustworthy. Uthmān ibn Umar alone transmitted it from Shu‘bah.” Abū Abd Allāh al-Maqdisī said: “The hadith is authentic.”

I say: Al-Tabarānī stated that he alone transmitted it according to the extent of his knowledge. The transmission of Rawh ibn Ubādah from Shu‘bah had not reached him. That chain of transmission is authentic and demonstrates that Uthmān ibn Umar was not its sole transmitter.

This route through Ibn Wahb supports what Ibn Adī mentioned, for Ibn Wahb did not preserve the wording of the report as precisely as Shabīb’s two sons did. Rather, he stated in it that the blind man offered the same supplication that Uthmān ibn Hunayf mentioned. That is not the case. Rather, the hadith of the blind man states that he said:

“O Allah, accept his intercession for me, and accept my intercession for him—or he said: for myself.”

Ibn Wahb did not mention this in his transmission. It therefore appears that Ibn Wahb related it from memory, as Ibn Adī said, and did not transmit it accurately.

Abū Bakr ibn Abī Khaythamah also narrated the hadith of Hammād ibn Salamah in his History, saying:

Muslim ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us; Hammād ibn Salamah narrated to us; Abū Ja‘far al-Khatmī informed us, from Umārah ibn Khuzaymah, from Uthmān ibn Hunayf, who related “that

---

## Page 277

a blind man came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and said: ‘My sight has been afflicted, so pray to Allah for me.’ He said: ‘Go and perform ablution, pray two units of prayer, then say: “O Allah, I ask You and turn to You through my Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy. O Muhammad, I seek your intercession with my Lord for the restoration of my sight. O Allah, accept my intercession for myself, and accept my Prophet’s intercession for the restoration of my sight.” If a need arises, do likewise.’ Then Allah restored his sight.”

Ibn Abī Khaythamah said:

“This Abū Ja‘far—from whom Hammād ibn Salamah narrated—is named Umayr ibn Yazīd. He is the same Abū Ja‘far from whom Shu‘bah narrates.” He then mentioned the hadith through Uthmān ibn Umar, from Shu‘bah.

I say:

This route contains the words “accept my intercession for myself,” as does the route through Rawh ibn al-Qāsim. It also contains another addition: “If a need arises, do likewise”—or he said—“If a need arose, he would do likewise.”

It may be said that this agrees with the statement of Uthmān ibn Hunayf. However, Shu‘bah and Rawh ibn al-Qāsim were more accurate in preserving reports than Hammād ibn Salamah. The differences in wording indicate that a narration such as this may have been related by meaning rather than verbatim. The statement “If a need arose, he would do likewise” may have been interpolated from Uthmān’s words, rather than being the words of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. For he did not say, “If you had a need, you would do likewise”; rather, he said, “If a need arose, he would do likewise.”

In short, even if this addition were established, it would provide no proof. At most, it would mean that Uthmān ibn Hunayf thought that some parts of the supplication could be recited while others were omitted. He did not instruct the man to recite the prescribed supplication, but only part of it, and he thought that this was prescribed after the Prophet’s death—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Yet the wording of the hadith contradicts this, for the hadith states that the blind man asked the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

---

## Page 278

to pray for him, and that he taught the blind man to supplicate and instructed him to say in his supplication:

“O Allah, accept his intercession for me.”

This supplication is made only when the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is praying and interceding for the person, unlike someone for whom this is not the case. Thus, it accords with his intercession and supplication for people during his earthly life and on the Day of Resurrection, when he intercedes for them.

The hadith also includes his words:

“And accept my intercession concerning him.”

This does not mean that the blind man intercedes on behalf of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to fulfil a need of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—even though we are commanded to invoke blessings and peace upon him and to ask Allah to grant him al-wasīlah. In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, Jābir ibn Abd Allāh reports that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever says upon hearing the call to prayer, ‘O Allah, Lord of this perfect call and the prayer to be established, grant Muhammad al-wasīlah and eminence, and raise him to the praised station You have promised him,’ will be entitled to my intercession on the Day of Resurrection.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abd Allāh ibn Amr reports that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“When you hear the caller to prayer, repeat what he says, then invoke blessings upon me, for whoever invokes one blessing upon me, Allah will bless him ten times. Then ask Allah to grant me al-wasīlah, for it is a rank in Paradise befitting only one of Allah’s servants, and I hope to be that servant. Whoever asks Allah to grant me al-wasīlah will be entitled to intercession.”

The community’s asking Allah to grant him al-wasīlah is a supplication on his behalf, which is what intercession means. Thus, the reward corresponds to the deed: whoever invokes blessings upon him receives blessings from Allah, and whoever asks Allah to grant him al-wasīlah, which entails

---

## Page 279

his intercession, will have the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—intercede on his behalf. Likewise, the blind man asked him to intercede, so he instructed him to ask Allah to accept this intercession. This is akin to interceding for an intercession to be accepted. Hence he said:

“O Allah, accept his intercession for me, and accept my intercession concerning him.”

Allah’s acceptance of the Prophet’s supplication—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in such a case reflects the honour in which the Messenger is held by his Lord. This is why it was counted among his signs and the proofs of his prophethood. It is thus like his intercession for creation on the Day of Resurrection. For this reason, he instructed the person requesting his supplication to say:

“Accept his intercession for me, and accept my intercession concerning him.”

This differs from the wording, “And accept my intercession for myself,” which no one narrated except through this singular route of transmission. The wording, “And accept my intercession concerning him,” was narrated from Shuʿbah by two eminent men: Uthmān ibn Umar and Rawh ibn Ubādah.

Shuʿbah is the most eminent of those who narrated this hadith. Through Uthmān ibn Umar, from Shuʿbah, it was narrated by the three: al-Tirmidhī, al-Nasāʾī and Ibn Mājah. Al-Tirmidhī narrated it from Mahmūd ibn Ghaylān, from Uthmān ibn Umar, from Shuʿbah. Ibn Mājah narrated it from Ahmad ibn Sayyār, from Uthmān ibn Umar. Ahmad also narrated it in al-Musnad from Rawh ibn Ubādah, from Shuʿbah. These narrators thus preserved the wording of the hadith more accurately.

Moreover, if the wording, “And accept my intercession for myself,” has been accurately preserved, it has the same meaning as what we have explained: he asked to intercede for himself alongside the Prophet’s supplication—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Had the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—not supplicated for him, he would merely have been making a request, like any other petitioner. Such a request is not called intercession; intercession occurs only when there are two people

---

## Page 280

seeking something, so that one intercedes for the other, unlike a lone petitioner for whom no one else intercedes.

This addition has several defects: this narrator alone transmitted it, whereas those more eminent and more accurate in their retention did not; the compilers of the Sunan collections omitted it; its wording is inconsistent; and its narrator is known to have transmitted objectionable hadiths from this same Rawh. Such considerations give rise to doubt about its authenticity, so it cannot serve as evidence. What counts is what the Companion narrated, not his understanding of it, when the wording he narrated does not support that understanding but instead indicates the contrary.

It is clear that if someone, after the Prophet’s death, were to say:

“O Allah, accept his intercession for me, and accept my intercession concerning him,”

when the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had not supplicated for him, these words would be invalid. Moreover, Uthmān ibn Hunayf did not instruct him to ask the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for anything, nor to say, “Accept his intercession for me.” He did not instruct him to recite the transmitted supplication in its proper form, but only part of it. There was no intercession from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in this situation, nor anything that might be thought to constitute intercession. Had someone said after his death, “Accept his intercession for me,” these words would have had no meaning. This is why Uthmān did not instruct him to say them.

He did not prescribe the supplication transmitted from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and what he did prescribe was not transmitted from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Something of this kind cannot establish a religious prescription, just as other accounts transmitted from individual Companions concerning acts of worship, permissions, obligations or prohibitions cannot do so when no one else among

---

## Page 281

the Companions agrees with him—and what is authentically established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, contradicts rather than supports his practice—his action is not a sunnah that Muslims are obliged to follow. At most, it may be a matter open to independent scholarly judgment and one over which the Muslim community has differed; it must therefore be referred back to Allah and the Messenger.

There are many comparable examples. Ibn Umar used to put water into his eyes during ablution and take fresh water for his ears. Abū Hurayra used to wash his arms up to the upper arms during ablution, saying, “Whoever can extend the radiant mark on his face should do so.” It is also reported that he used to wipe his neck, saying, “This is where the shackle is placed.”

Although some scholars considered these practices recommended, following these two Companions, others disagreed with them, saying:

The other Companions did not perform ablution in this manner. The Prophet’s ablution, as authentically reported through several chains in the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere, did not include taking fresh water for the ears, washing beyond the elbows and ankles, or wiping the neck. Nor did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say, “Whoever can extend the radiant mark on his face should do so.” Rather, these were Abū Hurayra’s own words, incorporated into some hadith reports. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said only:

“You will come on the Day of Resurrection with your faces, hands and feet radiant from the effects of ablution.”

When performing ablution, he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would wash just into the upper arm and the shin. Abū Hurayra said, “Whoever can extend the radiant mark on his face should do so.” Some supposed that washing the upper arm extended this radiant mark, but this makes no sense: that mark is on the face, not on the hands and feet. The hands and feet have their own radiant markings. The radiant mark on the face cannot be extended, for the face

---

## Page 282

is washed in its entirety. The head is not washed, nor does it bear the radiant mark of the face. Extending the radiant markings of the hands and feet is not recommended; extending them is a disfigurement.

Likewise, Ibn Umar would take care to travel along the routes taken by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and stop where he had stopped. During his travels, he would perform ablution wherever he had seen him perform it, and pour his remaining water over a tree over which the Prophet had poured his. Some scholars regarded these and similar practices as recommended, but the majority did not. Nor did the leading Companions, such as Abū Bakr, Umar, Uthmān, Alī, Ibn Masʿūd, Muʿādh ibn Jabal and others, consider them recommended or practise them. They did not do what Ibn Umar did. Had they considered it recommended, they would have done it, for they were intent on following the Prophet and emulating him.

This is because following him means doing what he did in the manner in which he did it. If he performed an act as worship, it is prescribed for us to perform it as worship. If he intended to single out a particular place or time for worship, we likewise single it out for that purpose. Thus, he deliberately circumambulated the Kaʿba, touched the Black Stone and prayed behind the Station of Abraham. He would take care to pray beside a particular pillar in the mosque in Medina. He deliberately ascended al-Safā and al-Marwa and offered supplication and remembrance of Allah there. The same applies to Arafat, Muzdalifa and other places.

As for what he did incidentally, without deliberately choosing it—such as stopping at a place and praying there because he had stopped there, rather than intending to single it out for prayer and stopping—if we deliberately single out that place for prayer or stopping, we are not following him. Rather, this is among the innovations that

---

## Page 283

Umar ibn al-Khattāb used to forbid. This is established through an authentic chain of transmission in the report narrated by Shuʿba from Sulaymān al-Taymī, from al-Maʿrūr (1) ibn Suwayd, who said:

“Umar ibn al-Khattāb was travelling. He offered the dawn prayer, then passed a place which people began visiting, saying, ‘The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed here.’ Umar said, ‘The People of the Book perished because they sought out the sites associated with their prophets and turned them into churches and monasteries. Whoever happens to be there when prayer is due should pray; otherwise, he should continue on his way.’”

Since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had not intended to single out that place for prayer, but had prayed there because it was where he had stopped, Umar considered that reproducing the outward form of his action without sharing his intention did not constitute following him. Rather, singling out that place for prayer was among the innovations of the People of the Book that brought about their ruin. He therefore forbade Muslims to imitate them in this regard. Whoever does so resembles the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in outward form, but resembles the Jews and Christians in intention, which is an act of the heart.

This is the fundamental principle: following the Sunnah is more important than reproducing the outward form of an action. Thus, when many scholars were uncertain whether he had performed the sitting for rest because it was recommended or because of a temporary need, they differed over it. Likewise, they differed over his stopping at al-Muhassab when leaving Minā, because it was unclear whether he had done so because it made his departure easier or because it was a Sunnah.

Other examples include Ibn Umar’s placing his hand on the seat of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the observance of the Day of Arafat through supplication and remembrance by Ibn Abbās in Basra and by Amr ibn Zādhān in Kufa. For, since this was not

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The printed edition incorrectly reads “al-Maʿrūf” instead of “al-Maʿrūr.”

---

## Page 284

something the other Companions did, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had not prescribed it for his community, it could not be called a recommended Sunnah. At most, one could say that this was a matter in which the Companions could legitimately exercise scholarly judgment, or that whoever practised it should not be censured, because it was open to scholarly judgment—not because it was a recommended Sunnah instituted by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for his community. Alternatively, concerning the observance of the Day of Arafat through supplication and remembrance, one could say that there is no harm in doing so occasionally in response to a particular circumstance, provided it is not made a regularly observed Sunnah.

This is how the leading scholars speak about this and similar matters: sometimes they disapprove of them, sometimes they allow scope for scholarly judgment, and sometimes they permit them provided they are not adopted as a Sunnah. No scholar versed in the Sunnah says that these are practices prescribed as Sunnah for Muslims. Such a statement can only be made about what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed, since no one else has the authority to institute a Sunnah or prescribe religious practices. Whatever his rightly guided caliphs instituted, they instituted at his command, and it therefore forms part of his Sunnah. Nothing in religion is obligatory unless he made it obligatory, forbidden unless he forbade it, recommended unless he recommended it, disliked unless he deemed it disliked, or permissible unless he permitted it.

The same applies to judgments of permissibility. For example, Abū Talha considered it permissible to eat hail while fasting, and Hudhayfa considered it permissible to eat the pre-dawn meal after the spreading light had appeared, to the point that it was said, “It was daytime, except that the sun had not yet risen.” Other Companions did not hold these views, so the matter had to be referred back to the Quran and the Sunnah.

The same applies to disapproval and prohibition, such as Umar and his son’s disapproval of applying perfume before circumambulating the House, and the disapproval expressed by some Companions of converting Hajj into tamattuʿ (performing ʿumra followed by Hajj), or of tamattuʿ in general;

---

## Page 285

or the view of someone who fixed a particular minimum travel distance for shortening prayers and held that they could not be shortened below that distance; or who held that a traveller may not fast while travelling.

Other examples include Salmān’s statement that saliva is ritually impure; Ibn Umar’s statement that marriage to a woman from the People of the Book is impermissible; Muʿādh and Muʿāwiya’s allowing a Muslim to inherit from a disbeliever; Umar and Ibn Masʿūd’s refusal to allow someone in a state of janāba (major ritual impurity) to perform dry ablution; and the statement of Alī, Zayd and Ibn Umar that a woman whose marriage was contracted without a specified dowry is not entitled to a dowry if her husband dies. They also include Alī and Ibn Abbās’s statement that a pregnant widow must observe a waiting period ending at the later of the two prescribed terms; and Ibn Umar and others’ statement that, when someone in ihrām (pilgrimage consecration) dies, his ihrām ends and he is treated as someone not in ihrām.

Further examples include Ibn Umar and others’ statement that stipulating a condition in Hajj is impermissible; Ibn Abbās and others’ statement that a widow is not required to remain at home; and Umar and Ibn Masʿūd’s statement that an irrevocably divorced woman is entitled to accommodation and maintenance. These and similar matters over which the Companions disagreed must be referred back to Allah and the Messenger. There are many such examples. Nothing constitutes prescribed religious law for the community except what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed.

Those scholars who said, “A Companion’s statement is authoritative proof,” meant this only when no other Companion disagreed with him and no text was known to contradict his statement. If the statement then became widely known and they did not object to it, this constituted assent to that statement. It may therefore be said, “This is consensus by assent,” provided it is known that they assented to it and that none of them objected, for they do not assent to falsehood. If, however, the statement did not become widely known, then, if it is known that no other Companion disagreed with him, it may be said, “It is authoritative proof.”

---

## Page 286

But if it is known that another Companion disagreed with him, his statement is not authoritative proof, by agreement. If it is not known whether another Companion agreed or disagreed with him, neither possibility can be asserted conclusively. Whenever the Sunnah indicates otherwise, authoritative proof lies in the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, not in what contradicts it. There is no doubt about this among the scholars.

Accordingly, if it is established that Uthmān ibn Hanīf or someone else considered it a prescribed and recommended practice to seek a means of approach to Allah through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, after his death, without the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, praying for that person or interceding on his behalf, we know that Umar and the leading Companions did not regard this as prescribed after his death as it had been during his lifetime. Rather, during his lifetime they sought a means of approach through him when praying for rain, but after he died they no longer did so.

Instead, Umar said in his authentic, well-known supplication, whose authenticity is unanimously acknowledged by the scholars, in the presence of the Emigrants and the Helpers during the well-known Year of Ashes—when the drought became so severe that Umar swore not to eat clarified butter until the people enjoyed plenty again—when he sought rain through al-Abbās:

“O Allah, whenever we suffered drought, we used to seek a means of approach to You through our Prophet, and You would grant us rain. Now we seek a means of approach to You through our Prophet’s uncle, so grant us rain.”

And they would be granted rain. All the Companions assented to this supplication, and none objected to it despite its being widely known. It is one of the clearest instances of consensus by assent. Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān made a similar supplication during his caliphate when he led the people in praying for rain.

Had their seeking a means of approach through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, after his death been the same as doing so during his lifetime, they would have said:

“How can we seek a means of approach through the likes of al-Abbās, Yazīd ibn al-Aswad and others like them, and turn away from seeking a means of approach through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who is the best of creation and the best means of approach

---

## Page 287

and the greatest in Allah’s sight?” Since none of them said this, and since it is known that during his lifetime they sought a means of approach through his supplication and intercession, while after his death they did so through the supplication and intercession of others, it is clear that what they regarded as prescribed was seeking a means of approach through the supplication of the person concerned, not through his person.

The hadith of the blind man supports Umar and the general body of the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them all. For the Prophet only instructed the blind man to seek a means of approach to Allah through his intercession and supplication, not through his person. He told him to say in his supplication:

“Say: ‘O Allah, accept his intercession on my behalf.’”

Even supposing that a Companion instructed someone else to seek a means of approach through the Prophet’s person rather than his intercession, and instructed him to recite only part of the prescribed supplication, omitting the remainder that included seeking a means of approach through his intercession, Umar ibn al-Khattāb’s practice would still be the one consistent with the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would refute whoever differed with Umar, and the hadith that this Companion narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would be evidence against him, not in his favour. And Allah knows best.

As for the third category of what is called “seeking a means of approach,” no one can transmit anything concerning it from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that scholars would accept as evidence—as has already been discussed at length. This consists of adjuring Allah, Mighty and Majestic, by the prophets and the righteous, or petitioning Him by their persons. No one can transmit anything authentically established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning either adjuring Allah or petitioning Him by the Prophet, or adjuring Allah or petitioning Him by any other created being. Although some scholars have permitted it, it is established that more than one scholar forbade

---

## Page 288

it. It is therefore a matter of dispute, as explained earlier. Their disagreement must be referred to Allah and His Messenger, and each party must present his evidence, as in other disputed matters. By the consensus of the Muslims, this is not a matter warranting punishment. Rather, anyone who punishes someone for it is transgressing, ignorant and unjust. For the person who holds this view has said what scholars have said, while the person who censures him has no transmitted evidence—from either the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or the Companions—that must be followed.

It has been established that swearing by anyone other than Allah is impermissible, whether by the prophets or anyone else, as has already been explained at length. The scholars have also agreed that no one may make a vow to anyone other than Allah, whether a prophet or anyone else, and that such a vow constitutes shirk and must not be fulfilled. Likewise, swearing by created beings does not constitute a binding oath, and no expiation is required for it. Even if someone swears by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his oath is not binding, as mentioned earlier, and no expiation is required of him according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, according to one of the two positions reported from him. Indeed, swearing such an oath was forbidden. If a person may neither swear such an oath nor use it to adjure a created being, how can he use it to adjure the Creator, exalted be His majesty?

As for petitioning Allah by a created being without adjuring Him by that being, this too is something that more than one scholar has forbidden. The authentic Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his rightly guided caliphs supports this prohibition. For a person does this only on the understanding that it is an act of drawing near to Allah and of obedience to Him, and that it is a means by which supplication is answered. Anything of this kind must be either obligatory or recommended.

---

## Page 289

and everything that is obligatory or recommended in worship and supplication must have been prescribed by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for his community. If he did not prescribe this for his community, then it is neither obligatory nor recommended, nor is it an act of drawing near to Allah or of obedience to Him, nor a means of having supplication answered. All of this has already been discussed at length. Whoever believes otherwise concerning either of these practices is misguided, and his innovation is among the blameworthy innovations. Authentic hadiths and an examination of the practice of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his rightly guided caliphs make it clear that this was not prescribed among them.

It has also become clear that this is petitioning Allah Most High on a basis that has no bearing on the answering of supplication. It is like petitioning Him by the Kaaba, Mount Sinai, the Kursī, mosques, or other created things. It is well known that petitioning Allah by created things is not prescribed, just as swearing by them is not prescribed; rather, it is forbidden. Thus, just as no one may swear by a created being, no one may adjure Allah by a created being or petition Him by a created being itself. Rather, one should petition Him only through means relevant to the answering of supplication, as explained in detail earlier. Reports and statements permitting this have, however, been transmitted from some scholars. Yet nothing transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on this matter is established; rather, all such reports are fabricated.

As for reports from those whose statements do not constitute authoritative evidence, some are established and others are not. The hadith narrated by Ahmad and Ibn Mājah includes the words:

“By the right that those who ask of You have upon You, and by the right of

---

## Page 290

this walk of mine.” Ahmad narrated it from Wakī‘, from Fudayl ibn Marzūq, from ‘Atiyya, from Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Whoever says, when setting out for prayer, ‘O Allah, I ask You by the right that those who ask of You have upon You, and by the right of this walk of mine. I have not set out in insolence or arrogance, nor to show off or gain a reputation. I have set out to avoid Your displeasure and seek Your pleasure. I ask You to save me from the Fire, admit me to Paradise, and forgive my sins, for none forgives sins except You,’ will be accompanied by seventy thousand angels seeking forgiveness for him, and Allah will turn His Face toward him until he completes his prayer.”

This hadith is narrated by ‘Atiyya al-Sālihiyya from Abū Sa‘īd, and he is weak by the consensus of the scholars. It has also been narrated through another chain, which is likewise weak. Nor does its wording provide any proof for this practice: the right that those who ask have upon Him is that He answer them, and the right that worshippers have is that He reward them. This is a right that Allah Most High has established upon His noble Self through His truthful promise, as the scholars unanimously agree, and by making it obligatory upon Himself, according to one of their views. This has already been discussed at length.

This is comparable to the three men who petitioned Him in the cave through their deeds. One petitioned Him through his outstanding devotion to his parents; another through his remarkable restraint from sexual immorality; and the third through his exemplary fulfilment of a trust. Allah commanded these deeds and promised to reward those who performed them. Thus, this is like what He relates of the believers in His words:

“Our Lord, we have heard a caller calling to faith, ‘Believe in your Lord,’ and we have believed. Our Lord, forgive us our sins, remove from us our misdeeds, and cause us to die among the righteous.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Indeed, there was a group of My servants who said, ‘Our Lord, we have believed, so forgive us and have mercy upon us, for You are the best of those who show mercy.’”

And He, Most High, said:

“Say, ‘Shall I inform you of something better than that? For those who fear Allah, with their Lord are

---

## Page 291

gardens beneath which rivers flow, wherein they will abide forever, purified spouses, and approval from Allah. And Allah sees His servants.”

“Those who say, ‘Our Lord, we have believed, so forgive us our sins and protect us from the punishment of the Fire.’”

Ibn Mas‘ūd used to say in the hours before dawn: “O Allah, You called me, and I answered; You commanded me, and I obeyed. This is the hour before dawn, so forgive me.”

The fundamental principle governing this matter may be stated as follows: adjuring Allah by any created thing, or petitioning Him by it, must fall into one of these categories: commanded as obligatory or recommended; prohibited as unlawful or disliked; or permissible, being neither commanded nor prohibited.

If it is said that this is commanded or permissible, then either a distinction must be drawn between one created thing and another, or it must be said that this practice is prescribed with revered created things, or with some of them.

Whoever says that this is commanded or permissible with all created things must consequently allow Allah Most High to be petitioned by the devils among humans and jinn. No Muslim would say this.

If, instead, someone says that He may be petitioned by revered created things, such as those by which He swears in His Book, it follows that He may be petitioned by the night as it envelops, the day as it shines forth, the male and the female, the sun and its brightness, the moon as it follows it, the day as it reveals it, the night as it conceals it, the sky and He who built it, the earth and He who spread it, and the soul and He who proportioned it. It also follows that Allah Most High may be petitioned and adjured by the retreating stars that run their courses and disappear, the night as it recedes, and the dawn as it breathes; that He may be petitioned by those that scatter far and wide, those that bear heavy loads, those that sail with ease, and those that apportion commands; and that He may be petitioned by the Mount, a Book inscribed on unrolled parchment, the much-frequented House, and the raised canopy

---

## Page 292

and the brimming sea; and that He may be petitioned and adjured by those lined up in rows and by everything else by which Allah swears in His Book.

Allah swears by those created things by which He swears because they are His signs and His creation. They bear witness to His Lordship, His right to be worshipped, His oneness, His knowledge, His power, His will, His mercy, His wisdom, His greatness, and His might. He, glorified be He, swears by them because His swearing by them magnifies Him. We, however, are created beings, and we may not swear by them, as established by the scriptural texts and scholarly consensus. Indeed, more than one scholar has reported consensus that nothing created may be sworn by, and they have reported the Companions’ consensus on this. Rather, doing so is a forbidden form of associating partners with Allah.

Whoever petitions Allah by these things must, by the same reasoning, petition Him by every male and female, and by every soul to which He has inspired its wickedness and its righteousness. He must petition Him by the winds, the clouds, the stars, the sun, the moon, the night, the day, the fig, the olive, and Mount Sinai. He must petition Him by the secure city, Mecca, and consequently by the House, al-Safā, al-Marwah, Arafah, Muzdalifah, Minā, and other created things. It would also follow that he must petition Him by created beings that have been worshipped besides Allah, such as the sun, the moon, the stars, the angels, the Messiah, Uzayr, and other things, whether they have been worshipped besides Allah or not.

It is well known that petitioning Allah by these created things, or adjuring Him by them, is among the gravest reprehensible innovations in the religion of Islam, and its offensiveness is apparent to the learned and the general public alike. It would further follow that Allah Most High could be adjured by the adjurations and incantations written in

---

## Page 293

protective amulets and hayākil (charms) written by the turuqiyya and practitioners of incantations. Indeed, it may further be argued: if petitioning Allah and adjuring Him by these things is permissible, then doing so with created beings is all the more permissible. The incantations and adjurations used to adjure the jinn would then be sanctioned in the religion of Islam. This position entails disbelief and departure from the religion of Islam—indeed, from the religion of all the prophets.

If someone says, “Rather, I petition Him or adjure Him by certain revered created beings, but not others: by the prophets alone, or by one prophet rather than another—as some have permitted swearing by such a prophet—or by the prophets and the righteous, but not others,” the reply is:

Although some created beings are superior to others, they all share this: none may be made a rival to Allah Most High. None may be worshipped, relied upon, feared, or held in awe; none may be fasted for, prostrated to, or turned to in hope. Nor may an oath be sworn by any created being, as established in an authentic report from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever wishes to swear an oath, let him swear by Allah or remain silent.”

He also said:

“Do not swear except by Allah.”

And in the Sunan collections, he is reported to have said:

“Whoever swears by other than Allah has associated partners with Him.”

Authentic, explicit texts from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus establish that it is impermissible to swear by any created being. In this respect, there is no distinction between angels, prophets, the righteous, and others, nor between one prophet and another. Likewise, Allah Most High has treated all created beings alike in condemning the association of them as partners with Him, even when they are revered.

Allah Most High says:

“It is not for a human being to whom Allah gives the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood

---

## Page 294

then say to the people, ‘Be my worshippers rather than Allah’s.’ Rather, ‘Be godly scholars through your teaching of the Scripture and your study of it.’”

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and the prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have submitted to Allah?”

Allah Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to remove your affliction or transfer it elsewhere.’”

“Those whom they call upon themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, striving to be closest to Him. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

A group of the early generations said:

“Some people used to invoke the Messiah, Ezra, and the angels. So Allah Most High said: ‘Those whom you invoke are My servants. They hope for My mercy just as you hope for My mercy; they fear My punishment just as you fear My punishment; and they seek to draw near to Me just as you seek to draw near to Me.’”

Allah Most High has also said:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, fears Allah, and is mindful of Him—it is they who will succeed.”

He thus made clear that obedience is due to Allah and the Messenger, for whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed Allah. He also made clear that reverent fear and mindfulness are due to Allah alone. He did not command that any created being be held in such fear or treated with such mindfulness.

Allah Most High says:

“If only they had been content with what Allah and His Messenger gave them and had said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us. Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger. To Allah alone we turn in longing.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“So when you have finished, strive diligently.”

“And to your Lord alone turn in longing.”

He, glorified and exalted, thus made clear that these people should have been content with what Allah and His Messenger gave them and should have said, “Allah is sufficient for us. Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger. To Allah alone we turn in longing.” Thus He mentioned

---

## Page 295

contentment with what Allah and His Messenger have given, because the Messenger is the intermediary between us and Allah in conveying His commands and prohibitions, what He makes lawful and unlawful, and His promises and warnings. Thus, what is lawful is what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful; what is unlawful is what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden; and religion is what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed.

For this reason, Allah Most High says:

“Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it; and whatever he forbids you, refrain from it.”

No one may take any wealth except what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful. As for funds in which a person has a shared entitlement, such as fay’ revenues, war booty, and alms, he must be content with what Allah and His Messenger have given him from them—that is, his rightful share—and not seek anything beyond it.

Then Allah Most High says:

“And had said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us.’”

He did not say, “and His Messenger,” for sufficiency means being enough, and Allah alone suffices His believing servants. As Allah Most High says:

“O Prophet, Allah is sufficient for you and for the believers who follow you.”

That is, He alone is sufficient for you and for the believers who follow you. This is the correct interpretation, held by the majority of the early and later generations, as explained elsewhere.

The meaning is that Allah suffices the Messenger and those who follow him. Everyone who follows the Messenger, therefore, has Allah to suffice him, guide him, help him, and provide for him. Then Allah Most High says:

“Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger.”

He thus ascribed giving to Allah and His Messenger, but placed the mention of bounty between them, for bounty belongs to Allah alone, saying:

“Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger.”

Then Allah Most High says:

“To Allah alone we turn in longing.”

He thus directed longing to Allah alone, not to the Messenger or any other created being. It is therefore clear that Allah has placed all created beings on an equal footing with respect to these rulings. He has not accorded to any of

---

## Page 296

the created beings—whether a prophet or an angel—the right to be sworn by, relied upon, turned to in longing, feared, or regarded with reverent fear.

Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those whom you claim to be gods besides Allah. They possess not an atom’s weight in the heavens or on earth, nor do they have any share in either, nor does He have any helper among them.’”

“Intercession is of no avail with Him except for those to whom He gives permission.”

Thus, He—glory be to Him—has warned those who invoke anything besides Allah, making clear that these beings possess nothing alongside Allah, have no share in His dominion, and that He has no assistant or helper among created beings.

He has thereby severed the hearts’ attachment to created beings in longing, fear, worship, and seeking help. He has left only intercession, which is real; but Allah Most High says:

“Intercession is of no avail with Him except for those to whom He gives permission.”

The authentic hadiths concerning intercession on the Day of Resurrection likewise indicate this:

“When people come to Adam and to the messengers of firm resolve—Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus son of Mary—each directs them to the next, until they come to the Messiah. He says to them, ‘Go to Muhammad, a servant whose past and future sins Allah has forgiven.’

“He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘They will then come to me, and I will go to my Lord. When I see Him, I will fall down in prostration and praise my Lord with words of praise that He will inspire me to utter, which I cannot now express. Then I will be told, “O Muhammad, raise your head. Speak, and you will be heard; ask, and you will be given; intercede, and your intercession will be accepted.”’ He said: ‘He will then set a limit for me, and I will admit them to Paradise.’”

He then related the remainder of the account. Thus, the Messiah made clear that Muhammad is the intercessor whose intercession is accepted because he is a servant whose past and future sins Allah has forgiven. And Muhammad, Allah’s servant and Messenger, explained—being the best of creation and the intercessor of highest standing

---

## Page 297

and the most honored of them before Allah Most High—that he will come, prostrate himself, and praise Allah. He will not begin interceding until permission is granted to him and he is told:

“Raise your head. Ask, and you will be given; intercede, and your intercession will be accepted.”

He also mentioned that his Lord will set a limit for him, and he will admit them to Paradise. All of this makes clear that the matter belongs entirely to Allah. He is the One who honors the intercessor by granting him permission to intercede, and the intercessor intercedes only for those for whom Allah permits him to do so. Then He sets a limit for the intercessor, and he admits them to Paradise. Thus, the matter rests upon His will, power, and choice. The intercessor of highest standing and greatest excellence before Him is the one whom He has favored above others, chosen, and selected for his perfect servitude, obedience, turning to Him, and agreement with his Lord concerning what He loves and approves.

If the rulings concerning swearing by anything other than Allah, directing one’s longing to it, reverently fearing it, guarding against its displeasure, and the like apply equally to all created beings, then no created being may be sworn by, regarded with such fear, or relied upon—even if it is the best of all created beings. None of the angels or prophets is entitled to this, let alone any of the shaykhs or righteous people.

As for asking Allah Most High by invoking created things: if this is on the grounds that He has sworn by them and honored them, then asking by invoking all such things would be permissible. If it is not permissible, then one may not ask by invoking any of them. Distinguishing in this matter between one person who venerates and another is like the distinction made by someone who claims that it is permissible to swear by some created things but not others. Just as the latter distinction is invalid, so too is the former.

If someone were to distinguish between that in which one must believe and that in which one need not believe, he would be told: One must believe in the angels and the prophets, and believe in everything the Messenger has conveyed, such as Munkar and Nakīr and the maidens

---

## Page 298

with beautiful, wide eyes, the youths, and other such things. Is it permissible to swear by these created beings because one must believe in them? Or is it likewise permissible to ask by invoking them?

It is therefore clear that, when asking by invoking a means, if what is invoked is not a means by which the supplication is answered, there is no distinction between invoking one created being and another, just as there is no distinction between swearing by one created being and another. All of this is impermissible. Thus, its impermissibility is clear, as those scholars who held this view have stated. And Allah knows best.

As for the words of Allah Most High:

“Previously, they used to pray for victory against those who disbelieved,”

the Jews used to say to the polytheists: “This Prophet will soon be sent, and we shall fight you alongside him and kill you.” They were not adjuring Allah by the Prophet himself, nor asking Him by invoking him. Or they would say:

“O Allah, send this unlettered Prophet so that we may follow him and kill these people alongside him.”

This is the established account among the scholars of Quranic exegesis, and it is what the Quran indicates, for Allah Most High said:

“Previously, they used to pray for victory.”

Praying for victory means seeking help to prevail: asking for triumph and victory. Seeking triumph and victory through him meant asking that he be sent, so that they could fight their enemies alongside him and thereby gain victory. It did not mean swearing by him or asking by invoking him. Had that been the case, they would have gained victory whenever they asked by invoking him or swore by him. Yet that was not what happened. Rather, when Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah granted those who believed in him and fought alongside him victory over those who opposed him.

What some commentators have mentioned—that they used to swear by him or ask by invoking him—is an anomalous report that contradicts the many widely transmitted accounts to the contrary. We have mentioned some of these in “Proofs of Prophethood” and in the book “Seeking Help

---

## Page 299

—the Larger Work.” Works of biography, proofs of prophethood, and Quranic exegesis are replete with such accounts.

Abū al-Āliya and others said:

“When the Jews sought victory through Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, over the Arab polytheists, they would say: ‘O Allah, send this Prophet whom we find mentioned in our scriptures, so that we may overcome the polytheists and kill them.’

“But when Allah sent Muhammad and they saw that he was not from among them, they disbelieved in him out of envy of the Arabs, although they knew that he was Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Allah Most High then revealed these verses:

‘But when there came to them that which they recognized, they disbelieved in it; so the curse of Allah is upon the disbelievers.’”

Muhammad ibn Ishāq narrated from Āsim ibn Umar ibn Qatāda al-Ansārī, from men of his people, who said:

“Among the things that led us to Islam—along with Allah’s mercy and guidance—was what we used to hear from Jewish men. We were polytheists and idol worshippers, whereas they were People of the Scripture, possessing knowledge that we did not have. Hostilities between us and them were constant.

“Whenever we inflicted something upon them that they disliked, they would tell us: ‘The time of a Prophet who is about to be sent has drawn near. With him, we shall slaughter you as Ād and Iram were slaughtered.’ We often heard this from them.

“When Allah sent Muhammad as a Messenger from Him, we responded to him when he called us to Allah, and recognized him as the one they had threatened us with. We hastened to him ahead of them and believed in him, whereas they disbelieved in him. These verses in al-Baqara were revealed concerning us and them:

‘And when there came to them a Book from Allah confirming that which was with them—although previously they used to pray for victory against those who disbelieved—but when there came to them that which they recognized, they disbelieved in it; so the curse of Allah is upon the disbelievers.’”

Ibn Abī Hātim and others who collected the statements of the early Quranic commentators mentioned only this account. It contains no report from any of the early generations of asking Allah by invoking him. Rather, they mentioned announcing his coming or asking Allah to send him. Thus, Ibn Abī Hātim narrated from Abū Razīn, from al-Dahhāk,

---

## Page 300

from Ibn Abbās concerning the words of Allah Most High:
