In the two Sahīh collections, Ā’ishah relates that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever introduces into this matter of ours something that does not belong to it, it is rejected.”

Another wording in the Sahīh reads:

“Whoever performs a deed that does not accord with our teaching, it is rejected.”

It is also reported in the Sahīh and elsewhere that Allah Most High says:

“I have no need whatsoever of partners. Whoever performs a deed in which he associates someone else with Me, I disown it, and it belongs entirely to the one he associated with Me.”

For this reason, the jurists have said that acts of worship must be based on revealed prescription. Thus, the two Sahīh collections relate that Umar ibn al-Khattāb kissed the Black Stone and said:

“By Allah, I know that you are a stone that can neither harm nor benefit. Had I not seen the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, kiss you, I would not have kissed you.”

Allah, glorified is He, has commanded us to follow the Messenger, obey him, remain loyal to him and love him, and to hold Allah and His Messenger dearer than all else. He has promised us His love and honour through our obedience to and love for His Messenger. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive you your sins.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“If you obey him, you will be guided.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, He will admit them into gardens beneath which rivers flow, to abide there forever. That is the great triumph.”

There are many similar passages in the Quran.

In this matter, no one should depart from what the Sunnah has established and the revealed Law has prescribed

---

## Page 337

and what the Book and Sunnah indicate and the early generations of the Muslim community followed. One should affirm what one knows and refrain from speaking about what one does not know, neither pursuing that of which one has no knowledge nor saying anything about Allah without knowledge, for Allah Most High has forbidden all of this.

The prophetic hadiths mention the grounds upon which he invoked Allah Most High, as in his words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“O Allah, I ask You by virtue of all praise belonging to You. There is no god but You, the Bestower of blessings, the Originator of the heavens and the earth. O Possessor of Majesty and Honour, O Ever-Living, O Sustainer of all!”

This was reported by Abū Dāwūd and others. Another wording reads:

“O Allah, I ask You by virtue of my testimony that You are Allah: there is no god but You, the One, the Eternal Refuge, who neither begets nor is born, and to whom none is equal.”

This was reported by Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasā’ī and Ibn Mājah.

The scholars agree that an oath sworn by anything other than Allah Most High—that is, by created beings or things—is not legally binding. Thus, if someone swears by the Kaaba, the angels, the prophets, any of the shaykhs, or kings, his oath is not binding. Such swearing is not prescribed; rather, it is forbidden, either as strictly unlawful or as merely disliked. The scholars hold two views on this, and the correct view is that it is strictly unlawful.

An authentic hadith relates that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath should swear by Allah or remain silent.”

Al-Tirmidhī also reports that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears by anything other than Allah has associated a partner with Him.”

None of the early scholars held that an oath sworn by any prophet is binding, except in the case of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Two opinions are reported from Ahmad concerning whether an oath sworn by him is binding. Some of Ahmad’s followers, such as Ibn Aqīl, extended this disagreement to all the other prophets, but this is a weak view. The underlying position that an oath sworn by the Prophet is binding is itself weak and anomalous, and none of the scholars has held it

---

## Page 338

as far as we know. The majority—including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Abū Hanīfa—hold that an oath sworn by him is not legally binding, as does one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad. This is the correct view.

The same applies to seeking refuge in created beings or things: refuge is to be sought only in the Creator Most High, His names and His attributes. Thus, among the proofs that the early scholars—including Ahmad and others—adduced to establish that Allah’s speech is uncreated were the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I seek refuge in Allah’s perfect words.”

They said: He sought refuge in them, whereas refuge is not to be sought in anything created.

An authentic hadith reports that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no harm in incantations so long as they involve no association of partners with Allah.”

He thus prohibited incantations involving such association, including those in which refuge is sought in the jinn. Allah Most High says:

“And there were men among mankind who sought refuge in men among the jinn, so they increased them in transgression.”

For this reason, the scholars prohibited the incantatory formulas and adjurations involving association of partners with Allah that some people use for those afflicted by possession and others. Indeed, they prohibited all such formulas whose meaning is unknown, for fear that they might involve such association. This is unlike prescribed incantations, which are permissible. Accordingly, one may swear an oath—whether simply swearing or adjuring another—only by Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and may seek refuge only in Allah, Mighty and Majestic.

One who asks Allah by invoking something other than Allah is either adjuring Him or seeking what he asks for through that means—as the three men in the cave sought to draw near to Allah through their deeds, and as one seeks to draw near to Him through the supplications of the prophets and the righteous.

---

## Page 339

If, then, he is adjuring Allah by something other than Him, this is not permissible. If, however, he is asking through a means that leads to what is sought—such as deeds involving obedience to Allah and His Messenger, including faith in the Messenger, love for him, allegiance to him, and the like—then this is permissible.

If he is asking merely by invoking the persons of the prophets and the righteous, this is not prescribed. A number of scholars have prohibited it, saying that it is impermissible, while others have allowed it. The former view is stronger, as explained above. This is asking through a means that does not lead to obtaining what is sought, unlike asking through a means that does lead to it, such as asking Him, glorified be He, through the supplications of the righteous and through righteous deeds. This is permissible, because the supplications of the righteous are a means of obtaining what we seek and for which they have prayed. Likewise, righteous deeds are a means of obtaining Allah’s reward for us. When we seek to draw near to Him through their supplications and our deeds, we are approaching Him Most High through a means of doing so, as He Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and seek the means of drawing near to Him.”

The means of drawing near are righteous deeds. He Most High also says:

“Those whom they invoke seek the means of drawing near to their Lord.”

But if we do not seek to draw near to Him, glorified be He, through their supplications or our deeds, and instead invoke their persons alone, their persons are not in themselves a means that leads to our supplications being answered. We would thus be seeking to draw near through something that is not a means of doing so. For this reason, this practice has not been authentically transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor is it widely reported from the early generations.

A supplication containing a request made by invoking the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has been reported from Ahmad in al-Marwadhī’s *Pilgrimage Rites*. This may be understood in terms of one of the two opinions reported from him concerning the permissibility of swearing by the Prophet.

---

## Page 340

and most scholars hold that both practices are prohibited. There is no doubt that they hold great honour with Allah—as He Most High has said concerning Moses and Jesus, peace be upon them both, as mentioned earlier. However, their stations and ranks with Allah benefit them themselves; we benefit from this by following them and loving them. Thus, seeking to draw near to Allah Most High through our faith in His Prophet, our love for him, our allegiance to him, and our adherence to his sunnah is the greatest means of drawing near. As for invoking his person alone, without seeking to draw near through faith in him and obedience to him, this cannot serve as a means. If someone invokes a created being without seeking to draw near through faith in that being or obedience to him, then by what means is he seeking to draw near?

When a person approaches another through an intermediary, he may ask the intermediary to intercede for him with that person. For example, he might say to the man’s father, his friend, or someone he holds dear: “Intercede for us with him.” This is permissible. Alternatively, he may adjure the person, saying, for example: “By the life of your son so-and-so, by the grave of your father so-and-so, and by the honour of your teacher so-and-so,” and similar expressions. Adjuring Allah Most High by created beings is impermissible, as is adjuring one created being by another. Or he may make his request on grounds conducive to obtaining what he seeks, as Allah Most High says:

“And fear Allah, in whose name you appeal to one another, and honour the ties of kinship.”

This will be explained below. It has already become clear that adjuring Allah, glorified be He, by anything other than Him is impermissible, and that swearing by a created being is never permissible. As for seeking to draw near to Him through the intercession of those granted permission to intercede, this is permissible. The blind man had asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to supplicate for him, just as the Companions asked him

---

## Page 341

to pray for rain. The words, “I turn to You through Your Prophet Muhammad, the Prophet of mercy,” mean through his supplication and intercession for me. Hence the hadith concludes: “O Allah, accept his intercession for me.” The permissibility of what this hadith describes is agreed upon; it is not the matter under discussion here. Allah Most High has said:

“And fear Allah, in whose name you appeal to one another, and honour the ties of kinship.”

According to the majority’s reading, in which “ties of kinship” is in the accusative, people appeal to one another in Allah’s name alone, not on the grounds of kinship. Their appeals in Allah’s name include adjuring one another by Allah and making covenants with one another in His name.

As for the reading in the genitive, a group of the early generations explained it as referring to their saying: “I ask you by Allah and by our ties of kinship.” This describes how they make their requests, and it may be argued that it does not establish the permissibility of doing so. If it does establish its permissibility, then “I ask you by our ties of kinship” does not mean adjuring someone by kinship—such adjuration is impermissible here—but rather making a request on the grounds of kinship. That is, kinship gives relatives rights over one another. This is like the three men asking Allah Most High through their righteous deeds, or our asking through the supplication and intercession of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Also in this category is the report concerning the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib: whenever his nephew, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar, asked him on the grounds of Jaʿfar’s right, he would give to him. This was not a form of adjuration, for adjuring by someone other than Jaʿfar would be greater still. Rather, it was an appeal to the rights of kinship, for Allah’s right became binding only through Jaʿfar, and Jaʿfar had a right over ʿAlī.

Also in this category is the hadith narrated by Ibn Mājah from Abū Saʿīd, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning the supplication of one setting out for prayer:

“O Allah, I ask You by the right that those who ask of You have upon You, and by the right of this walk of mine, for I have not set out in boastfulness or arrogance, nor to show off or gain a reputation

---

## Page 342

but I have set out to avoid Your wrath and seek Your pleasure. I ask You to save me from the Fire and forgive my sins, for none forgives sins but You.”

The chain of transmission of this hadith includes 'Atiyya al-'Awfī, and there is some weakness in it. If these are the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then it belongs to this category for two reasons:

— (First)

It contains a request to Allah Most High by the right of those who ask of Him and by the right of those who walk in obedience to Him. The right of those who ask is that He answer them, and the right of those who walk is that He reward them. This is a right that Allah Most High has made binding upon Himself; no created being can impose any obligation upon the Creator, Most High. Among the examples of this are His words:

“Your Lord has prescribed mercy for Himself.”

And His words:

“And it is incumbent upon Us to help the believers.”

And His words:

“A promise binding upon Him in the Torah, the Gospel and the Quran. And who is more faithful to his covenant than Allah?”

In the authentic collection, the hadith of Mu'ādh states:

“Allah’s right over His servants is that they worship Him and associate nothing with Him; and the servants’ right upon Allah, if they do so, is that He not punish them.”

The authentic collection also records, from Abū Dharr, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, related from his Lord, Blessed and Most High, that He said:

“O My servants, I have forbidden injustice for Myself and made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another.”

If the right of those who ask of Him and worship Him consists in His answering and rewarding them, then asking Allah on that basis is asking Him by His own actions. This is like seeking refuge through such actions, as in the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I seek refuge in Your pleasure from Your wrath, and in Your protection from Your punishment. I seek refuge in You from You. I cannot adequately praise You

---

## Page 343

; You are as You have praised Yourself.” Seeking refuge in His protection, which is His act, is thus like asking Him by His granting of reward, which is also His act.

Al-Tabarānī narrated in Kitāb al-Du‘ā’ (The Book of Supplication), from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that Allah says:

“O My servant, there are only four matters: one is Mine, one is yours, one is between Me and you, and one is between you and My creation. The one that is Mine is that you worship Me without associating anything with Me. As for the one that is yours, I shall reward you for it when you are most in need of it. The one between Me and you is that you supplicate and I answer. The one between you and My creation is that you treat people as you would like them to treat you.”

The division in this hadith, expressed in the words, “One is Mine, and one is yours,” resembles the division in the hadith concerning al-Fātihah, in which Allah Most High says:

“I have divided the prayer between Myself and My servant into two halves: one half is Mine, and the other half is My servant’s, and My servant shall have what he asks for.”

The servant benefits from both halves, and Allah Most High loves both halves. Yet He, glorified be He, loves to be worshipped; the assistance and guidance He grants the servant are a means to that end. He therefore loves them because they are a path to worshipping Him. The servant first seeks what he needs: he needs assistance in worship and guidance to the straight path, and through these he attains worship. There are further matters connected with this that would require a lengthy discussion, but this is not the place for it, even if we have digressed from our intended subject.

(The second reason)

Supplicating to Him, glorified and exalted be He, and performing deeds for His sake are means by which the servant attains what he seeks. This is therefore like seeking a means of approach to Allah through the supplication of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the righteous members of his community. It has already been explained that supplication through the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or a righteous person either entails swearing an oath by him

---

## Page 344

or using him as a means. If the words “by the right of those who ask You” constitute an oath, then one may adjure Allah only by Allah Himself. If they invoke a means, then that means is something He, glorified be He, has Himself made a means: supplicating to Him and worshipping Him. All these cases are similar. None involves supplicating to Him by means of a created being without either supplication on that being’s part or a righteous deed on ours.

When a supplicant says, “I ask You by the right of the angels, or by the right of the prophets and the right of the righteous,” yet does not say to another person, “I adjure you by their right”—if he may neither swear by that right nor adjure a created being by it, how can he adjure the Creator by it? If he is not swearing by it but merely invoking it as a means, the persons themselves do not constitute a means that ensures the attainment of his aim. Rather, there must be a means on his part, such as faith in the angels and the prophets, or on their part, such as their supplication.

Yet many people have become accustomed to this, just as they have become accustomed to swearing by them. One of them will even say, “By the right you have upon Allah, and by the right this gray hair has upon Allah!”

If someone says, “‘I ask You by the right of so-and-so, or by his standing’ means ‘I ask You by my faith in him and my love for him,’ and this is among the greatest means of approach,” the reply is:

For anyone who intends this meaning, it is a sound meaning. But this is not what most of these people intend. Whoever says, “I ask You by my faith in You and Your Messenger,” or something similar, or “by my faith in Your Messenger and my love for him,” or something similar, has done well. As Allah Most High says concerning the believers’ supplication:

“Our Lord, we have heard a caller calling to faith, ‘Believe in your Lord,’ and we have believed. Our Lord, forgive us our sins, remove from us our misdeeds, and let us die among the righteous.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Those who say, ‘Our Lord, we have indeed believed, so forgive us our sins and protect us from the punishment of the Fire.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“Indeed, a group of My servants used to say, ‘Our Lord, we have believed, so forgive us and have mercy upon us, and You are

---

## Page 345

the best of the merciful.’” And He, Most High, says:

“Our Lord, we have believed in what You have revealed and have followed the Messenger, so write us down among the witnesses.”

Ibn Mas‘ūd used to say:

“O Allah, You commanded me, and I obeyed; You called me, and I answered. Now it is the hour before dawn, so forgive me.”

Another example of this is the hadith of the three men who were caught in the rain and took shelter in a cave. A rock sealed them inside, so they supplicated to Allah, glorified be He, by means of their righteous deeds, and He delivered them. This report is authentically recorded in the two Sahīh collections.

Abū Bakr ibn Abī al-Dunyā said:

Khālid ibn Khirāsh al-‘Ajlānī and Ismā‘īl ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us, saying: Sālih al-Murrī narrated to us, from Thābit, from Anas, who said:

“We visited a man of the Helpers who was gravely ill, and we stayed until he died. We spread his garment over him. His elderly mother was sitting by his head, and one of us turned to her and said, ‘O woman, seek Allah’s reward for your affliction.’

“She asked, ‘What has happened? Has my son died?’

“We replied, ‘Yes.’

“She asked, ‘Is what you say really true?’

“We replied, ‘Yes.’

“She stretched out her hands to Allah and said, ‘O Allah, You know that I embraced Islam and emigrated to Your Messenger, hoping that You would grant me relief after every hardship. Do not lay this affliction upon me today.’”

He said: “Then she uncovered his face, and we did not leave until we had eaten with him.”

It is reported in Abū Nu‘aym’s Hilya:

“David said, ‘By the right my forefathers—Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob—have upon You.’ Allah Most High then revealed to him, ‘O David, what right do your forefathers have upon Me?’”

Although this is not evidence recognized by Islamic law, Israelite reports may be used as corroboration, but not relied upon.

---

## Page 346

The established Sunnah is that a living person may be asked to supplicate, just as he may be asked for anything else within his power. As for an absent or deceased created being, nothing is to be asked of him.

This matter becomes clear when we recognize that seeking access to Allah through him and turning to Allah through him are expressions that are ambiguous and bear different meanings according to usage. In the Companions’ usage, they mean asking him to supplicate and intercede, so that they seek access to Allah and turn to Him through his supplication and intercession. His supplication and intercession—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—are among the greatest means of drawing near to Allah, Mighty and Majestic.

In the usage of many people, however, they mean asking Allah Most High and adjuring Him by the Prophet’s person. Allah Most High may not be adjured by any created thing; indeed, created things may not be sworn by under any circumstances. Thus, one must not say, “I adjure You, my Lord, by Your angels,” or “by Your Kaaba,” or “by Your righteous servants,” just as a person may not swear by these things. Rather, one may swear only by Allah Most High, by His names and attributes. Hence, the Sunnah is to ask Allah Most High by His names and attributes, saying:

“I ask You by the fact that all praise belongs to You. There is no god but You, the Bestower, the Originator of the heavens and the earth. O Possessor of Majesty and Honour, O Ever-Living, O Sustainer of all! And I ask You by the fact that You are Allah, the One, the Eternal Refuge, who neither begets nor is born, and to whom none is equal. And I ask You by every name that belongs to You with which You have named Yourself”—and so on, as related in the hadith transmitted in the Sunnah.

As for asking Allah and adjuring Him by His created things, this has no foundation in the religion of Islam. The same applies to saying:

“O Allah, I ask You by the seats of might in Your Throne, by the utmost extent of mercy in Your Book, by Your greatest name, by Your supreme majesty, and by Your perfect words.”

---

## Page 347

However, scholars hold two opinions on whether this third supplication may be used. Shaykh Abū al-Hasan al-Qudūrī said in his book entitled Sharh al-Karkhī (Commentary on al-Karkhī):

Bishr ibn al-Walīd said: “I heard Abū Yūsuf say: ‘Abū Hanīfah said that no one should supplicate to Allah except by Him, and that he disapproved of saying, “By the seats of might in Your Throne,” or, “By the right of Your creation.”’”

This is also the position of Abū Yūsuf. Abū Yūsuf said: “‘The seat of might in His Throne’ is Allah, so I do not disapprove of this. But I disapprove of saying, ‘By the right of Your prophets and messengers, and by the right of the House and the Sacred Monument.’”

Al-Qudūrī said: “Asking Him by His creation is not permissible, because created beings have no right over the Creator; thus, it is not permissible”—meaning that this is agreed upon. These statements of Abū Hanīfah, Abū Yūsuf and others imply that asking Allah by anything other than Him is prohibited.

If it is said: “The Lord, glorified and exalted is He, swears by whatever He wills of His created things, whereas we may adjure Him only by Himself. Why, then, should it not be said that He may be adjured by His created things, while a created being may be adjured only by the Creator Most High?”

The answer is that His swearing by His created things is a form of praising and extolling Him and mentioning His signs. Our swearing by them, however, is associating partners with Allah when we do so to urge someone to act, to restrain him, or to affirm or deny a report.

When someone says to another, “I ask you by such-and-such,” he may be swearing an oath, in which case this is not permissible by anything other than Allah Most High. Responsibility for expiation in this case lies with the person swearing, not the person whom he adjures, as the leading jurists have explicitly stated. If he is not swearing an oath, then it is a request, and neither of them owes any expiation for it.

It is thus clear that someone who asks Allah by His creation is either swearing by a created being—which is not permissible—or asking Him by that being, a matter whose details have already been explained. And if he says, “By Allah, I do such-and-such,”

---

## Page 348

neither of them owes any expiation for it. If he says, “I adjure you by Allah: you must do this,” or, “By Allah, you must do this,” and the other person does not fulfil his oath, then the person who swore must make expiation.

When someone supplicates in the form of a request, this falls under asking by Him. But if he adjures Allah Most High—for example, saying, “I adjure You, my Lord: You must do such-and-such,” as al-Barā’ ibn Mālik and others among the early generations used to do—it is established in the authentic collection that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Many a dishevelled, dust-covered man, wearing two threadbare garments and turned away from people’s doors, would have his oath fulfilled by Allah if he adjured Him.”

It is also reported in the authentic collection that when Anas ibn al-Nadr said, “By Him who sent you with the truth, do not break al-Rubayyi‘’s front tooth,” the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, replied, “Anas, Allah’s Book prescribes retaliation.” The people then pardoned her, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Among Allah’s servants are those whose oath Allah would fulfil if they adjured Him.”

This is a matter of swearing by Allah, saying, “You must do this.” It is therefore adjuring Allah Most High by Himself, not adjuring Him by a created being.

People should supplicate using the prescribed supplications found in the Quran and Sunnah. There is no doubt about their merit and excellence, or that this is the straight path: the path of those whom Allah has blessed—the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs and the righteous. What excellent companions they are!

It has already been explained that the saying some ordinary people attribute to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“If you have a need, ask Allah by my standing with Him,”

is a false hadith. No scholar has transmitted it, nor does it appear in any hadith collection. What is prescribed, rather, is to invoke blessings upon him during supplication. Thus, when scholars discussed supplication when seeking rain and on other occasions, they mentioned invoking blessings upon him. Among the practices prescribed for Muslims in this situation, they did not mention seeking a means of approach to Allah through him, just as none of the scholars mentioned

---

## Page 349

invoking anyone other than Allah or seeking help without qualification from anyone other than Him in any circumstances, although there is a distinction between the two. Invoking anyone other than Allah is disbelief. Hence, invoking any of the dead or absent—whether prophets or others—has not been reported from any of the early generations or the leading scholars. It was mentioned only by certain later writers who were not leading scholars qualified to exercise independent legal judgment.

This differs from saying, “I ask You by our Prophet’s standing,” or “by his right.” Some earlier scholars are reported to have done this, but it was not widespread among them, nor is there any Sunnah from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, supporting it. Rather, the Sunnah indicates that it is prohibited, as has been reported from Abū Hanīfa, Abū Yūsuf and others.

I found the following statement in the fatwas of the jurist Abū Muhammad ibn ‘Abd al-Salām:

“It is not permissible to seek a means of approach to Allah through any of His creatures except through the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, provided that the hadith of the blind man is authentic.”

Its authenticity was not known. I subsequently found reports from Abū Hanīfa, Abū Yūsuf and other scholars stating:

“It is not permissible to adjure Allah by any of the prophets.”

I also found in Imam Ahmad’s statements that he permitted this in the case of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This may, however, be derived from one of the two opinions reported from him concerning the permissibility of swearing by him.

It has already been explained that this hadith indicates only seeking a means of approach to Allah through the Prophet’s supplication. It does not concern adjuring Allah Most High by a created being, nor asking by the Messenger’s person, as explained earlier.

Those who seek a means of approach through his person so that their supplications may be accepted have turned away from what they were commanded to do and what was prescribed for them—something among the most beneficial practices for them—to something that is not so. Invoking blessings upon him is among the greatest means by which supplication is answered, and Allah has commanded it.

Invoking blessings upon him during supplication is what the Quran, Sunnah and scholarly consensus establish. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah and His angels send blessings upon the Prophet. O you who believe, send blessings upon him and salute him with greetings of peace.”

---

## Page 350

It is also reported in the authentic collection that he said:

“Whoever invokes blessings upon me once, Allah will bless him ten times.”

Fadāla ibn ‘Ubayd, a Companion of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, reported:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, heard a man supplicating during his prayer without praising Allah or invoking blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said, ‘This man has been hasty.’ Then he called him and said to him—or to someone else—‘When one of you prays, let him begin by praising his Lord, then invoke blessings upon the Prophet, and then supplicate for whatever he wishes.’”

Reported by Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd—whose wording this is—al-Tirmidhī and al-Nasā’ī. Al-Tirmidhī said: “An authentic hadith.”

In Sahīh Muslim, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās reported that he heard the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say:

“When you hear the caller to prayer, repeat what he says, then invoke blessings upon me. Whoever invokes blessings upon me once, Allah will bless him ten times. Then ask Allah to grant me al-wasīla, for it is a rank in Paradise befitting only one of Allah’s servants, and I hope to be that servant. Whoever asks Allah to grant me al-wasīla will be entitled to intercession.”

In the Sunan collections of Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī, he also reported:

“A man said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, those who give the call to prayer surpass us.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Repeat what they say. Then, when you have finished, ask, and you will be given what you ask for.’”

In the Musnad, Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh said:

“Whoever says, when the caller gives the call, ‘O Allah, Lord of this enduring call and beneficial prayer, send blessings upon Muhammad and be pleased with him with a pleasure never followed by displeasure,’ Allah will answer his supplication?”

Anas ibn Mālik reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Supplication between the call to prayer and the call announcing its commencement is not rejected.”

Reported by Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī and al-Nasā’ī. Al-Tirmidhī said: “A sound hadith.”

---

## Page 351

Sahl ibn Sa‘d reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There are two times when the gates of heaven are opened and a supplicant’s supplication is rarely rejected: when the call to prayer is made and when the ranks are formed in Allah’s cause.”

Reported by Abū Dāwūd.

In the Musnad, al-Tirmidhī and other collections, al-Tufayl ibn Ubayy ibn Ka‘b reported from his father:

“When a quarter of the night had passed, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would rise and say, ‘O people, remember Allah! The quaking blast has come, followed by the next. Death has come with all that it brings.’”

Ubayy said:

“I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I frequently invoke blessings upon you. How much of my prayer should I devote to you?’ He replied, ‘As much as you wish.’ I asked, ‘A quarter?’ He replied, ‘As much as you wish; if you increase it, that is better for you.’ I said, ‘Half?’ He replied, ‘As much as you wish; if you increase it, that is better for you.’ I asked, ‘Two-thirds?’ He replied, ‘As much as you wish; if you increase it, that is better for you.’ I asked, ‘Should I devote all my prayer to you?’ He replied, ‘Then Allah will take care of whatever concerns you in the affairs of this world and the Hereafter.’ In another wording: ‘Then your worries will be taken care of and your sins forgiven.’”

By the questioner’s words, “Should I devote part of my prayer to you?”, he meant “part of my supplication,” for prayer in Arabic means supplication. Allah Most High said:

“And pray for them; indeed, your prayer is a reassurance for them.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, send blessings upon the family of Abū Awfā.”

A woman said, “Invoke blessings upon me, O Messenger of Allah, and upon my husband.” He replied, “May Allah bless you and your husband.”

Thus, what the questioner meant was: “O Messenger of Allah, I have a supplication that I make, through which I seek to obtain

---

## Page 352

good and ward off evil. How much of my supplication should I devote to you?” He replied:

“As much as you wish.”

When he went on to ask, “Should I devote all my prayer to you?”, he replied:

“Then your worries will be taken care of and your sins forgiven.”

In the other wording:

“Then Allah will take care of whatever concerns you in the affairs of this world and the Hereafter.”

This encompasses everything a person seeks through supplication: obtaining good and averting harm. Supplication brings about what is sought and wards off what is feared, as has been explained at length in the relevant discussions.

The scholars of Islam and the leading authorities in religion have set out the prescribed supplications and shunned innovated supplications. Their example should therefore be followed.

There are three levels in this matter:

— The first is to invoke someone other than Allah who is dead or absent, whether among the prophets and the righteous or anyone else, saying: “O my master so-and-so, rescue me,” “I seek your protection,” “I seek your help,” “Help me against my enemy,” or something similar. This is associating partners with Allah.

When someone seeks help from created beings, a devil may fulfill his need, or part of it, and may appear to him in the form of the person whose help he sought. He then imagines that this is a miraculous favor bestowed upon the one whose help he sought. In reality, it is only a devil that has entered him and led him astray because he associated partners with Allah, just as a devil speaks through idols, through a possessed person, and in other ways.

Such things occur frequently in our time and in other times. I know more instances than could be described briefly involving people who sought help from me or from others. They related that someone resembling me or someone else came to them and fulfilled their needs. They imagined that this was due to the blessing of seeking help from me or from others, whereas it was only a devil that misled them and led them astray.

This is the origin of idol worship and of taking partners alongside Allah Most High in the earliest generations of the past, as has been established. Thus, such a person has associated partners with Allah. We seek refuge in Allah from that.

---

## Page 353

And graver still is to say, “Forgive me and accept my repentance,” as some ignorant people who associate partners with Allah do.

Graver still is to prostrate oneself to his grave, pray facing it, and regard prayer in that direction as better than facing the prescribed direction of prayer. Some even say, “This is the prayer direction of the elite, while the Kaaba is the prayer direction of the common people.”

Graver still is to regard travelling to it as a form of Hajj, even saying that several journeys to it equal one Hajj. Their extremists say, “Visiting it once is better than performing the pilgrimage to the House many times,” and other such things. This is associating them with Allah, even though many people fall into some of these practices.

The second level is to say to a dead or absent prophet or righteous person, “Supplicate to Allah for me,” “Supplicate to your Lord for us,” or “Ask Allah for us,” as Christians say to Mary and others. No scholar doubts that this too is impermissible and that it is among the innovations never practised by any of the early generations of the Muslim community. Nevertheless, greeting the inhabitants of graves and addressing them are permissible. When his Companions visited graves, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would teach them to say:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these abodes, believers and Muslims. Allah willing, we shall join you. May Allah forgive us and you. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of their reward, do not put us to trial after them, and forgive us and them.”

Abū ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Barr narrated that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“No man passes the grave of someone he knew in this world and greets him without Allah restoring the deceased’s soul to him so that he may return the greeting.”

---

## Page 354

And in the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is reported to have said:

“No Muslim greets me without Allah restoring my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

However, asking the dead to supplicate, or asking anything else of them, is not prescribed.

In Mālik’s Muwatta’, it is reported that Ibn Umar would say:

“Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, O my father.”

Then he would leave.

Abd Allāh ibn Dīnār said:

“I saw Abd Allāh ibn Umar stand at the grave of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, invoke blessings upon the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and supplicate for Abū Bakr and Umar.”

Likewise, it is reported that Anas ibn Mālik and others would greet the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. When they wished to supplicate, they would face the direction of prayer and supplicate to Allah Most High; they would not supplicate while facing the burial chamber. Although some groups of jurists, Sufis, and ordinary people whose views carry no weight have fallen into some of these practices, no imam whose opinions are followed, nor anyone held in general esteem throughout the Muslim community, has endorsed this.

The position of the four imams—Mālik, Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad—and other imams of Islam is that when a man greets the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and wishes to supplicate for himself, he should face the direction of prayer. They differed regarding the direction to face when greeting him. The three—Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad—said that he should face the burial chamber and greet him from in front of him. Abū Hanīfa said that he should not face the burial chamber when greeting him, just as, by their unanimous agreement, he should not face it when supplicating.

There are then two opinions within his school:

---

## Page 355

according to one opinion, he should turn his back to the burial chamber; according to another, he should keep it to his left. This is their disagreement concerning the greeting. As for supplication, they did not disagree that he should face only the direction of prayer, not the burial chamber.

The account related about Mālik—that, when al-Mansūr asked him about facing the burial chamber, he instructed him to do so and said, “He is your means of approach and that of your father Adam”—is a fabrication attributed to Mālik. It has no known chain of transmission and contradicts what is reliably reported from him through chains of trustworthy narrators in the books of his disciples, as cited by Judge Ismāʿīl ibn Ishāq and others. For example, they reported that he was asked about people who stood for a long time facing the burial chamber, supplicating for themselves. Mālik condemned this and stated that it was among the innovations that neither the Companions nor those who followed them in righteousness had practised. He said, “Nothing will set the later generations of this community aright except what set its first generations aright.”

There is no doubt that the matter is as Mālik stated. The mass-transmitted reports from the Companions and their successors show that this was neither their practice nor their custom. Had facing the burial chamber when supplicating been prescribed, they would have known this better and would have preceded later generations in doing so. The person supplicating invokes Allah alone. Facing the burial chamber while supplicating to Allah Most High has been forbidden, just as facing it while praying to Allah Most High has been forbidden, as established in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere on the authority of Abū Marthad

---

## Page 356

al-Ghanawī, who reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not sit on graves, and do not pray facing them.”

In view of this authentic hadith, it is impermissible to pray facing any grave, whether that of a prophet or anyone else. Muslims do not disagree that deliberately praying towards a grave is not prescribed; rather, it is a newly introduced innovation. The same applies to deliberately facing any grave when supplicating, especially the graves of prophets and righteous people. If deliberately facing a grave when supplicating to Allah Most High is impermissible, then invoking the deceased himself is all the more impermissible. Likewise, if praying while facing him is impermissible, then praying to him is all the more so.

It is therefore clear that it is impermissible to ask the deceased for anything: he must not be asked to supplicate to Allah on one’s behalf, or for anything else. Nor is it permissible to complain to him of any affliction in worldly or religious matters, even if it were permissible to complain to him of such things during his lifetime. Doing so during his lifetime does not lead to associating partners with Allah, whereas doing so after his death does. During his lifetime, he is under an obligation to respond to the requests of those who ask him, for which he receives reward and recompense. After death, however, he is no longer subject to religious obligations.

Rather, whatever he does of remembering Allah Most High, supplicating, and similar acts—as Moses prays in his grave, as the prophets prayed behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in Jerusalem on the night of the Ascension, and as the inhabitants of Paradise and the angels glorify Allah—is something through which they are granted enjoyment. They perform these acts according to what Allah facilitates and ordains for them; this is not a matter of the religious obligations through which He tests His servants.

Thus, a person’s request to the deceased has no effect whatsoever on this. Rather, he does whatever Allah has appointed him to do, even if no servant asks him, just as the angels do what they are commanded, and they only

---

## Page 357

obey their Lord’s command, not the command of any created being. As He, glorified and exalted, says:

“They say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken a son.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, they are honored servants.”

“They do not precede Him in speech, and they act by His command.”

Thus, they act only by His command, glorified and exalted is He. The permissibility of something during his lifetime does not entail its permissibility after his death. For praying in his house was a prescribed practice, and it was permissible to make it a mosque. Once he was buried there, however, it became forbidden to take it as a mosque. As reported from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the two Sahīh collections, he said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

He was warning against what they had done. Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but its being taken as a place of prayer was disapproved of. In Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of prayer. Beware! Do not take graves as places of prayer, for I forbid you to do so.”

During his lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, people prayed behind him, and that was among the best deeds. After his death, however, it is impermissible for anyone to pray behind his grave. Likewise, during his lifetime, he could be asked to issue commands, give religious rulings, and adjudicate disputes; but it is impermissible to ask him to do these things after his death. There are many similar examples.

Mālik and others disapproved of a person saying, “I visited the grave of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,” because this wording has not been transmitted. The hadiths reported concerning visiting his grave are all weak—indeed, fabricated.

This expression has come to bear more than one meaning in the usage of later generations. It is used to refer to an innovated form of visitation that is of the nature of associating partners with Allah: as when someone visits a grave to ask its occupant for something, to ask Allah through him, or to ask Allah at his grave.

---

## Page 358

And the prescribed form of visitation is to visit him for Allah’s sake: to supplicate for him and greet him with peace, just as one offers the funeral prayer for him. This second form is what is prescribed. Many people, however, intend only the first meaning when speaking of visitation. Mālik therefore disapproved of saying, “I visited his grave,” because it could suggest the corrupt meaning intended by those who practise religious innovation and associate partners with Allah.

The third form is to say, “I ask You by so-and-so,” or “by so-and-so’s standing with You,” and similar expressions which, as previously related from Abū Hanīfa, Abū Yūsuf, and others, are prohibited. It was also explained earlier that this was not a well-known practice among the Companions. Rather, they turned away from it to seeking a means of approach to Allah through the supplication of al-‘Abbās and others.

The ambiguity of the term “tawassul” (seeking a means of approach to Allah) has now become clear: it is used both for what the Companions practised and for what they did not practise. In the Companions’ usage and language, tawassul and turning to Allah meant doing so through his supplication and intercession. Thus, it is permissible to seek a means of approach to Allah and turn to Him through the supplication of any believer.

Although some shaykhs who have followers cite as evidence a report they transmit from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he said:

“When matters prove too difficult for you, turn to the occupants of graves”—or, “seek help from the occupants of graves”—

this hadith is a lie fabricated against the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, according to the consensus of those versed in his hadiths. No scholar knowledgeable in this field has transmitted it, nor is it found in any of the authoritative hadith collections.

---

## Page 359

Allah Most High has said:

“Put your trust in the Ever-Living, who never dies, and glorify Him with praise. He suffices as One fully aware of His servants’ sins.”

It is known as a matter of necessity in Islam that this practice is not prescribed. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited even practices short of this—taking graves as places of worship and the like—and cursed those who engaged in them, warning against imitating them, for this is the origin of idol worship.

As Allah Most High has said:

“They said, ‘Do not abandon your gods; do not abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’”

These were righteous men among the people of Noah. When they died, the people remained devotedly at their graves, then made images of them, and then made idols in their likeness, as previously related from Ibn ‘Abbās and other scholars of the early generations.

Whoever understands the meaning of His words:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help,”

will recognize that Allah alone provides help in worship in an absolute sense, while a created being may be asked for help with what lies within its power. Likewise, deliverance is to be sought only from Allah, and trust is to be placed only in Him:

“Victory comes only from Allah.”

Victory in the absolute sense—that is, bringing into being that by which the enemy is overcome—is within Allah’s power alone. This much is sufficient for anyone whom Allah has guided. And Allah knows best.

The forms of associating partners with Allah that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited are likewise prohibited in the revealed laws of the other prophets. The Torah states that Moses, peace be upon him, forbade the Children of Israel to invoke the dead or engage in other forms of associating partners with Allah, and explained that this is among the causes of Allah’s punishment of those who practise it. This is because the religion of the prophets, peace be upon them, is one, though their revealed laws differ. As authentically reported from Abū Hurayra, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“We prophets share one religion.”

---

## Page 360

And Allah Most High has said:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, that which We have revealed to you, and that which We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: establish the religion and do not become divided within it. What you call them to is hard for those who associate partners with Allah.”

Allah Most High has also said:

“O messengers, eat of wholesome things and do righteous deeds. I know well what you do.”

“Indeed, this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

“But they divided their religion among themselves into factions, each rejoicing in what it had.”

Allah Most High has also said:

“So set your face towards the religion, inclining to the truth—the natural disposition Allah has instilled in mankind. There is no altering Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.”

“Turn to Him in repentance, fear Him, establish prayer, and do not be among those who associate partners with Allah.”

“Among those who divided their religion and became sects, each rejoicing in what it had.”

This is the religion of Islam, besides which Allah accepts no other religion from earlier or later generations, as has been discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 361

Section

Once it becomes clear what Allah and His Messenger have commanded and forbidden concerning the noblest of creation and the most honoured before Allah, the Almighty and Majestic—the master of Adam’s descendants, the last of the messengers and prophets, the best of all earlier and later generations, and the intercessor with the highest rank and greatest standing before Allah, Blessed and Exalted—it becomes clear that there is even greater reason not to associate any prophet or righteous person below him in rank as a partner with Allah, take his grave as an idol to be worshipped, or invoke him besides Allah, whether during his life or after his death.

It is not permissible for anyone to seek deliverance from any absent or deceased shaykh, saying, for example: “O my master so-and-so, come to my aid, grant me victory, and ward off harm from me,” or “I rely on you,” or similar expressions. Rather, all of this constitutes associating partners with Allah, which Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. Its prohibition is necessarily known to be part of the religion of Islam.

Since those who seek deliverance from the absent and the dead, whether at their graves or elsewhere, are of the same kind as idol worshippers, Satan leads them astray and entices them into error, just as he does idol worshippers. Devils assume the form of the person whose aid is sought and address them by disclosing hidden matters, just as devils address soothsayers. Some of this is true, but it must also contain falsehood; indeed, falsehood predominates over truth.

---

## Page 362

The devils may also fulfil some of their needs and ward off some of the things they dislike. One of them then imagines that the shaykh himself has come from the unseen to do this, or that Allah Most High has fashioned an angel—in the shaykh’s likeness—who has done it. One of them says: “This is the shaykh’s secret and spiritual state.” In reality, it is only a devil assuming his form to mislead the person who associates him as a partner with Allah and seeks deliverance from him. Likewise, devils enter idols, speak to their worshippers, and fulfil some of their needs. This happened with the idols of the Arab polytheists, and it still occurs today among polytheists from the Turks, Indians, and others.

I know of many such incidents involving people who sought deliverance from me and from others while we were absent from them. They saw me, or the other person whose aid they had sought, come through the air and protect them. When they told me about this, I explained to them that it was only a devil assuming my form or that of another shaykh whose aid they had sought. Its purpose was to make them think that these were miraculous favours granted to the shaykh, thereby strengthening their resolve to seek deliverance from absent and deceased shaykhs. This is one of the chief causes that have led polytheists and idol worshippers to associate partners with Allah.

Likewise, Christians who seek deliverance from their shaykhs, whom they call al-‘alāmis, also see someone coming in the form of the Christian shaykh whose aid they have sought and fulfilling some of their needs.

As for those who seek deliverance from deceased prophets, righteous people, shaykhs, and members of the Prophet’s household—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the most that happens to any one of them is that some of these things occur for him, or accounts of such things are related to them. He then imagines that this is a miraculous favour and a departure from the ordinary course of events brought about by this practice. Among these people are those who come to the grave of the shaykh

---

## Page 363

whom they associate as a partner with Allah and from whom they seek deliverance. Food, money, weapons, or other things they request then descend to them through the air. They imagine this to be a miraculous favour granted to their shaykh, whereas it is all the work of devils. This is one of the chief causes that have led to the worship of idols. Abraham, the intimate friend of Allah, peace be upon him, said:

“Keep me and my sons away from worshipping idols.”

“My Lord, they have indeed led many people astray.”

Noah, peace be upon him, also spoke similarly. It is well known that a stone does not lead many people astray without some cause that brings about their misguidance. None of the idol worshippers believed that their idols had created the heavens and the earth. Rather, they took them as intercessors and intermediaries for various reasons. Some fashioned them in the likenesses of prophets and righteous people. Others made them into statues and talismans associated with the stars, the sun, and the moon. Others made them for the jinn, and still others for the angels.

Thus, those whom they intend to worship are the angels, prophets, and righteous people, or the sun or the moon. In reality, however, they worship devils. It is the devils who seek to have human beings worship them and who show them things that induce them to do so. As Allah Most High says:

“And on the Day He gathers them all together, He will then say to the angels, ‘Was it you that these people used to worship?’”

“They will say, ‘Glory be to You! You are our Protector, not they. Rather, they used to worship the jinn; most of them believed in them.’”

If a worshipper does not regard worshipping devils as lawful, they make him think that he is only invoking

---

## Page 364

the prophets, righteous people, angels, and others of whom the worshipper thinks well. But if he is someone who does not regard worshipping the jinn as forbidden, they tell him that they are jinn.

A devil appearing to him in human form may ask him to prostrate before it, engage in an illicit sexual act with it, eat carrion and drink wine, or offer carrion to them. Most of these people do not realize this. Rather, they imagine that those addressing them are either angels or men from among the jinn, whom they call “men of the unseen.” They believe that these men of the unseen are allies of Allah hidden from people's sight. Yet they are jinn who have assumed human forms or have been seen in nonhuman forms. Allah Most High says:

“And there were men among mankind who sought refuge with men among the jinn, but they increased them in transgression.”

Whenever someone stopped in a valley whose inhabitants he feared, he would say, “I seek refuge with the chief of this valley from its foolish ones.” Human beings thus sought refuge with the jinn, which became a cause of the jinn's transgression. They would say, “Human beings seek refuge with us!”

The same applies to incantations and foreign-language conjurations: they contain the names of men among the jinn who are invoked and called upon for help, and who are adjured by those whom they revere. Because of this, the devils obey those using these formulas in certain matters.

This is a form of magic and associating partners with Allah. Allah Most High says:

“They followed what the devils recited during Solomon's reign. Solomon did not disbelieve, but the devils disbelieved, teaching people magic and what was sent down to the two angels at Babylon, Hārūt and Mārūt. The two would not teach anyone without first saying, ‘We are only a trial, so do not disbelieve.’ Yet people learned from them that by which they caused separation between a man and his wife. But they could harm no one by it except with Allah's permission. And they learn what harms them and does not

---

## Page 365

benefit them. And they certainly knew that whoever purchased it would have no share in the Hereafter. How wretched was that for which they sold themselves, if only they knew.”

Many of these people fly through the air, carried by devils who take them to Mecca and elsewhere. Yet such a person may be a heretic who denies the obligation of prayer and other duties prescribed by Allah and His Messenger, and regards as lawful what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. Those devils associate with him only because of his disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience. Once he believes in Allah and His Messenger, repents, and commits himself to obeying Allah and His Messenger, those devils leave him, and those satanic phenomena—the disclosures of information and the effects they produce—disappear.

I know many such people in Syria, Egypt, the Hijaz, and Yemen. In Upper Mesopotamia, Iraq, Khurāsān, and the lands of Rūm, there are more people of this kind than in Syria and elsewhere. These phenomena are still more pronounced in the lands of the disbelievers, whether polytheists or People of the Book.

These satanic phenomena, whose causes are disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience, manifest themselves in proportion to the prevalence of their causes. Wherever faith, belief in Allah’s oneness, and the light of discernment and faith are strong, and the effects of prophethood and the divine message are evident, these satanic phenomena weaken. Wherever disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience prevail, these satanic phenomena grow stronger. A person in whom both are present—who has within him a source nourishing faith and another nourishing hypocrisy—will experience something of both states.

The polytheists who have not entered Islam, such as the Bakhshiyya, the Tūniyya, and the Buddī

---

## Page 366

and similar figures among the polytheists’ scholars and spiritual masters who serve the disbelievers among the Turks, the Indians al-Jawār, and others, experience these satanic phenomena more frequently. One of them rises into the air and tells them of unseen matters. The frame drum used to accompany their singing keeps moving through the air and strikes the head of anyone who departs from their way, yet they see no one beating it. The vessel from which they drink passes around among them, yet they see no one carrying it. One of them may be staying somewhere and provide whoever comes to visit him with enough food for them all, serving them a variety of dishes. This is the work of devils: they steal the food from a nearby town or elsewhere and bring it to him. Such things are common among those of the Turks and other peoples who are polytheists or deficient in faith. Among the Tatars, there are many varieties of these phenomena.

As for those who have entered Islam, if they do not fully uphold Allah’s oneness and follow the Messenger, but instead invoke absent spiritual masters and seek their aid, they have a share of these satanic phenomena in proportion to what they harbour that pleases Satan. Among them are people devoted to worship and religion, yet affected by a degree of ignorance. One of them is carried away and set down at ʿArafāt with the pilgrims, without entering the consecrated state of ihrām when passing abreast of the designated entry points, spending the night at Muzdalifah, or performing the Hajj circumambulation. He thinks that he has thereby accomplished a righteous deed and received a great miracle of the kind granted to Allah’s close servants, unaware that Satan is playing tricks on him.

Such a Hajj is neither prescribed nor permissible, by the agreement of Muslim scholars. Whoever thinks that this is an act of worship and a miracle granted to Allah’s close servants is misguided and ignorant. This is why nothing of this kind ever happened to any of the prophets or Companions, for they are too exalted

---

## Page 367

in rank for such things. This happened to a man who, together with a group accompanying him, was carried from Alexandria to ʿArafāt. He saw angels descending and recording the pilgrims’ names, so he asked, “Have you recorded my name?”

They replied, “You have not performed Hajj as other people have. You have neither undergone hardship nor entered ihrām, nor have you attained what the pilgrims attain through the Hajj for which people are rewarded.”

Some of these people asked a spiritual master to perform Hajj with them by travelling through the air. He told them, “This Hajj does not discharge your obligation, because you have not performed Hajj as Allah and His Messenger commanded.”

The religion of Islam rests upon two foundations: that Allah alone should be worshipped, without associating anything with Him, and that He should be worshipped according to what He prescribed through His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. These two foundations constitute the true meaning of our declaration:

“I bear witness that none is worthy of worship except Allah, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger.”

A deity is one to whom hearts turn in worship, seeking help, love, veneration, fear, hope, reverence and honour. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has a right that none shares with Him: none but Allah is to be worshipped, none but Allah invoked, none but Allah feared, and none but Allah obeyed.

The Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, conveys from Allah Most High His commands and prohibitions, and what He declares lawful and forbidden. Thus, what he declares lawful is lawful, what he declares forbidden is forbidden, and religion is what he prescribes. The Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is an intermediary between Allah and His creation in conveying His commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings, what He declares lawful and forbidden, and all the rest of His speech that he conveys.

As for answering supplications, removing affliction, granting guidance and providing sufficiency, Allah Most High is the One who hears their words, sees where they are, and knows their secrets and private conversations; and He, glorified be He, is able to

---

## Page 368

send down blessings and remove harm and qasam, without needing anyone to inform Him of His servants’ circumstances or help Him meet their needs. He Himself created and made available the means by which this occurs. He is the One who brings those means into being; He is the One, the Eternal Refuge, who neither begets nor is born, and to whom none is comparable.

“All who are in the heavens and the earth ask of Him; every day He is engaged in a matter.”

The inhabitants of the heavens ask of Him, and the inhabitants of the earth ask of Him. Hearing one person’s words does not distract Him, glorified be He, from hearing another’s, nor does the diversity of their voices and languages confuse Him. Rather, He hears the clamour of their voices, in their different languages and with their manifold needs. The persistence of those who plead does not weary Him; indeed, He loves persistence in supplication.

When the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about religious rulings, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was commanded to answer them, as Allah Most High said:

“They ask you about the new moons. Say, ‘They are markers of time for people and for Hajj.’”

“And they ask you what they should spend. Say, ‘Whatever is beyond your needs.’”

“They ask you about fighting in the sacred month. Say, ‘Fighting in it is a grave offence.’”

There are other examples of their questions as well.

But when they asked him about Allah, glorified and exalted be He, He said:

“And when My servants ask you about Me, indeed I am near. I answer the supplication of the supplicant when he calls upon Me.”

He, glorified be He, did not say, “Then say”; rather, He Most High said:

“Indeed I am near. I answer the supplication of the supplicant.”

He is therefore near to His servants, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith when they were raising their voices in remembrance and supplication:

“O people, be gentle with yourselves

---

## Page 369

, for you are not calling upon one who is deaf or absent; you are calling upon One who hears and is near. The One upon whom you call is closer to each of you than the neck of his mount.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“When one of you stands to pray, he must not spit in front of him, for Allah is before him, nor to his right, for there is an angel to his right, but to his left or beneath his foot.”

This hadith is recorded in the authentic hadith collection through more than one chain of transmission. He, glorified be He, is above His heavens, upon His Throne, separate from His creation. Nothing of His essence is in anything He has created, nor is anything He has created in His essence. He, glorified be He, has no need of the Throne or any other created thing. He depends on nothing He has created; rather, by His power He upholds the Throne and its bearers.

He Most High has arranged the world in layers, without making its higher levels dependent upon its lower ones. The sky does not depend upon the air, nor does the air depend upon the earth. Thus the Most High, the Highest, the Lord of the heavens and the earth and all that is between them, who described Himself in His words:

“They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves, while on the Day of Resurrection the whole earth will be in His grasp and the heavens will be folded up in His right hand. Glory be to Him! Exalted is He above what they associate with Him!”

is far too majestic, great, self-sufficient and exalted to depend upon anything to support Him or in any other way. Rather, He is the One, the Eternal Refuge, who neither begets nor is born, and to whom none is comparable. Everything other than Him depends upon Him, whereas He has no need of anything other than Himself.

These matters have been discussed at length elsewhere, where the affirmation of Allah’s oneness in word and deed, with which Allah sent His Messenger, has been explained. Affirming His oneness in words is exemplified by Sūrat al-Ikhlās:

“Say, ‘He is Allah, the One.’”

And affirming His oneness in practice is exemplified by:

“Say, ‘O disbelievers.’”

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would

---

## Page 370

recite these two surahs in the two rak‘ahs of Fajr and the two rak‘ahs of tawāf (circumambulation), and on other occasions.

He would also recite in the two rak‘ahs of Fajr and the two rak‘ahs of tawāf the verse beginning:

“Say, ‘We believe in Allah and in what has been revealed to us.’”

In the second rak‘ah, he would recite His words, Most High:

“Say, ‘O People of the Scripture, come to terms common to us and you: that we worship none but Allah, associate nothing with Him, and do not take one another as lords besides Allah.’ If they turn away, say, ‘Bear witness that we are Muslims.’”

These two verses contain the religion of Islam and faith in word and deed. His words, Most High:

“We believe in Allah and in what has been revealed to us, and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes,”

through to the end of the verse, encompass faith professed in words and Islam. His words:

“Say, ‘O People of the Scripture, come to terms common to us and you,’”

through to the end of the verse, encompass Islam and faith expressed in deeds. The greatest blessing Allah has bestowed upon His servants is Islam and faith, and both are contained in these two verses. Allah, glorified and exalted be He, knows best.

This concludes the question-and-answer exchange that I wished to reproduce here in its original wording, because it concisely presents important points and beneficial principles concerning this subject. Affirming Allah’s oneness is the secret of the Quran and the heart of faith. Expressing it in varied ways that bring out its different implications is among the most important and beneficial means of securing people’s welfare in this life and the Hereafter. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 371

Shaykh al-Islām said concerning the words, “I ask You by the right that those who ask of You have over You,” and expressions of similar meaning:

Answer:

As for the words, “I ask You by the right that those who ask of You have over You,” they occur in a hadith attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and narrated by Ibn Mājah. However, its chain of transmission is insufficient to establish it as proof.

If this is authentically established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, its meaning would be that the right of those who ask Allah is that He answer them, and the right of those who worship Him is that He reward them. He has prescribed this for Himself, as He says:

“When My servants ask you concerning Me, I am indeed near. I respond to the call of the supplicant when he calls upon Me.”

Thus, this is asking Allah by what He has made binding upon Himself, like the words:

“Our Lord, grant us what You have promised us through Your messengers.”

It is also like the supplication of the three men who took shelter in the cave, when they asked Him by their righteous deeds, for which He had promised to reward them. End of quotation.

---

## Page 372

While the shaykh was in the al-Tarsīm hall, three monks from Upper Egypt came to see him. He debated with them and established the proof against them that they were disbelievers and did not follow the faith of Abraham and the Messiah.

They said to him: “We do just as you do: you turn to Lady Nafīsah, and we turn to Lady Mary. We and you agree that the Messiah and Mary are superior to al-Husayn and Nafīsah. You seek help from the righteous who came before you, and so do we.”

He replied: “Whoever does that bears a resemblance to you. This is not the religion that Abraham followed. The religion of Abraham, peace be upon him, is that we worship Allah alone, with no partner: He has no equal, no consort and no child. We associate with Him neither an angel, nor the sun, nor the moon, nor a star; nor do we associate with Him any prophet or righteous person.

‘There is no one in the heavens and earth but that he comes to the Most Merciful as a servant.’

Furthermore, things that none but Allah can accomplish must not be sought from anyone else, such as sending down rain, causing plants to grow, relieving distress, guiding people out of error and forgiving sins. No one in all creation can accomplish these things; Allah alone can do so. As for the prophets, may blessings and peace be upon them, we believe in them, esteem them, honour them and follow them

---

## Page 373

and affirm the truth of everything they brought, and obey them. As Noah, Sālih, Hūd and Shuʿayb said:

‘Worship Allah, fear Him and obey me.’

Thus they directed worship and reverent fear to Allah alone, and obedience to themselves, for obeying them is part of obeying Allah. If someone disbelieved in one prophet while believing in all the others, his faith would not benefit him until he believed in that prophet. Likewise, if he believed in all the revealed Books but disbelieved in one, he would be a disbeliever until he believed in that Book. The same applies to the angels and the Last Day.”

When they heard this from him, they said: “The religion you have described is better than the religion we and these people follow.” Then they left him.

---

## Page 374

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Does someone who habitually kisses the ground incur sin? And what about someone who does so to receive a stipend, under coercion?”

He replied:

Kissing the ground, raising the head, and similar acts involving prostration, as performed before certain shaykhs and kings, are impermissible. Indeed, even bending forward as in bowing is impermissible, as in the following report:

“They asked the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him: ‘When one of us meets his brother, should he bow to him?’ He replied: ‘No.’”

“When Muʿādh returned from Syria, he prostrated before the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. The Prophet asked: ‘What is this, Muʿādh?’ He replied: ‘O Messenger of Allah, I saw them in Syria prostrating before their bishops, and they attributed this practice to their prophets.’ He said: ‘They have falsely attributed it to them. If I were to command anyone to prostrate before another, I would command a woman to prostrate before her husband because of his rights over her. O Muʿādh, prostration is due to Allah alone.’”

Doing this as an act of religious devotion and a means of drawing nearer to Allah is among the gravest wrongs. Whoever believes such a practice to be a means of drawing nearer to Allah and an act of religious devotion is misguided and fabricates falsehood. Rather, it must be explained to him that this is neither part of the religion nor a means of drawing nearer to Allah. If he persists, he must be called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be put to death.

But if a man is coerced into doing this, such that refusing would result in his being beaten

---

## Page 375

or imprisoned, or having his wealth seized or the stipend to which he is entitled from the public treasury withheld, or suffering similar harm, then doing so is permissible according to most scholars. For most of them hold that coercion permits otherwise forbidden acts, such as drinking wine and the like. This is the well-known position reported from Ahmad and others. Nevertheless, he must detest it in his heart and strive to refrain from it as far as possible. Whoever Allah knows to be sincere, Allah Most High will help; through the blessing of his sincerity, he may even be spared being ordered to do this.

A group of scholars, however, held that coercion permits only otherwise forbidden words, not deeds. This position is reported from Ibn ʿAbbās and others, who said: “Dissimulation under coercion is confined to speech.” This is the other position reported from Ahmad.

As for doing this to obtain additional status and wealth, it is not permissible. If, however, he is coerced into such an act and intends in his heart that this submission is to Allah Most High, that is good—just as when he detests an expression of disbelief while intending a permissible meaning. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 376

The learned, godly imam who acted upon his knowledge, the illustrious scholar Abū al-ʿAbbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is it permissible for people to rise and stand, as they customarily do, to honour a particular respected person upon his arrival? What if the person who remains seated considers it very likely that the newcomer will feel embarrassed or inwardly hurt, and that this may lead to hatred, hostility and resentment? Likewise, when meeting people at gatherings or elsewhere, is bending the neck towards the ground and lowering oneself permissible or forbidden? If a man does this out of habit and natural inclination, without any deliberate intention, is it forbidden for him, or is such conduct impermissible towards nobles and scholars? As for someone who appears continually at ease with this, does he incur sin for it or not? If he says, ‘I prostrated to Allah,’ is that valid or not?”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

It was not the custom of the early Muslims, during the time of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his rightly guided caliphs, to stand up whenever they saw him, peace be upon him, as many people do. Rather, Anas ibn Mālik said:
