“No one was more beloved to them than the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—yet when they saw him, they did not stand up for him, because they knew of his dislike

---

## Page 377

of this.” Yet they would sometimes rise to welcome someone returning after an absence, as in the report:

“It is related that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—rose for ‘Ikrimah.”

“And when Sa‘d ibn Mu‘ādh arrived, he said to the Ansār, ‘Rise and go to your leader.’”

Sa‘d had come to pass judgment concerning Banū Qurayzah, because they had agreed to submit to his judgment.

People should make it their practice to follow the early Muslims as they were in the time of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for theirs was the best generation. The best speech is the speech of Allah, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. No one should turn away from the guidance of the best of creation and the best generation to something inferior. A person whose authority is respected should not allow his companions to adopt this practice; rather, when they see him, they should not rise for him except when meeting him in the customary manner.

As for rising to welcome someone returning from a journey or the like, that is good. If people customarily honour a newcomer by rising, and, were they to refrain, he would believe that they were withholding the respect due to him or deliberately belittling him, while being unaware of the practice consistent with the Sunnah, then it is better to rise for him. This better preserves good relations and removes mutual hatred and rancour.

But someone who knows that the people’s practice accords with the Sunnah is not hurt when they refrain from rising. Nor is this the standing mentioned in the Prophet’s saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever is pleased to have men stand before him, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

That refers to their standing for him while he remains seated, not to their rising upon his arrival. Hence the distinction made between saying, “I rose to meet him,” and “I stood for him.” Someone who rises for a newcomer stands on an equal footing with him, unlike someone who stands for a seated person.

[It is established in Sahīh Muslim:

“When the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—led them in prayer while seated,

---

## Page 378

they prayed standing, so he instructed them to sit. And he said: ‘Do not exalt me as the non-Arabs exalt one another.’”] (*)

He forbade them to stand in prayer while he was seated, so as not to resemble the non-Arabs who stand before their dignitaries while the latter remain seated. The sound approach underlying all of this is to follow the customs and conduct of the early Muslims and strive to do so as far as possible.

If someone has not adopted this practice and does not know that it is the customary way, and withholding the respect he is accustomed to receiving from people would cause a greater harm, then the greater of the two harms is averted by accepting the lesser, just as one must secure the greater of two benefits at the expense of the lesser.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (p. 18):
> This hadith does not appear in Muslim with this wording. Muslim’s wording, narrated from Jābir (may Allah be pleased with him) and attributed to the Prophet, is: “You nearly did just now what the Persians and Romans do: they stand before their kings while the latter are seated. Do not do this.” The wording quoted here was narrated by Ahmad from Abū Umāma (may Allah be pleased with him), with a chain of transmission that is open to question. It therefore appears that the shaykh (may Allah have mercy on him) intended the attribution “It is established in Sahīh Muslim” to extend only as far as “he instructed them to sit,” after which he cited another hadith: “And he said: ‘Do not exalt me as the non-Arabs exalt one another.’” Thus, the wāw (“and”) introduces a new statement rather than joining it to the preceding one. However, a difficulty remains: this recurs at 27/93, where he said, “It is established in the Sahīh,” and cited this hadith. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 379

Section

As for bowing when greeting someone, it is forbidden, as reported in al-Tirmidhī:

“They asked the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, about a man who meets his brother: ‘Should he bow to him?’ He replied: ‘No.’”

This is because bowing and prostration may be performed only for Allah, the Almighty and Majestic, even though they were used as greetings under a revealed law other than ours, as in the account of Joseph:

“And they fell down before him in prostration. He said, ‘O my father, this is the fulfilment of my earlier dream.’”

Under our revealed law, however, prostration is permissible only to Allah. Indeed, the Prophet’s prohibition against standing as the non-Arabs do for one another has already been mentioned. How much more, then, does this apply to bowing and prostration? Likewise, even a partial bow falls within this prohibition.

---

## Page 380

Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section

The polytheists used to dedicate themselves and their children in servitude to others besides Allah. They called some of them 'Abd al-Ka'bah (“servant of the Ka'bah”), which was formerly the name of 'Abd al-Rahmān ibn 'Awf; others, 'Abd Shams (“servant of the sun”), which was formerly the name of Abū Hurayrah and was also the name of 'Abd Shams ibn 'Abd Manāf; others, 'Abd al-Lāt (“servant of al-Lāt”), 'Abd al-'Uzzā (“servant of al-'Uzzā”), or 'Abd Manāt (“servant of Manāt”). They used other such names that ascribed servitude to something besides Allah—a sun, an idol, a human being, or anything else that might be associated with Allah as a partner. The Christian practice of naming someone 'Abd al-Masīh (“servant of Christ”) is similar.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, changed these names and made them servants of Allah alone. He named many of his Companions 'Abd Allāh (“servant of Allah”) and 'Abd al-Rahmān (“servant of the Most Merciful”), as he did with 'Abd al-Rahmān ibn 'Awf and others. Likewise, he renamed Abū Mu'āwiyah, whose name had been 'Abd al-'Uzzā, calling him 'Abd al-Rahmān. His client was called Qayyūm (“Self-Sustaining”), so the Prophet renamed him 'Abd al-Qayyūm (“servant of the Self-Sustaining”).

Somewhat similar to this is what occurs among the extremists of the Rāfidah and others like them who go to excess in venerating sheikhs. Thus it is said, “This is the servant of Sheikh Yūnus,” or “he belongs to Sheikh Yūnus,” or “the servant of Ibn

---

## Page 381

al-Rifā'ī,” or “the servant of al-Harīrī,” and similar expressions that involve a kind of worshipful devotion to human beings, like that which Christians may feel toward Christ and polytheists toward their gods, with hope and fear. They may also turn to them in repentance, just as the polytheists used to repent to some of their gods, and Christians to Christ or certain saints.

The law of Islam—which is the religion devoted sincerely to Allah alone—establishes people's servitude to their Lord, as prescribed by the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. It replaces polytheistic names with Islamic names, and names expressing disbelief with names expressing faith. The names the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, most commonly gave were 'Abd Allāh and 'Abd al-Rahmān.

As Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Call upon Allah or call upon the Most Merciful. Whichever name you call upon—to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names.’”

These two names are the foundation of all the other names of Allah Most High.

Shaykh al-Islām al-Harawī gave the people of his town names based on most of Allah's Most Beautiful Names. Likewise, names expressing servitude to Allah predominate in our family, such as 'Abd Allāh, 'Abd al-Rahmān, 'Abd al-Ghanī, 'Abd al-Salām, 'Abd al-Qāhir, 'Abd al-Latīf, 'Abd al-Hakīm, 'Abd al-'Azīz, 'Abd al-Rahīm, 'Abd al-Muhsin, 'Abd al-Ahad, 'Abd al-Wāhid, 'Abd al-Qādir, 'Abd al-Karīm, 'Abd al-Malik, and 'Abd al-Haqq.

It is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim, through Nāfi', from 'Abd Allāh ibn 'Umar, that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The names most beloved to Allah are 'Abd Allāh and 'Abd al-Rahmān; those most true to their meanings are Hārith and Hammām; and the ugliest are Harb and Murrah.”

Among the battle cries used by the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, when fighting alongside him were: “O sons of 'Abd al-Rahmān! O sons of 'Abd Allāh! O sons of

---

## Page 382

'Ubayd Allāh!” as they called out at Badr, Hunayn, the Conquest, and al-Tā'if. The battle cry of the Emigrants was “O sons of 'Abd al-Rahmān!”; that of the Khazraj was “O sons of 'Abd Allāh!”; and that of the Aws was “O sons of 'Ubayd Allāh!”

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Here ends what has so far been found of Kitāb Tawhīd al-Ulūhiyya (The Book of Oneness in Divinity). It is followed by Kitāb Tawhīd al-Rubūbiyya (The Book of Oneness in Lordship).
